# Desktop app and delivery

*What the desktop application is made of and what shell it runs in; how its modules reach the operating system and each other; where the local record lives and how it is migrated; what every local write must do on each of Mac, Windows and Linux; how a name arriving from a rented machine is judged against the receiving filesystem before anything is overwritten; where the one upload budget, the one download budget and the one session-admission point sit; what makes the operating system's own notifications reach a user working in Blender; how one repeating failure is said once and how every suppressed warning comes back at one press; how text is rendered, what tells the interface which of light or dark to draw in and how that reaches the surfaces the operating system draws, how menus and pickers are reached; and how the application is built, signed, hosted, verified and replaced.*

*2026-10-27 — Removed the withdrawn paid-access text and its obsolete references under the Creator’s free-and-open-source decision of 2026-10-04 (wave 5); retained protections and evidence keep their force. No auditor has seen this removal.*

Rule 1's plain-English rule is suspended here, per the Architecture stage's exception.

**Scope, from [The shape](The%20shape.md#7-desktop-app-and-delivery):** desktop process composition; module API boundaries; local database and migrations; platform adapters for Mac, Windows and Linux; receiving-filesystem collision judgement, including case-insensitive filesystems, through the platform file adapter used by every local writer; one desktop-side upload rate budget and one download rate budget shared across every server and transfer channel; one desktop-side session-admission point beside those budgets, through which every per-server session-admission point `Server protocol and transfers` defines is admitted, so admission knows about every server at once; automatic supervisor-unavailable package-repair trigger; UI event delivery; text rendering and the typography application point; the application's colour scheme, including how it follows or departs from the operating system's; the privileged web-content boundary, which fixes what the interface's own content may reach; notifications; progress and failure presentation plumbing; warning suppression; menu and picker integration; build outputs; signing; hosting; update verification and replacement. **The colour scheme ([2.6](#26-the-colour-scheme-read-and-declared)) and the privileged web-content boundary ([2.8](#28-the-privileged-web-content-boundary)) were brought into the shape's list at the Architecture seam close of 2026-08-22, and [Section 22](#22-unresolved) items 34 and 42 record it.** *(Corrected 2026-08-29 at the stage-close repair. The restatement above was two clauses short of the scope line it quotes, and the sentence that followed it said the shape's list does not name either of them — which items 34 and 42 in this same document both refuse, each carrying its CLOSED line since 2026-08-22 with the original entry preserved beneath it.)* It presents atomic and partial outcomes and does not define that boundary. This document hosts every feature module; it consumes `State, identity and recovery`, `Server protocol and transfers` and `Security and licence`, and exposes operating-system services to `Keys`, `Servers`, `File Browser`, `Download`, `Queue & History`, and the shared whole-app behaviour.

**Twenty-nine permanent coverage rows are answered here, and everything else below is a constraint the shape or an input document forced.** *(Twenty-eight until later on 2026-09-28, when Row 519 was filed and answered at §12 V11 to V16.)* *(Twenty-seven until 2026-09-28, when Row 518 was filed and answered at Z31 and Z32.)* *(Twenty-six until 2026-08-29, when the stage-close repair found **Row 484** answered here since 2026-08-27 and in neither the count nor the table below — the same failure the 2026-08-23 pass raised for Rows 444 and 446, recurring. Row 484 was answered on 2026-08-27 at the twelfth sitting in a subsection written for it, [6.6](#66-the-folder-identity-comparison) F21–F21c, and that subsection is given its Constraint line on the same date.)* *(Rows 504 and 508 were answered on 2026-08-29 at the twelfth sitting; both were filed on 2026-08-29 by the second stage-close re-sweep, and the count and the table caught up with them the same day. Row 504 is `Queue & History`'s promise and its presentation is this document's — [16.3](#163-row-286-presented-the-plain-sentence-and-the-machines-own-words) M13a and M13b, with M10's second bullet reworded to the spec's own narrowing; Row 508 is answered at [20.1](#201-what-the-application-does-and-what-the-user-does) Z26 to Z30, which widened Z23a's trigger, left Z1's sentence standing under a dated gloss that reads it narrower, and scoped Z10b. *(This read "narrowed Z1 by one word"; corrected 2026-08-29 at the re-gate repair, because no word of Z1 changed — see Z1's own note.)*)* *(Rows 474, 476, 480, 482, 483 and 500 were answered on 2026-08-25 at the tenth sitting, against the reading batch of that date; the same sitting wrote the five clauses `Reporting a problem` §12 item 5 routed here — the diary service ([5.3](#53-the-diary-service)), the folder-reveal service ([18.6](#186-the-folder-reveal-service)), A7's fifth declaration, and [16.8](#168-what-the-plumbing-hands-the-diary-and-the-report-entry-point)'s two hooks.)* *(Rows 321, 322, 327 and 347 were added on 2026-08-21, out of the stage-close sweep of the Design documents. So several sentences handed over together get the orphan rule once, at the end of the last one; where it has to hold for each of them — a failure's message that runs to two sentences is the ordinary case — they are handed over one sentence at a time. **That one thing is left to whoever writes the screen**, deliberately: a point that hunted for sentence ends would bind the wrong two words wherever it guessed wrong. And the point is for prose alone. The server's own words, paths, file names, hosts, ports and anything the user can copy reach the screen untouched, because a non-breaking space inside a path is a path that does not exist — so *everywhere the user reads*, above, means everywhere the user reads prose. ## What binds this before anything is decided

**Row 282 is answered before anything else and it governs every section below.** [Section 1](#1-three-operating-systems-and-every-local-answer-written-three-times) states it first for that reason. An answer written once against whichever system the writer had in mind is wrong on two of three.

**The technology choice comes before anything that depends on it**, which is why [Section 2](#2-the-shell) is second. It is not a free choice: `Preparation/Groundwork/Open Threads.md` carries a standing Creator ruling of 2026-08-13 and Fact 96 exists to serve it rather than to take it.

**There is no always-running local daemon** ([The shape](The%20shape.md#the-users-computer)). Work that must continue with the desktop application closed runs on the rented server. The updater may use a short-lived helper where the running application cannot replace itself, and that helper's platform-specific lifecycle is this document's ([20.5](#205-the-short-lived-helper-and-where-it-exists)). **The desktop application exposes no listener to the server** and does not require a rented server to call the user's computer.

**Seven architecture documents are inputs. Six have closed their round; the seventh, `Reporting a problem`, has no round at all, and every clause taken from it is cited at the point of use rather than resting on a closed round.** What each hands over is taken as decided: *(Corrected 2026-08-29 at the re-gate repair. This read **"Six architecture documents are inputs and every one has closed its round"** and listed six, while [5.3](#53-the-diary-service), [16.8](#168-what-the-plumbing-hands-the-diary-and-the-report-entry-point), [18.6](#186-the-folder-reveal-service) and [Z21b](#201-what-the-application-does-and-what-the-user-does) already take `Reporting a problem` clauses as decided — a seventh input consumed under a sentence that said six and said every one had closed. **This is a record correction and no clause that consumes it moves**: each names its exact source already.)*

- **[State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md)** hands this document [Section 11](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#11-retention-and-cleanup--the-local-lifetimes) entire, the store inventory of [§1.1](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#11-on-the-users-computer), the failure-record shape of [§8](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#8-failure-records--a-live-condition-and-a-recorded-ending) for presenting Row 285, the outcome record of [§9.4](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#94-the-outcome-record) for presenting partial and atomic outcomes, the repeat key of [§8.9](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#89-the-repeat-key-supplied-rather-than-applied) for Row 288 — **supplied rather than applied** — and the one suppression table of [§10.3](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#103-considered-and-not-filed) for Row 290. **Its §1.3 rules bind here with no carve-out of this document's own**; rule 5 has exactly one carve-out and it belongs to `Job files and outputs`. **Its §9 is the atomic-or-partial boundary: this document presents those outcomes and does not define them.**
- **[Server protocol and transfers](Server%20protocol%20and%20transfers.md)** hands over the per-channel enforcement it is told to apply and what the enforcement point has to be for one shared budget to hold ([§8](Server%20protocol%20and%20transfers.md#8-per-channel-rate-enforcement)); that the budget's one unit is **bytes per second** (R11); that **every admitted channel's draw is bounded below by a non-zero share**, so *waiting on the budget* is a bounded wait and C19's inactivity deadline has something to sit against — a constraint on how the budget is divided rather than a decision taken for its holder; the requirement that admission be one desktop-side point beside the budget (T5); the achieved-rate reading and its class (§8.4); the platform-file publication contract's four requirements (S18) — the fourth, supplying the staged and final objects' stable file identity where the platform has one, added 2026-08-22, with S15's identity-unavailable branch where the supplier cannot answer — and what the Mac's own manual says about the operation family (S19); the free-space reading and the next-write outcome as the disk-full condition's two observed values (F11); the reporting floor of [§10](Server%20protocol%20and%20transfers.md#10-nothing-is-carried-out-with-nothing-to-report-until-it-is-done); and, added 2026-08-29, the verdict on when an unanswered command-path request moves the supervisor-unavailable condition's observed value at all — a request left unanswered inside a break `Servers` then declares moves nothing ([C17a](Server%20protocol%20and%20transfers.md#46-per-waiter-deadlines)).
- **[Security and licence](Security%20and%20licence.md)** already decides the secret store on each system and it is not re-decided here (§3). Three things land here by name: **no feature module receives a stored secret as a value it may keep** (§1.4); **the local record is not encrypted and is written in the per-user application-data location this document's platform adapter names**, created for the owning user only (§9), which is what makes its migrations safe to write, copy and back up; and **macOS's post-update keychain dialog is an ordinary outcome of this document's own updater rather than a failure path**, without predicting when it appears (§3.1). Its fingerprinting clause and its two name-reading traps are [Section 10](#10-the-application-identity-adapter)'s.
- **[Job files and outputs](Job%20files%20and%20outputs.md)** hands over that a fetched derivative is a **viewing-format picture** (B28) — and B12 fixes that a viewing format is PNG or JPEG — so nothing about reading a working format crosses to the user's computer; and that **the inventory's names are the machine's**, which [Section 7](#7-the-receiving-filesystem-collision-judgement) is what stands between them and the loss observed on 2026-08-17.
- **[Rented-server runtime](Rented-server%20runtime.md)** hands over the supervisor-unavailable condition's observed value, which is whether the supervisor answered a request on the command path and is nothing else (V4); the two captured streams, kept apart, unedited, retained and readable with nobody connected, and the explicit collection outcome per stream from M13's closed set of four; that **an empty panel is never what the user meets**; that the exit status is carried verbatim and is never a claim about what was written (M22); and that **the automatic repair trigger is this document's**.
- **[Reporting a problem](Reporting%20a%20problem.md)** hands over the diary and the report handover, and it is **the one input with no audit round** — nothing here rests on that document having closed one. **R2**, that the diary is durable, bounded and **not state**, taken at [L2a](#51-one-store-and-its-contents-are-another-documents-list); **R4**'s one-writer rule, which is the whole ground of [5.3](#53-the-diary-service); **J6** and [§8](Reporting%20a%20problem.md#8-the-two-entry-points)'s **X4**, the two hooks at [16.8](#168-what-the-plumbing-hands-the-diary-and-the-report-entry-point); **V7** and **V8**, the written report file's reveal at [18.6](#186-the-folder-reveal-service); and, added 2026-08-29, **U5a**'s probe-mode suppression, whose launch-side half is [Z21b](#201-what-the-application-does-and-what-the-user-does). **Its §12 item 5 routed the first four here and all four are written; its §12 item 10 is closed on this side at Z21b.** **And U5b, the rollback relaunch's suppression, at Z21b's last bullet, which closes its §12 item 11 on this side** *(added 2026-10-24 by step 11's plan pass)*. *(Added 2026-08-29 at the re-gate repair, with the lead sentence above; this bullet was missing while five of its clauses were already consumed.)*
- **[The shape](The%20shape.md)** is the handoff and no change is proposed to it. Where this document disagrees with an input, the disagreement is recorded as a seam in [Section 22](#22-unresolved) and in the report to the runner, never repaired here.

**The machine and reading evidence is frozen.** Every observation about the user's own computer below was made on **macOS 26.5.2, build 25F84, arm64**, and the input document says so at each point. Read every quoted figure as *what that page said, or what that computer did, on that day*.

## 1. Three operating systems, and every local answer written three times

**Constraint:** Row 282, quoted at the head of this document, and [The user's own computer](The%20user's%20own%20computer%20—%20where%20a%20download%20lands,%20what%20it%20will%20take,%20what%20it%20will%20show,%20and%20how%20the%20app%20replaces%20itself.md) §7.

### 1.1 Every local answer is three answers

**Y1. The rented end is Linux alone, and narrower than that, and the user's end is all three — and they are not the same set. Quotation corrected 2026-08-22, on the Creator's ruling of the same day.** [Whole app](../Specs/Whole%20app.md) draws the line itself in the render direction, and it now draws it twice: *"**The machines Smoozy renders on run Linux, and the Linux Smoozy sets up is Ubuntu or Debian.** Smoozy works with Linux servers and no other kind."*, with a second, narrower boundary stated beneath it — *"Smoozy prepares **Ubuntu and Debian** machines. On a Linux machine of another family it says so plainly and prepares nothing, rather than half-preparing it and leaving the user with a machine that looks ready and is not."* **This clause used to quote the pre-ruling sentence word for word** — *"The machines Smoozy renders on run Linux. Smoozy works with Linux servers and no other kind."* — as a quotation from a spec that had already stopped saying only that. `Server Setup` M1a and M1b own the narrower boundary; nothing in **this** document changes with it, because the asymmetry below is the point and it is untouched. **So no observation taken on a rented Linux server answers for a Linux desktop computer**, and Fact 79's own *Machine* line says exactly that: *"A rented Linux server does not answer for the desktop application merely by sharing an operating-system family."*

**Y2. Every clause below that touches the user's own computer is written three times, or once against something all three demonstrably provide.** A clause written once and generalised is wrong on two of three. Where one system's third is absent, the clause names which.

### 1.2 What is observed, what is documented, and what cannot be posed

**Y3. No Windows machine has run any of this, there is no Linux desktop computer either, and none of that is going to change.** **PERMANENTLY UNRESOLVED, 2026-09-02, for every Windows reading and every Linux-desktop reading in this document. This is not a pending state and must not be read as one**, and no reader may leave a space in the code or in the record for an answer that is coming: the Creator ruled on **2026-08-31** that there will be **no further rented machine for answering a question**, so no Windows machine and no Linux desktop computer is ever run for one. *(Corrected 2026-09-02, where this clause said only that the Windows half is* **untried rather than untestable** *and left every Windows clause it governs reading as a test not yet taken. The precision that sentence carried is kept below; what it did not carry was that nothing schedules the test.)* **A Windows machine can be rented and four have been — that is history rather than a plan** — and **every one this project has read reported a Server edition**, not the desktop edition its image was named for, so a desktop-only question was already out of reach of what has been rented and is now out of reach entirely. Every Windows claim and every Linux claim below is **documentation alone, permanently**. Every observation is the Mac's, at the build named above. *(Build may rent a machine to run the app on. That is a different act and revives nothing here.)*

**Y4. `Preparation/Groundwork/Open Threads.md` already carries the named list of what a Windows computer would close**, with a Due line saying nothing schedules it. **That list is a record of what would have closed each reading, and not a queue — corrected 2026-09-02 with [Y3](#what-binds-this-before-anything-is-decided)**: under the ruling of 2026-08-31 nothing on it is coming, so it is read as the ground of a permanent silence rather than as work outstanding. **Bringing that document's own Due line into line is its owner's**, and nothing here reads it as scheduling a rental. Nothing here schedules it, nothing here works around it, and no clause below is written as though a Windows or Linux desktop reading had been observed.

### 1.3 How a clause closes where one system cannot be posed

**Y5. A clause that cannot be posed on Windows closes as answered for two systems and unresolved for the third, with what breaks named.** That is the correct outcome rather than a weak one. [Section 22](#22-unresolved) carries every one of them, and each names what breaks and what holds without it.

**Y6. A promise that merely gets weaker on one system closes as unresolved; a promise that cannot stand at all is [Method](Method.md)'s second escalation.** None of the twenty-seven rows reached it *(twenty-four until 2026-08-29, when the stage-close repair moved this figure in step with the head count, which had gone to twenty-six on 2026-08-29 and to twenty-seven with Row 484 the same day; this clause's claim is about the rows the head table lists and it is the only such figure below it)* — Row 446's Mac and Windows halves close as unresolved with what breaks at [Section 22](#22-unresolved) items 5 and 6, which is the first outcome and not the second; [Section 23](#23-moments-that-reached-the-creator) records what was checked.

## 2. The shell

**Constraint:** the Creator's standing ruling of 2026-08-13 in `Preparation/Groundwork/Open Threads.md` — the app is to be relatively small with no fixed ceiling; the comparison is against **real release artifacts** on all three systems; a route using an operating-system web view or another materially smaller shell is preferred; **a route bundling a complete browser engine is not chosen merely because it keeps more of the existing promises**; and **if the smaller route cannot keep one or more promises, Architecture stops** and brings the exact user outcome and its smaller replacement back to the Creator. The evidence is Fact 96, answered 2026-08-18, in [The user's own computer](The%20user's%20own%20computer%20—%20where%20a%20download%20lands,%20what%20it%20will%20take,%20what%20it%20will%20show,%20and%20how%20the%20app%20replaces%20itself.md) §6.

### 2.1 The figures, and every one is worth its own date

**H1. The comparison, macOS / Windows / Linux, read from each release listing's own metadata on 2026-08-18 and independently re-read to the byte by the citation check.**

| Candidate, and what the figure was measured on | Published artifact, MB |
|---|---|
| **Electron** — the shell alone, before one line of Smoozy | 122.1 / 144.4 / 125.6 |
| **Electron** — a real application on it, YouTube Music v3.12.0 | 124 / 287 / 142 |
| **Tauri v2** — the system's own web view, measured on Yaak v2026.6.0 | 66.4 / 42.9 / 79.9 |
| **Wails v2** — the system's own web view, measured on October v1.12.2 | **6.4 / 8.8 / 5.7** |
| **Qt 6** — a compiled widget toolkit, measured on KeePassXC 2.7.12 | 34.4 / 35.9 / 49.2 |
| **Flutter** — a compiled toolkit with its own renderer, measured on LocalSend v1.18.0 | 52.5 / 25.8 / 24.6 |
| **Neutralinojs** — one bundle for all three, per-system share unobtainable | 8.1, undivided |

**Three of the six publish no end-user application artifact of their own** — Tauri, Wails and Qt — so those three rows are real applications built with them and the application is named beside each.

**H2. Every figure above is worth exactly its own date, 2026-08-18.** Fact 96 is the fastest-changing entry in the register after Fact 10. A figure re-read later may differ, and nothing below rests on a figure staying what it was; what it rests on is the **ordering**, which is wide enough that no plausible movement reverses it.

**H2a. Two of the rows are the same kind of route and the gap between them is the application rather than the framework**, in the input document's own words: **Tauri v2 and Wails v2 are both compiled and both use the system's own web view**, and *"the difference in the figures is the application rather than the language."* **So 6.4 against 66.4 is October against Yaak, and it is not a measurement of what Smoozy would weigh on either.** That is carried as a limit on H4 rather than argued away, and [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 1 states what is left open by it.

**H3. Neutralinojs ships a single 8.1 MB bundle covering all three systems and its per-system share cannot be got without downloading it.** It is therefore not comparable against the ruling's own requirement — real release artifacts per system — and is not chosen.

### 2.2 The route chosen, and the properties it is held to

**H4. CLOSED 2026-09-02 by the Build stage's own measurement, and the answer is Tauri v2.** [H5b](#22-the-route-chosen-and-the-properties-it-is-held-to) was filled from equivalent artifacts on all three systems, and **Tauri v2 is materially smaller by that sheet's own rule** — 68.2 per cent and 1.605 MiB larger for Wails at the largest-system comparison, against a threshold of 10 per cent and 1 MiB, and smaller for Tauri on every system and every measure taken. **So the shell is Tauri v2 and the desktop application's language is Rust.** The record is `Preparation/Build/Shell measurement.md`. **This reverses the direction the provisional text pointed in, which is exactly what the sheet existed to settle**; nothing in H1 was wrong, and H2a already said its Wails and Tauri rows were October against Yaak. *(The original clause follows, unchanged, because every clause below that reads Wails as provisional was written against it.)*

**H4. The route class is a compiled shell over each operating system's own web view; the choice between Wails v2 and Tauri v2 is not proved yet.** H1 proves that this class is materially smaller than the bundled-engine and renderer/toolkit applications measured there. It does **not** prove that Wails makes Smoozy smaller than Tauri: 6.4 / 8.8 / 5.7 is October and 66.4 / 42.9 / 79.9 is Yaak. **The existing Wails direction is provisional, not a completed size decision, until H5b is filled from equivalent artifacts.** No Build artifact is authorized by this clause; the comparison is the next Build input.

**H5. What the figures do and do not decide, because H2a changes the shape of this choice.** The comparison establishes, against real release artifacts as the ruling requires, that **the two compiled system-web-view routes sit below every other kind measured** — below Qt 6 and Flutter, and far below Electron's 122.1 / 144.4 / 125.6 for the shell alone. **It does not establish Wails against Tauri**, and an unrelated application's smaller artifact is not a discriminator. The named negatives remain acceptance checks; they do not substitute for the like-for-like size comparison the Creator required. **CLOSED 2026-09-02: that comparison was taken and it establishes Tauri against Wails, the other way round from the direction this clause's own document was drafted in.** See [H4](#22-the-route-chosen-and-the-properties-it-is-held-to) and the filled sheet below. **What this clause says about H1 stands and is now proved by measurement rather than argued**: an unrelated application's artifact was never a discriminator, and when equivalent artifacts were built the ordering it implied reversed.

**H5a. The properties below are a check on a named thing rather than a substitute for a name.** Every one is checkable against Wails' own documentation, and a failure of any one moves the choice down H7's list rather than being carried.

**H5b. The cost-free Wails/Tauri comparison sheet. FILLED 2026-09-02 by the Build stage, and the result is Tauri v2.** This is the exact comparison Build must return to Architecture before either name becomes the selected shell. It changes no promise and asks the Creator for no money, place to look, thing to type or thing that can be lost. **No October or Yaak figure was copied into it; every figure below was measured on an artifact built for this sheet.** The whole record — how each variable was held fixed, what was read off the binaries, and what the measurement does not say — is `Preparation/Build/Shell measurement.md`.

| Fixed variable | Wails v2 artifact | Tauri v2 artifact |
|---|---|---|
| Source | the same minimal Smoozy comparison source: one frameless window, the same bundled HTML/CSS/JS bytes, one no-op typed application binding, no network request and no optional updater | byte-for-byte the same web payload and equivalent one-window/no-op-binding source |
| Framework identity | exact released version and dependency lock digest | exact released version and dependency lock digest |
| Build mode | fresh release build; production flags; symbols stripped; inspector, reload server and source maps absent | the same |
| Targets | the same CPU target on macOS, Windows and Linux; one fresh runner image per operating system recorded by digest | the same |
| Operating-system payload | system web view excluded from the artifact and named as an external dependency | the same |
| Packaging | the same signed/notarized state for both or, for the cost-free pass, both unsigned; the same archive algorithm and settings; installer/package metadata either present for both or absent for both | the same |
| **Held as stated** | v2.14.0; module lock `6e7366bf1a06e396…`; x86_64 on all three; WebView2 payload left out on Windows; unsigned but for the ad-hoc macOS signature noted below | 2.11.5 with build helper 2.6.3; package lock `c0e5adbb5d47a569…`; x86_64 on all three; installer web-view step set to skip; unsigned |
| macOS executable bytes | 8,828,608 | **6,625,072** |
| macOS complete `.app` bytes, unpacked | 8,857,766 | **6,642,165** |
| macOS downloadable archive bytes | 3,690,956 | **2,378,145** |
| Windows complete installed-directory bytes | 10,234,368 | **8,573,952** |
| Windows downloadable archive bytes | 4,149,389 | **2,466,373** |
| Linux complete packaged-payload bytes, excluding declared system dependencies | 9,076,872 | **6,867,136** |
| Linux downloadable package bytes | 3,767,499 | **2,349,240** |
| External runtime/dependency list and minimum versions | WebKit on macOS, WebKit2GTK 4.1 with GTK3 on Linux, WebView2 Runtime on Windows — all external. Published minimums: macOS 10.13+ for release and 11.0+ on Apple silicon, Windows 10/11, GTK3 with WebKitGTK on Linux | the same three external web views. Published minimums: macOS 10.15+, Windows 7+, WebKitGTK 4.1 on Linux |

**The comparison record includes the source digest, dependency locks, commands, runner-image identities and artifact digests, so either result can be reproduced without buying anything.** For each candidate, the decision figure is the largest of its three like-for-like downloadable artifacts, because all three must ship. A result is *materially smaller* only when the larger candidate is at least **10% and 1 MiB** larger at that largest-system comparison; otherwise size is a tie and the named properties, not an unrelated application, decide. ~~**Until the sheet is complete, the current discriminator is unproved and H7 is a contingency order rather than authority to call Wails selected.**~~ **The sheet is complete and the discriminator is proved.**

**H5c. What the filled sheet decides, stated against the rule above so nobody has to re-derive it.** Each candidate's largest downloadable artifact is its Windows one: **Wails 4,149,389 bytes against Tauri 2,466,373 bytes.** Wails is larger by **1,683,016 bytes — 1.605 MiB and 68.2 per cent** — so both thresholds are cleared and this is not a tie. **And the answer does not rest on that one figure**: Tauri is smaller on all three systems, in the archive and in the unpacked payload alike, by margins from 19.4 to 68.2 per cent. **Tauri v2 is the selected shell, and the desktop application is written in Rust.**

**H5d. Three limits travel with the figures and none of them moves the result.** **One**, Wails signs its own macOS bundle ad-hoc and Tauri does not, so the *both unsigned* row is met exactly on Windows and Linux and not on macOS; the signature is about 71 KB against a 2.1 MiB gap. **Two**, neither candidate was given release-profile size tuning beyond stripped symbols; Tauri's own pages recommend more, which would widen the margin rather than close it. **Three**, the macOS artifacts were cross-built for x86_64 on an Apple-silicon runner — both of them, identically — so no Apple-silicon artifact was measured. **A fourth is not a limit but a warning about one row**: on Windows the two dependency lists are not comparable, because a Go binary declares one system library up front and loads the rest while running. What is settled on that row is that neither artifact carries the web view.

**H6. The required properties, and a candidate failing any one of them is not this route.**

1. **One codebase, and real release artifacts that can be compared per system.** Neutralinojs fails this one: its single undivided bundle cannot be placed against the others, and the ruling requires the comparison to be per system.
2. **A compiled binary**, not an interpreted bundle, and **not a bundled browser engine**.
3. **The operating system's own web view** on each of the three.
4. **A signed downloadable binary on all three systems**, since [Whole app](../Specs/Whole%20app.md) settles that *"The user downloads a binary from Smoozy's website"*. **Corrected 2026-08-21: this said Flutter fails it, and Flutter's own pages say the opposite.** Its Windows build page lists three distribution options of which two are not the store, and says *"Distributing your application by self hosting it on a website requires a certificate signed by a Certificate Authority known to Windows"*; its Windows store page says outright *"You are not required to publish Windows apps through the Microsoft Store"*; its Linux build page documents a plain bundle directory; its macOS page documents distributing the `.app` *"perhaps from your own website"* (all four read 2026-08-20). **The four addresses were recorded nowhere in this project and are supplied 2026-08-23 by reading the pages themselves; all four quotations stand word for word and one further clause is corrected against its own page.** Windows build — *Building Windows apps with Flutter*, `https://docs.flutter.dev/platform-integration/windows/building`, read 2026-08-23, whose *Distributing Windows apps* section carries the three options and the self-hosting sentence. Windows store — *Build and release a Windows desktop app*, `https://docs.flutter.dev/deployment/windows`, read 2026-08-23, in full: *"You are not required to publish Windows apps through the Microsoft Store, particularly if you prefer more control over the distribution experience or don't want to deal with the certification process."* Linux build — *Build Linux apps with Flutter*, `https://docs.flutter.dev/platform-integration/linux/building`, read 2026-08-23: *"The executable binary can be found in your project under build/linux/x64/&lt;build mode&gt;/bundle/"*, with `lib` and `data` beside it. macOS — *Building macOS apps with Flutter*, `https://docs.flutter.dev/platform-integration/macos/building`, read 2026-08-23: *"To distribute your macOS application, you can either distribute it through the macOS App Store, or you can distribute the .app itself, perhaps from your own website."* **The corrected clause: this said the Linux build page *names no store*, and it names one** — *"To learn how to publish a Linux application to the Snap Store, consult Build and release a Linux application to the Snap Store"*, beside a separate packaging guide for `.deb` and `.rpm`. **It names them as alternatives and requires neither**, so the property is held on the documented bundle directory rather than on a silence, and **the strike is unaffected.** **And this document's own Flutter figures were read off a downloadable, non-store release** — LocalSend v1.18.0's `.dmg`, `.zip` and `.deb` are 52.49 / 25.80 / 24.58 MB, which is H1's Flutter row to two decimals. **It measured the thing it then said did not exist.** No candidate fails this property.
5. **A licence that permits a closed-source download**, resolvable to the module. **Corrected 2026-08-21: this said Qt 6's module split *could not be resolved*, and Qt publishes it on one page.** `https://doc.qt.io/qt-6/licensing.html` (Qt 6.11, read 2026-08-20) states *"Note: For open-source licensed Qt, some specific parts (modules) are not available under the GNU Lesser General Public License version 3, but under the GNU General Public License (GPL) instead."* and then lists them under the heading *Modules available under GNU General Public License v3*: Qt Canvas Painter, Qt CoAP, Qt Graphs, Qt GRPC, Qt HTTP Server, Qt Lottie Animation, Qt MQTT, Qt Network Authorization, Qt Qml Compiler, Qt Quick 3D, Qt Quick 3D Physics, Qt Quick Timeline, Qt Virtual Keyboard, Qt Wayland Compositor. Every module page repeats its own terms — Qt Widgets is *"available under free software licenses: The GNU Lesser General Public License, version 3, or the GNU General Public License, version 2"*. And The Qt Company's own obligations page says the LGPLv3 *"allows for keeping your application source code closed as long as all the requirements of LGPLv3 are met"* (read 2026-08-20). **That page's address was recorded nowhere in this project and is supplied 2026-08-23 by reading it: *Obligations of the GPL and LGPL*, `https://www.qt.io/licensing/open-source-lgpl-obligations`, read 2026-08-23.** The sentence stands word for word, in its own context — *"The primary open-source license is the GNU Lesser General Public License v. 3 ("LGPL"). With the LGPL license option, you can use the essential libraries and some add-on libraries of Qt. This allows for keeping your application source code closed as long as all the requirements of LGPLv3 are met."* — and the same page carries the split `doc.qt.io/qt-6/licensing.html` publishes: *"Please be advised that some parts of Qt are only provided under GPL for open source users, not under LGPL."* **So the licence is resolvable and this property does not disqualify it.** What LGPLv3 does cost — a relinkable binary and installation information — is real and is a change to what ships, and it is not this property's business.
6. **The platform's native menu bar and the platform's native file picker exposed to the application** ([18.3](#183-what-the-shell-must-expose)).
7. **Documented signing and notarization support**, which Fact 96 records for Wails by name.
8. **A window frame that follows the operating system's light-or-dark setting, or nothing set at all.** Added 2026-08-21 with [2.6](#26-the-colour-scheme-read-and-declared). **Wails holds it on two of three and publishes nothing for the third** (H23), which is why it is written as *or nothing set at all* rather than as a flat requirement: a shell that offers no Linux appearance option leaves the frame to the window manager, and that is the same outcome as not setting one. **What a candidate must not do is force a theme**, since Design settles that Smoozy takes no theme decision.

**H6a. The window frame is given up on all three systems and the title bar is the application's own — the buttons, the drag region and the bar's height. The shell exposes it, and the residue is per system. Added 2026-08-21, per Row 425.** [11.4](#114-the-achieved-rate-reading) R15's reading is drawn in a bar the operating system's own title bar cannot hold, at a height [Design values](../Design/Design%20values.md) fixes, beside window buttons the application lays out. That is an application-drawn title bar, and until this clause the surface it needs was in neither H6's property list nor [2.4](#24-what-the-small-shell-costs-and-it-lands-on-the-interface)'s costing. **All quotations below are the shell's own documentation, read 2026-08-21; `wails.io` returns 403 to a fetch, so these are the site's own documentation sources and the release notes, the same route Facts 117 and 120 took.**

- **The frame is given up by one option, and the shell states the effect in its own words.** `https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wailsapp/wails/master/website/docs/reference/options.mdx` — **Frameless**, type `bool`: *"When set to `true`, the window will have no borders or title bar."* The guide it points at, `https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wailsapp/wails/master/website/docs/guides/frameless.mdx`, opens *"Wails supports application that have no frames."* **The option carries no per-platform table**, unlike `WindowStartState` on the same page, which does.
- **The drag region is a CSS property on any element, and it is not a shell-drawn strip.** Same guide: *"Any HTML element that has the CSS style `--wails-draggable:drag` will act as a "drag handle". This property applies to all child elements. If you need to indicate that a nested element should not drag, then use the attribute '--wails-draggable:no-drag' on that element."* The property and value are themselves configurable — `CSSDragProperty`, *"Indicates the CSS property to use to identify which elements can be used to drag the window. Default: `--wails-draggable`"*, and `CSSDragValue`, *"Indicates what value the `CSSDragProperty` style should have to drag the window. Default: `drag`."* **So the drawn bar is the drag handle and the buttons inside it are marked `no-drag`.**
- **The buttons are the application's own and are wired to the shell's runtime, which publishes each act.** `https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wailsapp/wails/master/website/docs/reference/runtime/window.mdx` — `WindowMinimise`, *"Minimises the window"*; `WindowToggleMaximise`, *"Toggles between Maximised and UnMaximised"*; with `WindowMaximise`, `WindowUnmaximise`, `WindowIsMaximised` and `WindowIsMinimised` beside them, each published in a Go and a JS form. Closing is `Quit` — `https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wailsapp/wails/master/website/docs/reference/runtime/intro.mdx`, *"Quits the application."* **There is no shell-supplied button set in frameless mode and none is looked for.**
- **The bar's height is not the shell's at all**, because with the frame gone the web view is the whole window. `--title-h: 38px` is one row of the page's own grid, and no platform figure is read, matched or reconciled.
- **That it works on each of the three is the shell's own release notes rather than an inference from one flag.** `https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wailsapp/wails/master/website/src/pages/changelog.mdx`: **Linux** — v2.1.0, 2022-10-18, *"Add missing resize for frameless window on Linux"* and *"Fixed Linux frameless window drag"*; **macOS** — v2.0.0-beta.43, 2022-08-08, *"Allow MacOS frameless window to be miniturisable"*; **Windows** — v2.4.0, 2023-03-08, *"Frameless resize now sets the cursor on documentElement, otherwise resizing cursor won't be shown outside of the body rectangle"*, and v2.10, 2025-02-15, *"[windows] Fixed frameless window flickering when minimizing/restoring by preventing unnecessary redraws"*. **The route is named on all three and is still being maintained on all three.**
- **Windows carries one option and it is left at its default.** Same options page, under the Windows block: `DisableFramelessWindowDecorations` — *"Setting this to `true` will remove the window decorations in [Frameless](#Frameless) mode. This means there will be no 'Aero Shadow' and no 'Rounded Corners' shown for the window. Please note that 'Rounded Corners' are only supported on Windows 11."* Default `false`, and it stays there: Smoozy gives up the title bar and keeps the shadow and the corners.
- **macOS is the one system with a second route, and it is named so a later reader does not have to find it.** The same page publishes a `mac.TitleBar` struct — *"TitlebarAppearsTransparent | Makes the titlebar transparent. This has the effect of hiding the titlebar and the content fill the window"*, *"FullSizeContent | Makes the webview fill the entire window"*, *"HideTitleBar | Removes [NSWindowStyleMaskTitled] from the style mask"* — with the preset `mac.TitleBarHiddenInset()`. **That route keeps the system's own window buttons in the system's own place and lets the page fill the window behind them.** Which of the two the Mac takes is Design's, because the difference is entirely in what the user sees; the architecture supplies both and takes the frameless one, which is what the mockup draws. **DESIGN TOOK THE SECOND ROUTE FOR THE MAC ON 2026-09-02, Creator-stated, and this clause is noted rather than changed** — it already supplies both and already says the choice is Design's, so nothing here moves. The Mac keeps the system frame and the system's own three buttons, with that system's rounded corners and shadow; **Windows and Linux take the frameless route and Smoozy draws the buttons**, so every sentence above still governs those two — with the placement each system's own rather than the drawing's: Windows reserves the upper right and Linux publishes a setting naming which buttons and which side, its own default being a close button alone. `Preparation/Design/Whole app.md` carries both and its sources file the citations. `Preparation/Design/Whole app.md`, *The window's own chrome*, carries the reasoning and the one cost: with the frame kept, the selected shell publishes that a window cannot be dragged while it is not the front window. **On the selected shell the two routes are the same two**, read 2026-09-02 from its configuration reference: `decorations` false for the frameless one, and `titleBarStyle: "Overlay"` with `decorations: true` for this one, whose own published caveats are that title-bar height varies by system version, that a custom drag region is needed, and the not-in-focus drag limit above.
- **What is refused rather than guessed: the shell says what frameless removes only as *no borders or title bar*, and names the standard window buttons nowhere.** Whether a frameless macOS window keeps them is therefore not established here, and nothing below rests on it — Smoozy draws three buttons of its own on every system either way. [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 39.
- **Two costs that land on the user and are stated rather than absorbed.** Full screen: the frameless guide says outright *"If you allow your application to go fullscreen, this drag functionality will be disabled"*, which bears on Row 305's Window menu offering a full-screen item. And **the shell publishes no statement about the behaviours a native frame carries** — drag-to-edge arrangement, double-click-to-zoom, the system window menu — so **nothing here claims any of them survives.** Item 39.
- **[2.6](#26-the-colour-scheme-read-and-declared) H23 is narrowed rather than contradicted, and saying so is the point.** H23 reads the Windows `Theme` and macOS `Appearance` defaults as leaving *"the title bar and border"* to the system. **With the frame gone there is no system title bar to follow on any of the three**, so those defaults govern the border alone and the bar's colour is the page's own — H17's `prefers-color-scheme` reading and H19's `color-scheme` declaration, which are what the mockup already carries. **Rows 321 and 327 do not move**: the same preference is read either way, and the surface that reads it is now Smoozy's rather than the system's, which is the stronger of the two positions.
- **This is not a ninth entry on H6's list, and the reason is what rests on it.** H6's properties disqualify a candidate; what rests on this one is a **drawing**, and `Preparation/Design/Mockup/About this mockup.md` already rules that where a drawing and the documents disagree the drawing moves. So a candidate that could not give the frame up would cost the drawn title bar and no promise. ~~**Whether Tauri v2 or Electron publishes the same facility has deliberately not been read; that check is required if H5b places Tauri first or a fallback reaches Electron.**~~ **H5b placed Tauri first, so that check was run on 2026-09-02 and it holds — see [H6b](#22-the-route-chosen-and-the-properties-it-is-held-to).** Electron's remains unread and is owed only if a fallback ever reaches it. It is checked exactly as a property is checked, per H5a, and it holds for Wails against the shell's own documentation.
- **[H16](#25-the-rulings-stop-run-and-not-reached)'s walk is extended by this clause and its answer does not move.** This is a promise Wails touches that was not on that walk, which is the condition the Design sweep was run to find; walked here, it does not fail on any of the three, and nothing reaches the Creator.
- **[Method](Method.md)'s third escalation, checked and declined.** Nothing new is put on a screen — the three window buttons replace controls the user already had rather than adding any — there is no place to look, nothing to type, and nothing that can be lost. **What the user could lose is a window-manager gesture, not a thing Smoozy holds**, and the window's own chrome is Design's drawing rather than this document's addition.

**H6b. The selected shell held to H6's eight properties and to H6a's facility, added 2026-09-02 when H5b placed Tauri v2 first. Every source is that project's own documentation, read 2026-09-02. Label: documented. Nothing fails.**

1. **One codebase, real release artifacts per system** — three were built and measured separately at H5b.
2. **A compiled binary, not a bundled engine** — H1's own classification, and the built binaries link the system web view rather than carrying one.
3. **The operating system's own web view on all three** — read off the binaries: WebKit on macOS, `libwebkit2gtk-4.1` on Linux, the WebView2 Runtime on Windows.
4. **A signed downloadable binary on all three** — the project publishes a signing page for each of the three systems.
5. **A licence permitting a closed-source download** — published as *Apache-2.0 OR MIT*.
6. **The platform's native menu bar and native file picker** — *Window Menu*, `https://v2.tauri.app/learn/window-menu/`: *"Native application menus can be attached to both to a window or system tray. Available on desktop."* And *Dialog*, `https://v2.tauri.app/plugin/dialog/`: *"Native system dialogs for opening and saving files along with message dialogs"*, with all three systems listed.
7. **Documented signing and notarization support** — *macOS Code Signing*, `https://v2.tauri.app/distribute/sign/macos/`, carries a Notarization section naming both of Apple's routes; *Windows Code Signing*, `https://v2.tauri.app/distribute/sign/windows/`, covers the other.
8. **A frame that follows the system's light-or-dark setting, or nothing set at all** — the configuration reference, `https://v2.tauri.app/reference/config/`, on the window's theme: *"The initial window theme. Defaults to the system theme. Only implemented on Windows and macOS 10.14+."* **That is the same two-of-three shape [H23](#26-the-colour-scheme-read-and-declared) records for Wails**, and it is why property 8 was written as *or nothing set at all*. Nothing forces a theme.

**H6a's facility is published in full and the drawn title bar survives the change of shell.** From *Window Customization*, `https://v2.tauri.app/learn/window-customization/`, and the configuration reference. The frame is given up by one setting — `decorations`, *"Whether the window should have borders and bars"*, default true, with no per-system table, exactly the shape Wails' equivalent has. The drag region is a page attribute, `data-tauri-drag-region`. The three buttons are the application's own, wired to published calls — minimise, toggle-maximise and close on the current window — each with a named permission. macOS keeps the same second route Wails offered, a transparent title bar, so the choice recorded for the Mac stands.

**Two differences a builder must carry, and neither costs a promise.** **The drag region does not inherit:** *"data-tauri-drag-region will only work on the element to which it is directly applied. If you want the drag behavior to apply to child elements as well, you'll need to add it to each child individually."* Wails' property applied to child elements; this one does not, so the drawn bar marks its own drag elements. **And one cost is published where Wails published none:** *"For macOS, using a custom titlebar will also lose some features provided by the system, such as moving or aligning the window."* H6a already recorded that Wails states nothing about what a native frame carries and that nothing here claims any of it survives. **This narrows a cost already accepted rather than adding one** — what is lost is a window-manager gesture, not a thing Smoozy holds — and [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 39 stands as written.

**H6c. [H35](#28-the-privileged-web-content-boundary)'s five development surfaces, re-checked against the selected shell as [2.8](#28-the-privileged-web-content-boundary) requires. Three are answered by that project's own publications and two carry over unchanged as this document's own build requirement.** **The inspector** is off in a release build unless a named build feature turns it on: the configuration reference says *"This API works in debug builds, but requires devtools feature flag to enable it in release builds"*, and the debugging guide says *"By default, the inspector is only enabled in development and debug builds unless you enable it with a Cargo feature"* and *"To enable the devtools in production builds, you must enable the `devtools` Cargo feature"* (`https://v2.tauri.app/develop/debug/`, read 2026-09-02). The feature is not enabled. **The development and reload server** is the project's own development command rather than a build output, so a release artifact carries none — the same shape Wails has. **The context menu** has no separate published setting on this shell that any page read names; what is established is that the *Inspect Element* entry belongs to the inspector, which is absent from a release build. **Whether the web view's own ordinary context menu is present in a release build is not established by any publication read, and is not claimed here.** **Debug bindings and source maps** were established by no publication on either shell and stay exactly what H35 made them: this document's own build requirement, carried at [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 44. **A build in which any of the five is enabled is not a release artifact and may not be signed or published** — H35's rule, unchanged by the change of shell.

**H7. The contingency order is shorter than a list by unrelated application size would be. ORDERED 2026-09-02, H5b being complete.** Wails v2 and Tauri v2 are the two candidates in the preferred system-web-view class. ~~**Their order is deliberately not stated until H5b is complete.**~~ **The sheet is complete, so the order is stated: Tauri v2 first, Wails v2 second.** Qt 6 and Flutter are not system-web-view routes on H1's own classification, and Electron bundles the engine the standing ruling says not to choose merely to keep more promises. Those facts place the candidate classes; the Wails/Tauri comparison is now answered by measurement.

1. **Tauri v2 — selected.** Materially smaller on H5b's own rule, and every one of H6's eight properties holds against its own documentation (H6b). Tauri is also the one candidate that addresses cross-platform signing head-on (E9).
2. **Wails v2**, if Tauri fails a required property at Build. None has failed.
3. **Electron**, only under the ruling's own warning that it is not chosen merely because it keeps more promises.

**Qt 6 and Flutter are not steps in this order.** Both fail property 3 — neither is a system-web-view route on H1's own rows — and that failure is outright rather than a matter of degree, **so the order that a list by size alone would have started at 49.2 does not exist.** *(Corrected 2026-08-21: this said Qt 6 fails property 5 and Flutter fails property 4. Both of those disqualifications were wrong and are struck; the order is unchanged and the ground for it moved.)* **Neutralinojs cannot be placed at all**, per H3 and property 1.

**Where a step is taken, it is measured on H5b's largest of the candidate's three equivalent figures**, because all three must ship. A property failure is a step down this written contingency list; **the first step itself remains unproved until the sheet closes it.**

### 2.3 The two negatives that bear on Row 280, weighed rather than noted

**H8. Wails documents no self-update anywhere, and that does not decide Row 280.** Row 280 is kept by **the operating system's own replacement mechanism**, which Fact 74 answers on all three systems — not by any candidate's updater. [Section 20](#20-update-verification-and-replacement) is written against Apple's, Microsoft's and the kernel's own documented behaviour and calls no framework facility. **So a framework that ships no updater costs work and not a promise.**

**H9. The same argument disposes of Electron's own negative, and the negative is narrower than this clause used to state. Ground repaired 2026-08-23; the conclusion is unchanged.** This read *"it documents no automatic update on Linux at all"*, flat. What Electron's own page says is *"There is no built-in support for auto-updater on Linux"* (read 2026-08-20) — a statement about the framework's own facility rather than about the route. **Linux auto-update on that route is documented, outside Electron's own docs:** electron-builder, `https://www.electron.build/docs/features/auto-update/`, read 2026-08-20 — *"Linux is supported (not only macOS and Windows)"*, with AppImage, DEB, Pacman and RPM as auto-updatable targets (`Facts.md` Fact 96, corrected 2026-08-21). **Nothing in the argument turns on either reading**, which is why it is stated rather than absorbed: the update promise is kept **below** the framework on every route ([Section 20](#20-update-verification-and-replacement) calls no framework facility on any of the three), so **Electron's larger size buys nothing here** — and that is the argument that stops the ruling being read backwards.

**H10. What Wails' own version notice costs is named rather than absorbed. Kept whole, and restated 2026-09-06 as a reading of Wails rather than a statement about Smoozy**, [H5b](#22-the-route-chosen-and-the-properties-it-is-held-to) having selected Tauri v2 on 2026-09-02. Every page of Wails' current major version carries a notice that the next one is in beta. **The rule this clause exists for is not Wails' and does not move with the shell:** nothing in this document rests on a facility announced for a version that has not shipped. **What the selected shell publishes is H10a below**, and [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 21 is written on that reading rather than on this one.

**H10a. The selected shell's own position on a next major version, which is the re-reading H10 owed once the shell changed. Added 2026-09-06, found at Build. Every source below is that project's own publication, read 2026-09-06. Label: documented.**

- **The current documentation carries no next-version notice anywhere read.** Neither the documentation home `https://v2.tauri.app/`, nor the project home `https://tauri.app/`, nor a deep guide page — `https://v2.tauri.app/develop/` — carries a banner, admonition or notice that a next major version is in beta, in development, or that this documentation is for an old version.
- **The silence is a silence in a place where that project does write such notices**, which is what makes it worth anything. Its previous major version's documentation carries one, in its own words: *"This documentation covers Tauri 1 (**old version**). For the latest version, see https://tauri.app/"* (`https://v1.tauri.app/`).
- **The newest published release is the one this document is measured on, and no prerelease of a next major version is listed beside it.** *Tauri Core Releases*, `https://v2.tauri.app/release/core/`: the newest entry is **2.11.5, dated Jul 1, 2026** — the exact release [H5b](#22-the-route-chosen-and-the-properties-it-is-held-to) built its artifact from. The page carries a *2.0 Prereleases* section for that version's own prereleases and no section for any later major version.
- **A next major version is planned and unreleased, and that is published as a work list rather than as a notice.** `https://github.com/tauri-apps/tauri/milestone/5` — milestone **3.0**, open, no description, **no due date**, 29% complete, 12 items open and 5 closed.
- **One item on that list touches something this document declares, and it is named here rather than left to be discovered.** The open item *"feat(linux): migrate to GTK4 and WebKitGTK 6.0"* is a move off exactly the pair [E4](#191-what-each-system-is-built-as) declares as the Linux package's runtime dependencies. **Nothing is owed today** — the released version is 2.11.5 and E4 is written on what 2.11.5 publishes — but a Linux artifact built on a later major version would declare a different pair, and E4's row is where that lands.
- **What none of it says, stated as silence rather than filled in:** no date, no beta, no release candidate, no changelog, no statement of what breaks. Nothing here is a prediction that a next version arrives, and nothing in this document waits on one.

### 2.4 What the small shell costs, and it lands on the interface

**H11. The three system web views are three different engines** — one self-updating, one frozen to the operating-system version, and one whose picture its own project calls *"very hard to compile accurate information about"*, telling developers to check feature support per version. **That is the documented cost of the small shell**, in the projects' own words, and it lands on every clause the interface is drawn by.

**H12. Two consequences are taken here rather than left to Build.** **Row 304 is arranged as a character substitution and not as a layout rule** ([17.2](#172-why-a-substitution-and-not-a-layout-rule)), which is what makes it hold on three engines and on the native surfaces no engine draws. And **no interface behaviour is built on a capability that is not present on all three engines**; where one is, the feature is declared per engine in Design and Build rather than assumed. [Section 22](#22-unresolved) records what has not been checked against the frozen engine.

**H13. The artifact ships against each system's own web view, and on Windows it is built with the shell's `-webview2 embed` option; the paragraph below carries the ruling that settled which option.** **Corrected 2026-08-21, and the correction changed what was on the table rather than only a figure.** This weighed exactly two options — one project's Windows offline installer adding *"~127MB"*, its fixed-version option *"~180MB"*, and a self-contained Linux bundle that *"bundles all dependencies"* — declined both, and left the application to say so rather than run. **Wails documents four, on its own page, and one of them costs ~150 kilobytes.** `https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wailsapp/wails/master/website/docs/guides/windows.mdx`, read 2026-08-20 (wails.io returns 403 to a fetch; this is the site's own documentation source): *"By using the `-webview2` flag when building, you can decide what your application will do when a suitable runtime is not detected (including if the installed runtime is too old). The four options are: 1. Download 2. Embed 3. Browser 4. Error"*, and of Embed — *"This option embeds the official bootstrapper within the application. If no suitable runtime has been found, the application will offer to run the bootstrapper. This adds ~150k to the binary size."* Same page: *"Wails applications built for Windows have a runtime requirement on the Microsoft WebView2 Runtime. Windows 11 will have this installed by default, but some machines won't."*

**So *grows back toward engine scale* is true of the two options this paragraph named and false of Embed.** **This document takes Wails' option 2, *Embed*, and that is the Creator's ruling rather than this document's choice. Creator-stated 2026-08-23**, in their own words: *"150 KB and you're bothering to ask me? of course it's okay."* **What was put to them:** a user on a supported Mac, Windows or Linux machine whose system web view is absent or too old gets a downloaded Smoozy that starts and says it cannot run; the alternative grows the download by roughly 150 kilobytes and always works. **They ruled: take the cost.** **What it replaces is option 4, *Error***, which this document took until that date and which was chosen without the other three being on the page. **So the Windows artifact is built with `-webview2 embed`**, and where no suitable runtime is detected the application offers to run the embedded official bootstrapper instead of refusing to run. [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 23 is closed on it and [Section 23](#23-moments-that-reached-the-creator) records the moment.

**Two things the offer carries, from Microsoft's own page for the thing being embedded**, `https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/microsoft-edge/webview2/concepts/distribution`, page dated 2024-06-27 and updated 2026-07-15, read 2026-08-23. **It needs no administrator:** *"If you run the installer from an elevated process or command prompt, the Runtime is installed as per-machine. If you don't run the installer from an elevated process or command prompt, the Runtime will be installed as per-user."* — so the route is consistent with [E3](#191-what-each-system-is-built-as) and [T4](#131-what-a-plain-downloaded-binary-must-carry-before-it-can-post-at-all), under which nothing in this application ever runs elevated, and the user is never asked for an administrator. **And it needs Microsoft's servers:** *"The WebView2 Runtime Bootstrapper downloads and installs the Evergreen Runtime from Microsoft servers that matches the user's device architecture."*, that page's own online-client route, against a separate standalone installer it names *"For offline clients"*. **The same page prices the bootstrapper at *"a tiny (approximately 2 MB) installer"* where the shell prices embedding it at *"~150k"***; the two publications are not reconciled here and neither figure changes the ruling's direction, both being far below the *"~127MB"* and *"~180MB"* this paragraph declined. Both are recorded at [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 49.

**H13a. What the ruling reaches, and it is one system of three.** `-webview2` is a **Windows** build flag against the Microsoft WebView2 Runtime and is neither a Linux nor a macOS mechanism. The ruling closes the Windows half; the other two are closed or open on their own grounds, and nothing here claims the ruling fixes a system it does not reach. **The flag is the provisional shell's**, on the same footing as E4's GTK and WebKitGTK halves: **what the Creator ruled is that the cost is taken, not which option name carries it**, so if [H5b](#22-the-route-chosen-and-the-properties-it-is-held-to) selects Tauri v2 the equivalent is re-derived from that project's own publications and the ruling stands unchanged. **H5b did select Tauri v2, on 2026-09-02, so the re-derivation is owed and is named here rather than left implicit.** The equivalent on the selected shell is the installer's own web-view step, which that project publishes as a setting with an embedding choice among its values; **the comparison artifact was built with that step set to skip, deliberately, because H5b's own row requires the system web view to be excluded and named as an external dependency.** **A comparison build is not a release build, and the ruling is about the release.** **What is owed before the first Windows release artifact is one reading of that setting's published values, to name which one carries the ruled cost** — it is a reading of one page and no machine, and it is recorded at [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 65.

- **Windows — closed by the ruling, on both halves of the condition.** The flag's own scope is *"when a suitable runtime is not detected (including if the installed runtime is too old)"* (H13), so a device Microsoft's distribution page describes as not carrying the Runtime pre-installed, and a device carrying one too old, both meet the offer rather than the refusal.
- **Linux — closed already, and not by this ruling.** [E4](#191-what-each-system-is-built-as)'s artifact is a signed package declaring the shell's own `libgtk3` and `libwebkit` requirement as dependencies for its release-matrix row, and [Z10](#203-linux-a-package-transaction) installs exactly that package through the distribution's package mechanism. Debian Policy §7.2, quoted at E4 — *"A package will not be configured unless all of the packages listed in its `Depends` field have been correctly configured"* — so the Ubuntu-server case Canonical's manifest establishes is met **before there is anything to launch**: the declared runtime libraries are satisfied by the transaction, or the transaction refuses and names the missing one. **Which of those two the distribution's own tooling does for a local package install is stated by no page read here**, and nothing in this clause needs it — both outcomes happen before a launch. **There is therefore no Linux case of a downloaded Smoozy that starts and says it cannot run**, which is the case the ruling was put on. That the package route is the binary [Whole app](../Specs/Whole%20app.md) promises is the Creator's separate ruling of 2026-08-22 ([Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 45), and what a refusal costs the user is what that ruling was shown and took.
- **macOS — the absent half cannot arise, and the too-old half is untouched by the ruling.** `WKWebView` is a class of the WebKit framework Apple lists as available from **macOS 10.10** onward (`https://developer.apple.com/documentation/webkit/wkwebview`, read 2026-08-23; that page renders by script and its own data endpoint `https://developer.apple.com/tutorials/data/documentation/webkit/wkwebview.json` carries the content — module *WebKit*, macOS `introducedAt` `10.10`, and the abstract *"An object that displays interactive web content, such as for an in-app browser."*), and the shell's own installation page states no macOS runtime requirement at all — only *"Wails requires that the xcode command line tools are installed."*, which is a build machine's (`https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wailsapp/wails/master/website/docs/gettingstarted/installation.mdx`, read 2026-08-23; the page E4 quotes for Linux). **So there is nothing on macOS for a bootstrapper to install and no option to take.** **What stays open is the too-old half**, which H11 already names — that engine is frozen to the operating-system version — and which no publication read here bounds: [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 48.

**H14. Installed and unpacked sizes are documented by none of the six**, so every figure above is a download size and this document claims nothing about what the application occupies once installed.

**H15. Two hazards in the evidence itself are carried.** A release listing is not frozen — one asset was added days after publication — so a byte figure is durable while *that listing holds only these assets* is not. And one same-application comparison of 209 MB against 80 MB **rests on a filename alone** and is not leaned on anywhere.

### 2.5 The ruling's stop, run and not reached

**H16. Every promise Wails touches was checked against the ruling's own instruction to stop, and none of them is shown to fail.** Row 280 by H8. **Row 282 by H13, and the shell choice no longer costs anything on it — restated 2026-08-23 on the Creator's ruling of that day.** This clause read *"and this is the one the shell choice costs something on"*, on H13's former answer that where a system's web view is absent or too old the application says so **rather than running**, so that a machine Row 282 says Smoozy runs on could meet a downloaded binary that will not start. **H13 now takes option *Embed*** and the application offers to run the embedded bootstrapper instead, so on Windows that machine gets a working Smoozy; H13a states what the ruling reaches on each of the three and what it does not. **Whether any supported Mac, Windows or Linux configuration is such a machine is established for two of the three, and it was established by reading — corrected 2026-08-22, out of the first sanity sweep's verification round; this clause used to say it *is not established*, and added that there was no computer to pose it on for two of the three.** Microsoft's own distribution page says *"some devices might not have the Runtime pre-installed"* (`https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/microsoft-edge/webview2/concepts/distribution`, read 2026-08-20) and Wails says the same of its own applications — *"Windows 11 will have this installed by default, but some machines won't"* (H13). And Canonical's own image manifests, read 2026-08-20, show the Ubuntu 24.04.3 **server** image carrying no `libwebkit*` and no `libgtk-3-0` at all, while the **desktop** image carries `libgtk-3-0t64`, `libwebkit2gtk-4.1-0` and `libwebkitgtk-6.0-4`. **Both addresses were recorded nowhere in this project and are supplied 2026-08-23, by fetching the two manifests and reading them again**: `https://releases.ubuntu.com/24.04.3/ubuntu-24.04.3-live-server-amd64.manifest` and `https://releases.ubuntu.com/24.04.3/ubuntu-24.04.3-desktop-amd64.manifest`, both read 2026-08-23. **The re-reading agrees with the record in every particular and changes nothing that rests on it**: the server manifest is 689 lines and contains no line matching `libwebkit` or `libgtk`, and the desktop manifest carries `libgtk-3-0t64` 3.24.41-4ubuntu1.3, `libwebkit2gtk-4.1-0` 2.48.3-0ubuntu0.24.04.1 and `libwebkitgtk-6.0-4` 2.48.3-0ubuntu0.24.04.1 among 1,819 lines. **A published release manifest is a frozen artefact rather than a page that moves**, which is why the two dates carry the same reading. **So a supported Windows machine and a supported Linux install can each be such a machine, and no Windows or Linux computer was needed to find that out.** [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 23 carries what it broke and is closed on the ruling; [Section 23](#23-moments-that-reached-the-creator) records the check, the filing and the ruling. Row 284 by [13.1](#131-what-a-plain-downloaded-binary-must-carry-before-it-can-post-at-all), whose preconditions are the operating systems' and are the same on every route. Row 304 by [17.2](#172-why-a-substitution-and-not-a-layout-rule). Row 305 by [18.3](#183-what-the-shell-must-expose), which is a required property rather than an assumption. Rows 288, 290 and 303 touch no shell facility at all. **So the smaller route keeps every promise on every configuration this project can establish, and no promise is offered up for size.** **One thing here reached the Creator, and the *nothing here reaches the Creator* reading is refused rather than restored — decided 2026-08-23 on the record rather than on the prohibition that stood while the filing was owed.** The escalation was filed and the Creator ruled the same day (H13, [Section 23](#23-moments-that-reached-the-creator)), so one answer in this section did reach them and was decided by them; what is true now is that **nothing here is outstanding**, which is a different sentence and is the one this clause makes. **Row 282's edge is closed on Windows by that ruling and on Linux by the package transaction, and on macOS the absent case cannot arise while the too-old case is unresolved** (H13a, [Section 22](#22-unresolved) items 23, 48 and 49). **Naming the candidates changed no answer in this section but the fallback order**, and H7 says how. **The walk above is Wails', which is what it says; it is kept whole because every reading in it is still a true reading, and [H16a](#25-the-rulings-stop-run-and-not-reached) is the same walk re-run on the shell that was actually chosen.**

**H16a. The same walk re-run against the selected shell. Added 2026-09-06, found at Build, [H5b](#22-the-route-chosen-and-the-properties-it-is-held-to) having selected Tauri v2 on 2026-09-02.**

**Why [H6b](#22-the-route-chosen-and-the-properties-it-is-held-to) does not discharge H16, said first because the two look alike.** H6b asks whether the selected shell holds H6's **eight required properties** — a check on a candidate, whose failure moves the choice down H7's list. H16 asks a different question: whether any **promise** the shell touches fails, under the standing ruling's own instruction to stop if the smaller route cannot keep one. **Two of H16's legs are discharged by H6b** — Row 305's, which is property 6, and H6a's own extension — **and the rest are not**, because no property on H6's list mentions them. So the walk is re-run leg by leg. **Every leg keeps H16's answer. One of them rested on a citation belonging to the withdrawn shell, and that is a finding rather than a wording repair.**

- **Row 280 — kept, and the change of shell makes the argument easier rather than harder.** H8's ground is that Wails documents no self-update anywhere. **That ground is false of the selected shell**, which publishes an updater: *"Automatically update your Tauri app with an update server or a static JSON."*, `https://v2.tauri.app/plugin/updater/`, read 2026-09-06, listing Windows, Linux and macOS as supported. **Smoozy uses none of it.** [Section 20](#20-update-verification-and-replacement) is written against Apple's, Microsoft's and the kernel's own documented behaviour and calls no framework facility on any of the three, which is what both H8 and H9 turn on. **So the row is kept the way it always was — by the operating systems — and a framework that ships an updater buys nothing here, exactly as one that ships none cost nothing.**
- **Row 282, Windows — kept, and the option name is the selected shell's rather than H13's flag.** `-webview2 embed` is Wails' build flag; [H13a](#24-what-the-small-shell-costs-and-it-lands-on-the-interface) says so and names what is re-derived, and the re-derivation is done and carried at [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 65 — the installer's own web-view step, value `embedBootstrapper`. **The Creator ruled on 2026-08-23 that the cost is taken, not which option name carries it**, so the ruling stands unchanged on the selected shell and a Windows machine with no suitable runtime still meets an offer rather than a refusal.
- **Row 282, macOS — kept, and the absent case cannot arise on the selected shell either.** H13a's ground is Apple's own availability floor for `WKWebView`, macOS 10.10, which is that system's and no shell's. The selected shell's own published minimum is **macOS 10.15**, recorded at [H5b](#22-the-route-chosen-and-the-properties-it-is-held-to) — above the floor, so there is still nothing on macOS for a bootstrapper to install and no option to take. **The too-old half is untouched by the change of shell and stands at [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 48.**
- **Row 282, Linux — this is the leg that did not survive as written, and it is the finding of this re-walk.** H13a's Linux argument is that [E4](#191-what-each-system-is-built-as)'s package declares the shell's own GTK and WebKitGTK requirement, so the distribution's transaction satisfies it or refuses before there is anything to launch. **E4's declared pair was Wails', and E4's own sentence said the GTK and WebKitGTK halves are re-derived from the selected shell's publications if H5b chose it. H5b chose it on 2026-09-02 and no re-derivation was written until 2026-09-06** — the same half-kept promise [2.8](#28-the-privileged-web-content-boundary)'s constraint turned out to carry. **What breaks if it is left:** a package declaring the wrong runtime libraries as dependencies, which E4 itself names as precisely the failure its shape exists to prevent — a Smoozy that installs against libraries the shell does not use and starts against ones nothing declared. **It is repaired at E4 from that project's own publication, and the answer does not move:** the pair the selected shell's own bundler declares is `libwebkit2gtk-4.1-0` and `libgtk-3-0`, the same GTK3 and WebKitGTK 4.1 pair Canonical's two manifests are read against in H16 above. **So H16's Linux reading and item 23's closure stand exactly as they are**, and what was wrong was the citation and not the conclusion.
- **Row 284 — kept, untouched.** 13.1's three preconditions are the operating systems' own: a macOS bundle carrying an identifier, a Windows per-user Start-menu shortcut carrying an application identifier, and a Linux session bus with a process owning the notification name. **No shell facility appears in any of them**, and [E3](#191-what-each-system-is-built-as)'s Windows route is the application's own code on Microsoft's own worked example rather than an installer's. The same on every route, which is what H16 says.
- **Row 304 — kept, untouched.** 17.2's arrangement is a substitution in the text rather than a rule handed to a layout engine, which is what makes it hold on three engines and on the native surfaces no engine draws. [X4](#172-why-a-substitution-and-not-a-layout-rule) already carries the selected shell's name and records that the argument never depended on which shell it was.
