- **Row 305 — kept, and this is the leg H6b does discharge.** [18.3](#183-what-the-shell-must-expose) makes the platform's native menu bar a required property of the shell, and H6b's property 6 answers it on the selected project's own pages. **[K9](#183-what-the-shell-must-expose) still named the withdrawn shell and is repaired**, its old wording quoted there; the requirement was never Wails' and K10's fallback already names Tauri v2.
- **Rows 288, 290 and 303 — kept, untouched.** They touch no shell facility at all, which is a statement about those rows and not about any candidate, so no change of shell reaches them.
- **H6a's extension of this walk — kept.** H6a added the application-drawn title bar as a promise the shell touches and walked it there. H6b re-ran that facility in full on the selected shell and it holds, with two published differences named — the drag attribute not inheriting, and a macOS cost the other shell published nothing about — and neither costs a promise.

**So the smaller route keeps every promise on every configuration this project can establish, on the shell that was chosen as well as on the one that was not, and no promise is offered up for size.** **Nothing here reaches the Creator and nothing here is outstanding** — H16's own ending, and the re-walk does not change it.

### 2.6 The colour scheme, read and declared

**Constraint:** Rows 321 and 327, quoted at the head of this document, and Facts 117 and 118. **It is Section 2's because it is a property of the three engines Section 2 chose** and of the shell that puts them there; nothing about it is a service a module calls. [The shape](The%20shape.md#7-desktop-app-and-delivery)'s section 7 list **did not name it when this subsection was written, and was brought into line on 2026-08-22** ([Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 34) — and no other document could hold it. *(Put into the past tense 2026-08-29 at the stage-close repair; it went on making the claim in the present tense for seven days after item 34 closed on the shape's own edit.)*

**H17. There is one reading and it is the page's own `prefers-color-scheme` media query.** No platform appearance call is made on any of the three systems, and the name-reading adapter ([Section 10](#10-the-application-identity-adapter)) is not extended to carry it. Fact 117 establishes the query on all three engines from their own publications, and the standard's own words are narrower than the promise — *"The method by which the user expresses their preference can vary. It might be a system-wide setting exposed by the Operating System, or a setting controlled by the user agent."* **That each engine takes it from the operating system is per-engine evidence rather than the standard's**: WebView2's `PreferredColorScheme` defaults to `Auto`, *"which will follow whatever color scheme the OS is currently set to"*, and sets the value *"by setting the media feature `prefers-color-scheme`"*; WebKit matches *"the current system appearance"*; WebKitGTK enables the query and takes it from GTK, whose `gtk-interface-color-scheme` *"communicates the system-wide preference"*.

**H18. A change made while Smoozy is running arrives as an event, and that is what makes this cost [3.3](#33-ui-event-delivery) P10 nothing.** The `MediaQueryList` returned by `window.matchMedia()` fires `change` when its match state changes (*CSSOM View*, Fact 117), and WebKit's own words for the effect are *"auto switching with any appearance change"*. **Nothing is polled and no timer is created**, so P9 holds unchanged and P10's *exactly one timer* is not touched. **A builder who reaches for an interval here has broken P10**, and there is no case that needs one.

**H19. The declaration is the `color-scheme` property, and it is what reaches the surfaces the engine draws and Smoozy does not.** Fact 118: the used colour scheme must affect *"the default colors of scrollbars and other interaction UI"*, *"the default colors of form controls and other 'specially-rendered' elements"*, *"the default colors of other browser-provided UI, such as 'spellcheck' underlines"*, *"the value of system colors"* and *"the value of the light-dark() function"*, and on the root additionally *"the surface color of the canvas"* and *"the viewport's scrollbars"*. WebKit states the negative in as many words: *"Without this declaration, it would not be safe for the engine to use dark form controls or a dark color scheme"*. **So the declaration is not decoration and its absence is not a light-touch fault** — it is the whole of Row 327.

**H20. Both are stylesheet facts and neither is a capability call, which is why H12 does not bite the reading.** X4's argument holds here unchanged: a character substitution survives three engines because it is in the string, and **a media query and a property survive three engines because every one of them implements CSS**. What H12 does bite is H22 below, which is a per-engine declaration rather than an assumption.

**H21. Where the reading is unavailable, the answer is light, and there is no control to fall back on.** [Design](../Design/Whole%20app.md) settles that Smoozy has no theme switch of its own, so there is no third state and nothing to ask the user. **GTK's own default for the system-wide preference is `GTK_INTERFACE_COLOR_SCHEME_UNSUPPORTED`** — *"The system doesn't support color schemes"* — so a Linux desktop that has no such setting is a real case rather than a hypothetical one. `prefers-color-scheme` not matching `dark` yields the light tokens, which is the mockup's own bare `:root` block, and **that is the outcome rather than a failure to report**: nothing is written down, no condition record is raised, and the user is told nothing.

**H22. What the interface must carry, stated so a builder does not have to derive it from the mockup.** [Design values](../Design/Design%20values.md) fixes the arrangement and this document fixes what it rests on. Every token is declared four times — the bare root, `@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark)`, and the two explicit-declaration blocks — **and three `color-scheme` declarations sit beside them**: the bare root declares `light dark`, which covers the system's own dark as well and is why the media block needs none, and each of the two `:root[data-theme]` blocks declares the one it is. `Preparation/Design/Mockup/Smoozy.html` is the one home of the values and already carries all four. **The two explicit-declaration blocks are the mockup's own affordance and the shipped application never enters them**: nothing in Smoozy sets that attribute, because Smoozy has no switch, so the blocks are inert in the product and are kept only so the two files stay one file. *(Corrected 2026-08-31: this described three token blocks each carrying its own declaration, which is neither what the mockup carries nor what Row 327 asks for.)*

**H23. The window frame is not the web view, and it is answered separately on each system.** Fact 117, out of the shell's own documentation. **Windows**: the `Theme` option's default is `SystemDefault` — *"The theme will be based on the system default. If the user changes their theme, the application will update to use the new setting"* — and it is left at the default, so the title bar and border follow the system and follow a change. **macOS**: the `Appearance` option's default is *"DefaultAppearance uses the default system value"*, and it is left at the default; the system's own menu bar ([18.1](#181-what-each-platform-provides-read-from-its-own-documentation) K1) is drawn by the system and needs nothing from Smoozy. **Linux**: the shell publishes **no theme or appearance option at all** — its Linux options are the icon, translucency, the hardware-acceleration policy and the program name — so the frame is the window manager's and Smoozy neither sets it nor reads it. **A per-system value is never set on any of the three**, because setting one would be Smoozy taking the theme decision Design says it does not take.

**H24. Neither row costs the user anything, checked against [Method](Method.md)'s third escalation and declined.** Nothing new is put on a screen, nothing is added to Settings, there is nothing to type, and nothing can be lost. The user meets the theme their operating system is already in, which is the promise as written.

---

### 2.7 The two faces

**Constraint:** Row 322, quoted at the head of this document, and Fact 119. **Section 2's for the same reason [2.6](#26-the-colour-scheme-read-and-declared) is** — which faces the three engines resolve is a property of the engines Section 2 chose. **Section 17 is untouched by all of it**: that section is two character substitutions, and which face draws a string is a stylesheet question X4 deliberately does not depend on.

**H25. Neither face is shipped and neither is named per platform. Both are asked for by generic family**, which is what the mockup's token block already carries: `system-ui, -apple-system, "Segoe UI", sans-serif` for the interface, and `ui-monospace, "SF Mono", Menlo, Consolas, monospace` with `font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums` for anything that lines up in a column. **No platform's face name has to be known by this project, and none is written down anywhere.** Fact 119: `system-ui` is *"the default user interface font … on the platform on which the UA is running"*, whose stated purpose is *"to allow web content to integrate with the look and feel of the native OS"*; `ui-monospace` is *"the monospaced variant of the system's user interface"*.

**H26. The stack is a fallback chain and its last entry is the guarantee, which is why H12 does not bite.** The standard divides generics into ones that *"will always match a locally installed font"* and ones that *"may not match to a locally installed font on some systems"* — and it says outright of the second kind that *"ui-monospace is not expected to map to any font on platforms without an appropriate system font."* **`sans-serif` and `monospace` are of the first kind and each stack ends in one.** So no interface behaviour rests on a capability that might be absent: **the worst case is a different face, never a failure.**

**H27. An engine that has never heard of `ui-monospace` skips it, and does not throw the declaration away.** `font-family`'s value is `[ <font-family-name> | <generic-font-family> ]#`, so an unquoted identifier an engine does not recognise as a generic keyword is read as a **family name** — one that matches nothing, so matching moves on to the next entry. **That is what makes one stack safe across three engines** without any per-engine declaration, and it is stated here because a builder who quoted the generics would break exactly this: the standard notes that generic keywords *cannot be quoted*.

**H28. The column promise rests on the standard and not on any face's feature table.** *"The sole criterion of a monospace font is that all glyphs have the same fixed width"*, and *"monospace must always map to at least one matched font face."* **So a run set in that stack lines up down a column on any conforming engine, whatever the platform resolved it to.** `font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums` is kept and is not what the promise stands on — it *"Enables display of tabular numerals (OpenType feature: tnum)"*, an OpenType feature a face may not carry, and a monospaced face lines figures up with or without it.

**H29. Which strings go in which face is a stylesheet class and is declared by the module that hands the string over**, exactly as X8's prose-or-data choice is, and for the same reason: nobody drawing a screen can be asked to remember. **The closed list of what takes the fixed-width face is Design's** — sizes, frame counts, memory, hosts, ports, paths — and this document adds nothing to it and removes nothing from it. **One clause outside Design already leans on this and is now supplied**: `Keys` F3 requires a key fingerprint's display line *"(in the fixed-width face, always)"*, citing the Design document for it.

**H29a. The italic, per engine, now that italic carries a meaning of its own app-wide. Added 2026-08-25 at the tenth sitting, per Row 483; Fact 136's answer of record is [The three desktop systems read again](The%20three%20desktop%20systems%20read%20again%20—%20dying,%20revealing,%20resolving,%20and%20what%20the%20shell%20does%20not%20expose.md) §5.** The Design close took the reserved colour away from *a value Smoozy cannot confirm* and put the weight on the interface face's italic, with the dash beside it — so whether that italic exists is now load-bearing, and H25's own rule that no face is named per platform means the answer is per engine.

- **macOS: both generic families resolve faces with designed italics, and the ground is the strongest in this stage's typography evidence** — observed on the one machine by five independent routes, the decisive one being the synthesis-off test run in the real system web view with positive and negative controls that failed on demand: with synthesis disabled the two system families rendered unchanged while the no-italic controls collapsed byte-for-byte to upright. **Settled and good.**
- **Windows: not established, and the open question is narrower than it looks.** No publication names the face the Windows web view resolves `system-ui` to; what the check relocated is *where* the answer lives — **the engine's Windows font code returns the Windows menu font as the operating system reports it**, so the question is *which face Windows reports as the menu font*, a Windows question answerable against Microsoft's own window-metrics documentation and without a web view at all. The risk is real either way: **Segoe UI Variable ships fifteen named instances and not one italic, its only axes weight and optical size** — so if the menu font is the variable face, the app's italic is a machine shear; plain Segoe UI ships six italics. Both plausible `ui-monospace` faces ship italics. *(Documentation only; [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 50.)*
- **Linux: the platform has no answer to give, and no publication could give one.** `system-ui` resolves the desktop's own configured font — a property of the distribution and the user, not of the engine — and the monospaced family may resolve to nothing at all, the engine's mapping function having no branch for it. *(Documentation only, from the engine's own source.)*

**What holds the meaning up everywhere, and what does not.** The engines match italic and oblique as synonyms and synthesize a shear where the resolved face has no italic, and **a synthesized oblique is distinguishable from upright at small sizes** — at eleven pixels the synthesized obliques differ from their upright by *more* than the real italics do (measured, with the perceptual claim beyond the pixel count carried as a plausible expectation rather than a finding). And **the design's own sentence never rests the meaning on slant alone**: the dash travels beside the italic. Two limits carried rather than smoothed: the standard's own escape hatch — a strict engine refusing to synthesize for italic — cannot be relied on in either direction, so *a designed italic* is not promised off the Mac, only *a second state that reads*; and **Microsoft's own guidance excludes italic from the Windows type ramp** — *"Italic is excluded because it can reduce readability and legibility, particularly for people with dyslexia"* — which is vendor guidance recorded for Design's weighing, not a disagreement with the drawn meaning. **[Method](Method.md)'s third escalation, checked and declined**: nothing is added to a screen and the worst outcome the answer permits is a slanted machine shear, or upright text still carrying its dash.

**H30. Row 322 costs the user nothing, checked against [Method](Method.md)'s third escalation and declined.** Nothing is added to a screen, nothing is asked, and the worst outcome is text in a face that is not the platform's own.

---

### 2.8 The privileged web-content boundary

**Constraint:** [Security and licence](Security%20and%20licence.md#14-inside-the-desktop-application) §1.4 — *"`Desktop app and delivery` owns the adapter, the helper and the module API boundaries"* — and the scope line's *"module API boundaries"*: the binding surface between the page and the application is the module API boundary's web-view face, and what may cross it is this document's for the same reason [Section 4](#4-module-api-boundaries) is. **Corrected 2026-09-06, found at Build.** This read *"the JavaScript-to-Go binding surface"*, and Go is the language of the shell that did not win: [H4](#22-the-route-chosen-and-the-properties-it-is-held-to) settled Tauri v2 on 2026-09-02 and Build's step 0 settled Rust on both sides of Smoozy ([the Build plan](../Build/Plan.md)). **Nothing about the boundary moved and no line of code changed with it** — [Section 4](#4-module-api-boundaries) names no language and never did. **It is Section 2's because it is a property of the shell, which is Section 2's to name** — the privileged origin, the bindings and the production surfaces are the shell's own facilities. **Corrected 2026-08-22, out of the third sanity sweep's verification round:** this line used to say *the shell Section 2 chose*, written the same day [H4](#22-the-route-chosen-and-the-properties-it-is-held-to) withdrew the choice — Wails is the provisional direction and not a completed decision until H5b is filled. **What that costs here is nothing structural and one re-check**: [H5a](#22-the-route-chosen-and-the-properties-it-is-held-to) already licenses every property below to be checked against Wails' own documentation, and says that a failure of any one moves the choice down H7's list; so H31's and H35's option names are this direction's properties, and **if H5b selects Tauri they are re-checked against that shell's own publications and not carried over** — **which is what happened: H5b selected Tauri v2 on 2026-09-02 and the re-check is [H6c](#22-the-route-chosen-and-the-properties-it-is-held-to), where three of H35's five surfaces are answered by that project's own pages, one is stated as not established rather than claimed, and the two H35 already made this document's own build requirement carry over unchanged** — and [The shape](The%20shape.md#7-desktop-app-and-delivery)'s section 7 list **did not name a content-security boundary when this subsection was written, and was brought into line on 2026-08-22** ([Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 42). *(Put into the past tense 2026-08-29 at the stage-close repair, in step with [2.6](#26-the-colour-scheme-read-and-declared)'s; both went on making the claim in the present tense after their own entries closed on the shape's edit.)*

**The promise above was half kept for four days, and the other half is [H31a](#28-the-privileged-web-content-boundary). Recorded 2026-09-06, found at Build.** This paragraph promises the re-check for **two** option names, H31's and H35's. **H6c is H35's and it was written on the day the shell changed; nothing was ever written for H31's**, so the allow-list H31 rests on stayed the losing shell's own option, quoted as though it were Smoozy's, from 2026-09-02 until this date — and H31's opening sentence and [H33](#28-the-privileged-web-content-boundary)'s closing one named that shell's language with it. **H31a is H31's re-check and with it the promise is kept in full.** It closes with the selected shell's own publications and adds one thing the other shell published nothing about, which is [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 67.

**H31. The packaged application origin is the only privileged origin.** The shell exposes the application's own services only to the page shipped inside the signed application. **Corrected 2026-09-06, found at Build.** This opened *"The shell exposes Go application services only to the page shipped inside the signed application"*, which named the losing shell's language as Smoozy's; step 0 settled Tauri v2 and Rust ([H4](#22-the-route-chosen-and-the-properties-it-is-held-to), [the Build plan](../Build/Plan.md)). **The rule did not move.** A top-level or child-frame navigation away from the packaged origin is cancelled before commit; `window.open`, remote frames and executable content fetched at run time are refused. An approved external HTTPS address is handed to the operating system's browser and never opened inside the privileged web view.

**The allow-list quoted next is Wails' and is kept whole, because it is still a true reading of Wails and has simply stopped being a statement about Smoozy.** `BindingsAllowedOrigins`, type `string`: *"Comma-separated list of additional allowed origins for JS ↔ Go bindings. Supports "*" wildcards in hostnames for subdomain matching."* (`https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wailsapp/wails/master/website/docs/reference/options.mdx`, read 2026-08-22; wails.io returns 403 to a fetch, the same route H6a and H13 take) — **a list of *additional* allowed origins, so on that shell, left unset, the packaged origin stands alone.** ~~and it is left unset in production~~ **The selected shell's own answer to the same question is [H31a](#28-the-privileged-web-content-boundary)**, and it is that answer, not this one, that governs what Smoozy ships.

**H31a. The selected shell's own answer to H31, which is the re-check [2.8](#28-the-privileged-web-content-boundary)'s constraint promised for H31's option name. Added 2026-09-06, found at Build. Every source below is that project's own publication, read 2026-09-06. Label: documented.**

- **The property H31 needs is that shell's published default rather than an option left unset.** *Capabilities*, `https://v2.tauri.app/security/capabilities/`: *"By default the API is only accessible to bundled code shipped with the Tauri App."* **So the packaged origin stands alone with nothing configured**, which is the same production state H31 asked of the other shell and is reached by a default instead of by an omission.
- **The equivalent of the allow-list is a field on each grant rather than one list for the whole surface, and it is unset by default.** The configuration schema published at the exact measured release, `https://raw.githubusercontent.com/tauri-apps/tauri/tauri-v2.11.5/crates/tauri-schema-generator/schemas/config.schema.json`, on a capability's `remote`: *"Configure remote URLs that can use the capability permissions. This setting is optional and defaults to not being set, as our default use case is that the content is served from our local application."* — and on `local`: *"Whether this capability is enabled for local app URLs or not. Defaults to `true`."* The same words are on the human page, *Capability*, `https://v2.tauri.app/reference/acl/capability/`. **No capability Smoozy ships carries a `remote` field**, and that is this document's requirement in the place the other shell's *left unset in production* used to sit.
- **The shape differs and it differs in Smoozy's favour.** The other shell's single option opened or closed the whole binding surface at once; this one is per capability, and a capability names the permissions it grants, so a remote grant — were one ever written — could reach only what that one capability lists. **Nothing here is weaker than what H31 was written against.**
- **The navigation half is not a configuration option on this shell, and that is the one thing a builder must not carry across.** The schema above gives the security block exactly eight members — `csp`, `devCsp`, `freezePrototype`, `dangerousDisableAssetCspModification`, `assetProtocol`, `pattern`, `capabilities` and `headers` — **and no navigation allow-list among them.** What the project publishes instead is on the webview builder at the measured version, `https://docs.rs/tauri/2.11.5/tauri/webview/struct.WebviewWindowBuilder.html`: `on_navigation`, *"Defines a closure to be executed when the webview navigates to a URL. Returning false cancels the navigation."*, and `on_new_window`, *"Set a new window request handler to decide if incoming url is allowed to be opened. A new window is requested to be opened by the window.open API."* **So H31's two refusals are written at window construction rather than declared in configuration, and a build that sets the configuration and installs neither closure keeps neither refusal.**
- **One published limit is materially different from anything the other shell published, and it lands on Linux.** Same capabilities page: *"On Linux and Android, Tauri is unable to distinguish between requests from an embedded `<iframe>` and the window itself."* **What that costs here:** [H36](#28-the-privileged-web-content-boundary)'s *"A binding call whose caller origin is not the packaged origin is rejected before dispatch"* cannot be enforced on Linux against a frame inside the packaged page, so on that one system the whole of the defence is [H33](#28-the-privileged-web-content-boundary)'s `frame-src 'none'` and H31's refusal of remote frames — one layer where the other two systems have two. **Nothing Smoozy ships carries a frame at all, so nothing is open today**; what changed is that a directive which read as a second line of defence is the only line on Linux. [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 67.

**H32. Values are content, never markup.** Filenames, paths, computer names, server names, captured streams, logs, seller/service text and every other value not compiled into the interface are inserted with text-node or value APIs. They are never passed to `innerHTML`, `outerHTML`, `insertAdjacentHTML`, HTML-template compilation, `document.write`, `eval`, `Function`, a script URL, an event-handler attribute or a stylesheet parser. Image bytes needed by a surface are returned through a typed application service, exposed as a process-held `blob:` URL and revoked when that surface releases them; a server-provided path is never made a web-view URL.

**H33. The production document carries this policy:** `default-src 'none'; script-src 'self'; style-src 'self'; img-src 'self' blob:; connect-src 'none'; object-src 'none'; base-uri 'none'; frame-src 'none'; form-action 'none'; frame-ancestors 'none'`. Scripts and styles are shipped files: there is no inline script, inline style, remote font or remote import. The application makes network calls through the typed application services of [Section 4](#4-module-api-boundaries), not through browser `fetch`, WebSocket or an injected bridge. *(**Corrected 2026-09-06, found at Build.** This read *"the typed Go services"*, naming the losing shell's language; step 0 settled Rust ([the Build plan](../Build/Plan.md)). **The policy string above did not move and neither did the rule** — [Section 4](#4-module-api-boundaries) names no language, and [H32](#28-the-privileged-web-content-boundary) already said *typed application service* two clauses up.)*

**H33a. One directive of H33 is narrowed on the selected shell, and nothing else in the policy moves. Added 2026-09-02 at Build step 1, when the policy was first put on a running window.** H33 was written against the shell that did not win, and its `connect-src 'none'` cannot stand on this one: **the selected shell's own bridge between the page and the application is reached over a local scheme rather than by a function call**, and that project's own example policy names it — `"connect-src": "ipc: http://ipc.localhost"` (*Content Security Policy*, `https://v2.tauri.app/security/csp/`, read 2026-09-02; label: **documented**). **So the shipped directive is `connect-src ipc: http://ipc.localhost` and every other directive of H33 is exactly as written.** **This narrows the wording and not the boundary**: what H33 forbids is the page reaching the network, and both values name the shell's own local bridge on the machine the page is already running on — no host, no port, nothing routable. **The rest of H33 holds unchanged and was proved rather than assumed**: the window ran with `default-src 'none'`, `script-src 'self'` and `style-src 'self'`, with no inline script and no inline style, and the page's one binding call returned. **What a builder must not do is widen it further** — a `fetch` to anything else is still refused by `default-src 'none'`, and [H33](#28-the-privileged-web-content-boundary)'s own sentence stands: the application makes network calls through typed services, never from the page.

**H34. The binding surface is the application-service surface and nothing beneath it.** No raw filesystem, process execution, shell, database, secret-store or unrestricted network object is bound into JavaScript. Each exported method has a closed request and response type, validates lengths and enum values before touching an adapter, and returns only the values its owning module permits. The web view never receives a stored secret or a capability that can ask an adapter for arbitrary work.

**H35. Development surfaces do not ship, and three of the five are the shell's own published defaults.** All quotations are the shell's own documentation sources, read 2026-08-22, by the same route H31 names. **The inspector:** off in a production build unless a named flag turns it on — the `wails build` flag `-devtools` is *"Allows the use of the devtools in the application window in production (when -debug is not used)"* with the page's own *"Use for debugging only"*, and the options page says *"the devtools inspector won't be available unless the `-devtools` build flag is used"* (`https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wailsapp/wails/master/website/docs/reference/cli.mdx`, and H31's options page). Neither flag is passed and `Debug.OpenInspectorOnStartup` is left `false`. **The context menu:** *"By default, the browser's default context-menu is only available in development and in a `-debug` build along with the devtools inspector"* (same options page); `EnableDefaultContextMenu` is not set. **The development/reload server:** it is `wails dev`'s own facility and not a build output — *"A webserver is started on `http://localhost:34115` which serves your application (not just frontend) over http"*, and *"If they are changed, the application will automatically reload (not rebuild)"* (the same cli.mdx page) — so a release artifact carries none. **Debug bindings and source maps are established by no publication read** and stand as this document's own build requirement — H5b's build-mode row already demands *"symbols stripped; inspector, reload server and source maps absent"* — carried at [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 44. A build in which any of the five is enabled is not a release artifact and may not be signed or published.

**H36. This boundary fails closed.** A disallowed navigation is cancelled and no application service runs for it. A binding call whose caller origin is not the packaged origin is rejected before dispatch. If the shell cannot establish the packaged origin, apply the policy or disable the development surfaces, it does not compose feature modules or open the user's record. The condition is recorded through the ordinary failure plumbing; there is no permissive fallback.

---

## 3. Desktop process composition and UI event delivery

**Constraint:** [The shape](The%20shape.md#the-users-computer) — one desktop application containing every feature module and the shared desktop services they call; **no always-running local daemon**; no listener exposed to the server.

### 3.1 One process, one instance, and two short-lived helpers

**P1. One process holds the interface, every feature module, and every shared desktop service.** There is no background service, no agent registered with the operating system's own service facility, and nothing that runs when the application is closed. Anything that must continue with the application closed runs on the rented server, which is the shape's ruling and is not re-taken here.

**P2. One ordinary running instance per user, and a second ordinary launch raises the running one.** The local record is a single durable store with a single writer ([Section 5](#5-the-local-records-database-and-its-migrations)); two processes writing it is a fault rather than a case. **The only overlap is the update handover of Z21: the old process remains the sole record writer while it starts the already-verified new process in probe mode; the old process stops writing and hands the instance lock to the probe before the probe runs [5.2](#52-migrations)'s migration; from that moment the probe is the sole writer and the old process performs no record write of its own; on the success token the old process exits, and on a failed start the probe is ended before the actor that launched the probe takes the instance lock back — the old process on macOS and Linux, Z19's relaunch helper on Windows — to perform L13's restore of L8's copy, which is that rollback's first act.** The mechanism that enforces it on each system is ordinary construction and is Build's; the rule is here because the store's single-writer property depends on it.

**P3. Exactly two short-lived helper processes exist, and the list is closed.** The **askpass helper** that carries a key passphrase to its one endpoint ([Security and licence](Security%20and%20licence.md#14-inside-the-desktop-application) §1.4 hop 3), and the **relaunch helper** the Windows update needs ([20.5](#205-the-short-lived-helper-and-where-it-exists)). Neither holds state, neither survives the action that started it, and neither binds a listener. **A third helper is a fault in this section rather than a case it does not cover.**

### 3.2 The interface thread, and what may never run on it

**P4. One thread draws the interface and nothing that can block runs on it.** The rule is forced by observation rather than chosen: a `SecItemCopyMatching` with user interaction left allowed **did not return within 45 seconds and was killed**, and Apple's own pages say of all four `SecItem` entry points that each *"blocks the calling thread, so it can cause your app's UI to hang if called from the main thread"* ([Security and licence](Security%20and%20licence.md#30-the-contract-every-system-implements) §3.0 rule 3, Fact 38).

**P5. The closed list of what runs off it**: every secret-store call; every filesystem call the platform file adapter makes; every connection, request, reading and transfer; every notification post; every database read, write and migration. **What runs on it is the drawing and nothing else.**

### 3.3 UI event delivery

**P6. Results reach the interface as events on one ordered queue, and the interface holds no other route to a module.** One queue rather than one per module, because two queues are two orders and a user watching two panels would see one action's outcomes arrive in an order neither queue promises.

**P7. A UI event never carries state that has not already been written durably.** [State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#94-the-outcome-record) §9.4 requires each unit's outcome to be durable **before** it is presented, and D6 requires every durable write to happen at the moment the fact becomes true. **So the order is: write, then post the event, then draw.** A design that drew first and recorded afterwards would satisfy the sentence in the ordinary case and go quiet in exactly the case Row 292 exists for.

**P8. A UI event is discarded when its subject is gone and never queued against a subject that no longer exists.** Events are volatile — process memory for the life of the run, per [§11.1](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#111-five-classes-and-the-reason-there-is-no-sixth)'s run-held class — and **nothing durable is ever written from one**.

**P9. Nothing on this side polls.** [State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#84-nothing-is-polled-to-withdraw-a-record) §8.4 names the three moments an observed value is refreshed and forbids a watcher; this section adds no timer of its own, and [Section 12](#12-the-automatic-supervisor-unavailable-repair-trigger) — the place a watcher would most obviously have been reached for — is built without one. **[P10d](#33-ui-event-delivery)'s live-reading sequence is not this sentence's poll**: it is a bounded sequence under P10a's form, not a standing timer and not a watcher, and P10d states why in full. **Nor is any other sequence of that same admitted form**, `Download` W1–W3's walk among them *(said 2026-08-22, routed item 5, because P10d alone read as though it were the only one)*.

**P10. There is exactly one timer on this side and it is the update check** ([20.1](#201-what-the-application-does-and-what-the-user-does) Z23). **It is not a poll in §8.4's sense**: it refreshes no condition's observed value, withdraws no record and reaches no machine — which is the same test V3 applies to its own trigger. **A second timer anywhere in this application is a fault in this section rather than a case it does not cover.**

**P10a. What *a timer* means here, narrowed 2026-08-21 per Row 347, because the sentence above was already false of a closed document.** P10 was written about **a standing timer: one that fires with nothing under way, on a schedule of its own, and starts work.** That is the thing that turns into a poll, and the update check is the only one Smoozy has. **Two things that are plainly not that were being caught by the wording**, and one of them predates this clause:

- **A delay inside a sequence that is already running.** `Servers` N2 pauses 15 seconds between attempts and N6 pauses 60 between runs; `Servers` §4.2's reconnect policy for a marked period runs four such runs and gives up at ten minutes — **a length rather than a floor**, since N2a fixed a run's span at 105 seconds *(corrected 2026-08-22, scenario replays routed item 21; this read "a ten-minute floor", which was M4's own word until N2a bounded the run)*. **Every one of those is a scheduled wake-up that then reaches a machine**, and by P10's plain reading each is a second timer — while `Servers` is closed, audited and correct. What separates them from the update check is that **a declared break started them, they end when the period ends, and nothing outside the run they belong to can outlive them.** They are not standing timers and never were. **Those three are examples and the list is not closed** — other clauses in this and other documents declare sequences of the same form, and **a clause is judged against P10b's rule rather than against this list**, which nothing here undertakes to keep in step.
- **A drawing that re-renders a value the application already holds.** A per-second countdown against `Servers` N10a's *moment the next attempt is due* refreshes no observed value, withdraws no record, reaches no machine, posts nothing on P6's queue and writes nothing durable. **It is the same test P10 already applies to itself**, and it passes it more cleanly than the update check does.

**P10b. So the rule is stated in the form a builder can apply, and it is narrower rather than looser.** **No standing timer exists but the update check.** A delay inside a running sequence is that sequence's and is bounded by it. **A drawing may tick against a moment already published, and may do nothing else** — it takes no reading, makes no request, and stops existing when the thing it draws is not on screen. **Anything that fires with nothing under way, or that does work rather than redraw, is a second timer and is the fault P10 names.** P9's *nothing on this side polls* is untouched by all of it. [P10d](#33-ui-event-delivery) declares the one sequence of the admitted kind this section itself starts. **And [P10e](#33-ui-event-delivery) admits a third shape, added 2026-09-02, because the sentence before last catches a closed document's one route to the spec's half-hour idle statement and a builder holding it would not build that route at all.**

**P10c. And one hazard is named rather than defended.** A moment on this computer's clock says nothing about a computer that was suspended between the moment being written and the drawing reading it, so a countdown can wake reading long past due. **It is not repaired here** — the repair is driven by attempts ending rather than by the clock, so a late attempt is still an attempt — and `Servers` [Section 16](Servers.md#16-unresolved) item 21 carries it from the other side.

**P10d. One bounded sequence of this section's own reads a connected server again between one connection and the next, and it is declared here because every clause that could have carried it forbids it. Added 2026-08-22, scenario replays Pile 1 item R1; scoped to this section's own sequence on 2026-08-22, routed item 5.** `Preparation/Specs/Queue & History.md` promises a rendering entry that is live while the render runs — two moving bars, a commentary read as it arrives — and [T18](#135-telling-now-from-catching-up-without-a-clock) classifies as *now*, and posts notifications for, only an event read while the connected period is live, after [§7](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#7-reconciliation-on-the-desktop) step 3 has completed. So something must read a connected machine repeatedly inside a standing period — and P9 and P10 here, `Queue & History` [A1](Queue%20&%20History.md#13-reading-a-machine-and-adoption) and [P3](Queue%20&%20History.md#15-the-previews), `Servers` [W4](Servers.md#11-the-machines-work-rows-42-43-40-44-and-317) and `Rented-server runtime` [U26](Rented-server%20runtime.md#87-what-reaches-a-person-who-is-not-connected), each read alone, forbid every route such a reading could take. The route is this clause, in P10a's admitted form: **a declared event starts it, it ends when the period ends, and nothing outside the period can outlive it.** It is a running sequence with delays inside it, never a standing timer.

**It is the one sequence of the admitted kind this section itself starts** (P10b) **and not the only one in the architecture.** `Download` [W1–W3](Download.md#3-the-watch-row-210) declares another in a closed document — the auto-download arrangement's repeated walk of a job output folder, paced from the end of the previous walk, running only while the arrangement's desired state is on, a connection to that server stands, and the job is inside its own attempt window; its record survives a run and its walks do not. **That walk is admitted by P10a's form and not by this clause**, and a builder reading P10d alone must not take it for the fault P10 names. **Nothing here bounds it or is bounded by it**: the two sequences are separate, and W2's own aggregate bound on a server's walks is that document's. **A third is `Queue & History` W4's idle re-check, admitted by [P10e](#33-ui-event-delivery) rather than by this clause and likewise not in the closed set above — added 2026-09-02.**

- **What starts it.** `Servers` [Q1](Servers.md#10-the-first-connection-in-order-rows-36-and-35) step 7 completing for that server — desktop reconciliation through §7 step 5 done and the first queue reading taken. One sequence per server, per primary connection. The adoption offer neither gates it nor is recomposed by it.
- **What a pass reads, and the set is closed.** **The read-forward from the event cursor** under [§4](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#4-the-event-journal-and-acknowledgement) — every pass, for the whole connected stretch, whatever is on screen, because T18's *now* bucket and the fetches `Queue & History` P3 triggers serve a user who is looking elsewhere (T22); each event is applied idempotently and the cursor advances only after the application is durable, which is §4's own rule and not a new one. **The queue reading** — `Queue & History` A1's one request, carrying R5's live figures — repeated only while a surface showing that server's queue or its running entry is on screen. **The running entry's commentary read** — `Queue & History` [E8](Queue%20&%20History.md#8-the-ending-and-the-words)'s read of the sink's current bytes — repeated only while that commentary is on screen. **Nothing else joins it**: not the hardware reading (`Servers` [H15](Servers.md#83-where-the-reading-is-taken-and-why-no-blender-is-in-it)), not a health reading (`Rented-server runtime` [V2](Rented-server%20runtime.md#12-health-readings)), not a free-space re-read (M15), and no condition record is its subject.
- **What stops when the entry is off screen.** The queue reading's and the commentary read's repetition. The read-forward continues; a pass with nothing on screen is the read-forward alone.
- **How it paces itself.** One pass at a time, the pause between passes measured from the end of the previous pass and never from its start, so a slow machine is never stacked with concurrent reads. **The interval is Build's to pick and this document names no number**, which is [Z23](#201-what-the-application-does-and-what-the-user-does)'s own discipline. Its requests are ordinary requests on the primary connection under `Server protocol and transfers` [T5](Server%20protocol%20and%20transfers.md#62-concurrency-and-one-admission-point-rather-than-one-per-server)'s admission and alternation — a waiting command takes the session first, and no pass preempts one.
- **What ends it.** The break declaration (`Servers` [K3](Servers.md#21-the-break-reporting-mechanism), K5) — no pass is composed while the primary connection is not live — and the period ending, which ends everything period-held. A reconnection's own Q1 step 7 starts a fresh sequence, whose read-forward resumes from the durable cursor; the events it applies there are T18's catch-up, exactly as T20 already rules. **Nothing outside the period can outlive it**: the sequence is run-held and writes nothing durable of its own — what its reads write, the cursor's advance among them, is those mechanisms' own and unchanged.
- **Why it is not the fault P10 names, and not §8.4's poll.** P10a's form, applied once more: a declared event started it, it is bounded by the connection and the period it belongs to, and it fires nothing with nothing under way. §8.4's rule is that nothing is polled *to withdraw a record*, and no condition gets a watcher — this sequence exists to keep the queue's live promise, keys on no condition and withdraws nothing. Where a pass's outcome moves the supervisor-unavailable condition's observed value ([Section 12](#12-the-automatic-supervisor-unavailable-repair-trigger) V1), or finds the standing condition and so sends the repair under V3, it does so exactly as the same request taken singly would — §8.4's second moment arriving by an ordinary request, counted by V3 as it counts every command-path request and bounded by V4's one outstanding request per server. The sequence does not stop for that condition: its failing passes are the fresh chances V5 names, and the pass the supervisor answers again is what withdraws the record.
- **What cites it.** `Queue & History` A1, P3 and E8; `Servers` Q18 and W4; `Rented-server runtime` U26. T18's *now* bucket is delivered by it and says so.

**P10e. A third shape is admitted, and it has exactly one instance: the scheduled re-check that delivers the half-hour idle statement. Added 2026-09-02, scenario replay *The user stops a running job* finding 1.** [P10a](#33-ui-event-delivery) names two things that are not standing timers — a delay inside a sequence already running, and a drawing that ticks against a published moment — and **neither reaches `Queue & History` [W4](Queue%20&%20History.md#12-the-idle-half-hour)**: *"While connected and short of the half hour, the desktop schedules a re-check and re-verifies against the machine before speaking."* It fires with nothing under way, because an idle machine is its whole subject, and it does work rather than redraw, because it re-takes the claim from the machine — so under [P10b](#33-ui-event-delivery)'s last sentence read alone it is the fault P10 names, in a closed and correct document. **A builder holding that sentence does not build the re-check**, and then the only thing in the application standing between the user and a machine billing for nothing never fires for as long as Smoozy stays connected. `Preparation/Specs/Whole app.md` fixes that statement as the fourth notification kind, so the sentence was catching a promise.

- **The shape, stated so it can be applied.** A wake-up **scheduled from a durable fact the application already holds**, which **re-verifies against the machine before it says anything**, and which **dies with the connected period it belongs to**. All three parts are required together.
- **Why each part is there.** The first is what keeps it out of [P9](#33-ui-event-delivery)'s poll: nothing is watched and the schedule is arithmetic over a fact already in hand. The second is what keeps the desktop's clock out of every promise — it schedules the **look** and never the **claim**, which is W4's own reason (*"drift costs a re-check, never a wrong statement"*). The third bounds it exactly as P10a's first shape is bounded, and it is why nothing here outlives a break.
- **Its one instance is `Queue & History` W4**, named here the way P10a already names `Servers` [N2](Servers.md#31-a-run-and-the-two-figures-in-it) and [N6](Servers.md#32-the-standing-repair-which-never-ends-while-the-app-is-open). **The instance list is closed at one in this document**; a further one is judged against the three parts and against P10b, which is P10a's own discipline — the rule decides, never the list.
- **It is not [§8.4](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#84-nothing-is-polled-to-withdraw-a-record)'s poll.** It refreshes no condition's observed value and withdraws no record. The idle statement is not a condition record, and `Queue & History` W5's idle-spoken mark is Smoozy's own note of Smoozy's own act rather than a copy of machine state.
- **It is not a live-reading sequence and does not join [P10d](#33-ui-event-delivery)'s closed set**, which stays the read-forward, the queue reading and the commentary read. This is a separate sequence of an admitted form, as `Download` [W1–W3](Download.md#3-the-watch-row-210)'s walk is, and **nothing here bounds it or is bounded by it.** Its requests are ordinary requests on the primary connection under `Server protocol and transfers` [T5](Server%20protocol%20and%20transfers.md#62-concurrency-and-one-admission-point-rather-than-one-per-server)'s admission, like any other.
- **What it costs, said rather than hidden.** One scheduled look per connected server per idle stretch short of the half hour, and **[P10c](#33-ui-event-delivery)'s hazard rides it** — a suspended computer can wake it reading long past due. That is harmless here, and it is why the second part of the shape is not optional: the claim is re-taken from the machine at that moment, so a late wake states the truth late instead of stating a wrong thing on time.

**P11. A dragged pane width is durable state, written at the moment the drag ends, and the user's own drag is its only writer. Added 2026-08-25 at the tenth sitting, per Row 476.** The design's sentence is the constraint — *"The widths are remembered between runs, on the user's own computer, like every other thing they have set"* — and the building is decided here in three parts.

- **The keys are the design's own sharing rules and there is one stored value per key, never one per drawing**: one width per declared seam, **one rail width for every screen**, and **one list width shared by the queue and the history**. Each is a settings value in the local record; **the inventory row is `State, identity and recovery` §1.1's, being written in this same sitting, and is not added here** (L1).
- **The write happens at the end of the drag and at no other moment** — the release is the fact becoming true, which is D6's rule, and nothing is written on exit. **The responsive narrowing steps write nothing**: each step's re-declared width is a drawing-time computation over the stored value and the window's width, so *set aside — not destroyed* is true by construction — the stored width is never overwritten by a step, and widening the window finds it exactly where the user left it. A width the narrowing wrote back would be the failure the design's own sentence warns against, and it cannot occur because the steps have no write.
- **A record holding no width for a key is the drawn default and not a failure** — a fresh installation has dragged nothing — and L8a's rule is untouched: an absent setting is an ordinary state, a read that did not answer is not. **[Method](Method.md)'s third escalation, checked and declined**: nothing new is put in front of the user — the remembering is the design's promise kept, and a lost record costs widths the user can drag again.

---

## 4. Module API boundaries

**Constraint:** the scope line's *"module API boundaries"*, and [Security and licence](Security%20and%20licence.md#14-inside-the-desktop-application) §1.4 — *"`Desktop app and delivery` owns the adapter, the helper and the module API boundaries; this document owns the rule and does not choose their implementation."*

**A1. A feature module reaches the operating system only through a named service of this document, and never directly.** The complete list of services is [Section 21](#21-what-this-document-supplies-and-to-which-document). A module that opens a file, posts a notification, reads a name, draws a menu or opens a picker for itself has broken this boundary, and the failure is invisible from inside that module.

**A2. No module has a durable store of its own.** There is one local record ([§1.1](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#11-on-the-users-computer)) and this document builds it. **That is the structural half of Row 290**: a module cannot remember its own suppressed warning in its own way, because there is nowhere else to put one ([15.1](#151-there-is-nowhere-else-to-put-one)).

**A3. No feature module receives a stored secret as a value it may keep.** A module receives either a **use of the secret carried out on its behalf** or a **handle it can present again** ([Security and licence](Security%20and%20licence.md#14-inside-the-desktop-application) §1.4). [Section 9](#9-the-secret-adapter-and-its-helper) is the shape of that boundary.

**A4. Every local write goes through the platform file adapter** ([Section 6](#6-the-platform-file-adapter)), which the shape requires of *"every local writer"*. **A module that writes by any other route is outside Row 303 entirely**, and the loss it can cause is the one observed on 2026-08-17.

**A5. Every transfer channel is admitted through the one desktop-side admission point and draws from the one budget for its direction** ([Section 11](#11-the-two-rate-budgets-and-the-one-session-admission-point)).

**A6. Every string of prose a module hands to the interface passes the typography application point** ([Section 17](#17-text-rendering-and-the-typography-application-point)), and that is enforced by the interface accepting prose from nowhere else. **Every other string reaches the interface by the verbatim route** ([17.3](#173-what-it-must-never-touch) X9), which performs no substitution of any kind. **The two are the complete set of ways a string reaches a surface**, and the enforcement is structural on both sides of it.

**A7. A module declares, in its own document, five things this document's services need and does not decide at run time**: its actions' partition unit and which of [§9.2](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#92-three-tests-applied-in-order-and-the-answer-is-declared-rather-than-inferred)'s three tests decided it; each transfer's priority class ([Server protocol and transfers](Server%20protocol%20and%20transfers.md#63-priority-and-what-is-owned-here-is-the-mechanism) T8); each dismissible warning's identity ([15.2](#152-the-warning-identity)); each condition it raises, against [§8.3](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#83-the-condition-identity-and-who-supplies-each-observed-value)'s shape; **and each act it writes to the diary — the act's fixed line form and the identities it carries** ([Reporting a problem](Reporting%20a%20problem.md#3-what-goes-into-the-diary) J2, J3), where an act is diary-worthy exactly where the owning spec names it as an outcome, and **a module with no declaration writes nothing, which is a hosting fault and not a default** — a diary each module filled to its own taste would produce a record that reads as complete and is not, which is [15.1](#151-there-is-nowhere-else-to-put-one) G2's invisible failure arriving by a new door. *(Four became five on 2026-08-25, routed from `Reporting a problem` §12 item 5.)* **A declaration missing is a module that cannot be hosted, not a default this document supplies.**

**A8. Modules do not call one another's adapters and do not pass one another operating-system handles.** What crosses between two modules is what the specs' own connections say crosses, and it crosses as data.

---

## 5. The local record's database and its migrations

**Constraint:** [Security and licence](Security%20and%20licence.md#9-the-local-record) §9 — the record holds no secret value, is **not encrypted**, and is *"written in the per-user application-data location the platform adapter names, created for the owning user only, and protected by the operating system's own file permissions and nothing further"*; and [State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#11-on-the-users-computer) §1.1, whose inventory is the complete list of what it holds.

### 5.1 One store, and its contents are another document's list

**L1. There is one durable local record and this document builds it to the inventory of [§1.1](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#11-on-the-users-computer).** **No row is added to that inventory here.** That inventory's one home is that section, and writing it a second time would give a fact two homes. Where a mechanism needs a record the inventory does not name, it is recorded as a seam ([Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 14) rather than written in.

**L2. The other four durable things on this side are not state and are not in this store** *(the word read `three` over a list of four from the day it was written; corrected 2026-08-25, clerically, by counting the sentence's own list — no thing was added or removed here, and [L2a](#51-one-store-and-its-contents-are-another-documents-list)'s fifth is counted against the corrected four)*: the operating system's secret store (`Security and licence` §3), the staged-transfer area (`Server protocol and transfers`), the fetched-derivative area and the throwaway known-hosts area. **The user's downloaded outputs are not Smoozy's state at all** — no lifetime, no cleanup, no cap, never counted against anything ([§11.3](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#113-the-two-rulings-those-documents-were-promised)).

**L2a. A fifth joined that list on 2026-08-25: the diary area, `Reporting a problem` §1's.** The ruling that it is durable, bounded and **not state** — nothing branches on anything it holds, nothing is presented from it, and its total loss costs the contents of a future report and nothing else — is [Reporting a problem](Reporting%20a%20problem.md#1-the-diary) R2's and is cited rather than remade. It sits in the per-user application-data location under L3's own rule, beside the local record and never inside it, protected exactly as the record is and not encrypted (`Security and licence` §9's argument, applied there at R6). **Its §1.1 durable-table row and its §11.1 naming are `State, identity and recovery`'s** — routed by `Reporting a problem` §12 item 4, and not added here, which is L1's rule holding. [5.3](#53-the-diary-service) is the one writer it has.

**L3. Where it sits is the platform adapter's answer and never a composed path.** The per-user application-data location comes from each system's own call for it and is **never assembled from the login account name** — [Section 10](#10-the-application-identity-adapter) records why reading that name is a trap, and a path built out of it inherits the trap.

**L3a. Two readings this document was asked for and supplied nowhere: the user's home directory, and Smoozy's own temporary area. Added 2026-08-29 at the stage-close repair, routed by the runner from [Keys](Keys.md#13-unresolved) §13 item 20** — the one of that document's seven routed items whose receiving clause was never written, its six siblings having closed by this same route. [`Keys`](Keys.md#3-the-key-locations-and-the-one-place-premise-row-242) L1a asks this document for a **user home directory** reading, *"exactly as `Desktop app and delivery` L3 already answers for the per-user application-data location"*, and [`Keys`](Keys.md#2-the-supported-types-the-offered-set-and-the-default-row-241) T2 writes three real private key files per application run into **Smoozy's own temporary area**, which no document named. **[A1](#4-module-api-boundaries) closes every route to a service [Section 21](#21-what-this-document-supplies-and-to-which-document) does not list**, so until this clause neither reading had a permitted route at all, while [A4](#4-module-api-boundaries) required T2's write to go through the adapter to a destination nothing placed. Both are supplied here, in L3's shape and [L2a](#51-one-store-and-its-contents-are-another-documents-list)'s. **What `Keys` does with either stays `Keys`'** and is not remade here, and `Keys` is not edited from here.

- **The user home directory reading, per system, is that system's own call for it, taken through the platform adapter and never composed** — L3's rule for the per-user application-data location, applied to a second location, and it is a named service of [Section 21](#21-what-this-document-supplies-and-to-which-document) in its own right exactly as [Section 8](#8-where-a-download-is-proposed-to-land)'s folder is. **The two ways closed by name are `Keys` L1a's and they hold here rather than only there**: it is never assembled from the login account name ([I4](#10-the-application-identity-adapter), and L3's own reason for closing it), and **it is never assembled from an environment value**.
- **It is not [Section 8](#8-where-a-download-is-proposed-to-land)'s Linux route, and the difference is stated rather than left for whoever reaches for [W3](#8-where-a-download-is-proposed-to-land) as the model.** W3 reads `$HOME` because `$HOME` is the downloads tool's own documented fallback for a folder the user may relocate; what is wanted here is the directory `ssh` and `ssh-keygen` themselves resolve, which `Keys` L1a records as observed **not** to follow `$HOME` on macOS — run with `$HOME` set elsewhere, the offered default was the real account home (Fact 33, observed, cited there and not re-taken here). **W3 stands exactly as it is and neither route is read across to the other.** Which call each system publishes for this reading, and whether it reproduces what those tools resolve, is unread on Windows and Linux — [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 59.
- **Smoozy's own temporary area is the sixth durable non-record thing on this side**, joining [L2](#51-one-store-and-its-contents-are-another-documents-list)'s four and L2a's fifth in L2a's own shape and by the same route. **It sits in the per-user application-data location under L3's own rule, beside the local record and never inside it, protected exactly as the record is and not encrypted** (`Security and licence` [§9](Security%20and%20licence.md#9-the-local-record)'s argument, the same one L2a applies to the diary). **It is never the operating system's shared temporary directory** — the place a builder reaches for on macOS and Linux, and the one [State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#13-rules-that-bind-every-store) §1.3 rule 2 already refuses on the other side of the wire. **It is never `Keys` L1's usual key location and never a directory any module scans for keys**: that is stated here as this document's rule about the area, because `Security and licence` [§6](Security%20and%20licence.md#6-the-private-half-of-an-ssh-key) and [§7](Security%20and%20licence.md#7-host-trust)'s absolute sentences are written about Smoozy's own **host-key** material and reach a generated key pair nowhere. It is resolved through the adapter and by nothing else, and every write, deletion and clearing in it is [F10a](#62-what-the-adapter-is-for)'s first bullet like any other.
- **It is swept at application start, and the sweep is the area's rather than the writing module's.** **Anything found in it at application start is removed before any module uses it** — the shape `Security and licence` §7 already gives the throwaway known-hosts area, for the same reason it gives it: *discarded at once* does not cover a process that exits first, so `Keys` T2's *"delete it at once"* is completed at the other end rather than softened. Without it an interrupted run leaves three real private key files in the area until some later run, which is key material with no owner and no end rather than a broken promise, and it is why the sweep is written beside the placement instead of in the module that writes.
- **What is routed and not written here.** The area's row in `State, identity and recovery` [§1.1](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#11-on-the-users-computer)'s durable table is that document's under [L1](#51-one-store-and-its-contents-are-another-documents-list)'s *"No row is added to that inventory here"*; [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 58 carries it with what breaks, exactly as item 54 carried the three rows of 2026-08-28. **Nothing in `Security and licence` is written here either**, and the width of its §6 and §7 against a generated key pair is that document's own question.

**L3b. The call each system publishes for the two readings, named 2026-09-03 at Build step 2 rather than left for a builder to pick.** [L3](#51-one-store-and-its-contents-are-another-documents-list) and [L3a](#51-one-store-and-its-contents-are-another-documents-list) fix that each reading is the system's own call and that neither is assembled from the login account name or from an environment value, **and neither named the call, on any system, for either location** — which leaves a builder holding a rule it cannot keep without knowing what to call. [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 59 asked for exactly this and said it costs nothing; **the half of that item no page settles — which of `$HOME` and `%USERPROFILE%` wins on Windows where they disagree — is unchanged here and stays permanently unresolved.** Every call below is read from its own system's publication, quoted, with the address and the date read, in the shape [W1](#8-where-a-download-is-proposed-to-land) already uses for the downloads folder; the quotations and addresses are in this document's sources file.

**The per-user application-data location, per system.**

| | macOS | Windows | Linux |
|---|---|---|---|
| The route | `FileManager.url(for: .applicationSupportDirectory, in: .userDomainMask, appropriateFor: nil, create:)` | `SHGetKnownFolderPath(FOLDERID_LocalAppData, 0, NULL, &p)`, then `CoTaskMemFree` | `XDG_DATA_HOME`, and where that is unset or empty the specification's own default of `$HOME/.local/share` |
| Governed by | Apple's Foundation reference | Microsoft's Known Folders | the XDG Base Directory Specification, version 0.8 |
| Can return nothing | yes — the call reports an error rather than a location | `E_FAIL` or `E_INVALIDARG` | yes, where the variable is unset and `HOME` is unset or is not an absolute path |

- **macOS is [W1](#8-where-a-download-is-proposed-to-land)'s own mechanism with one constant changed**, which is why no second route is introduced for it. Apple's own words for the constant are *"Application support files (`Library/Application Support`)"*, and for the domain *"The user's home directory—the place to install user's personal items (`~`)"*.
- **Windows is the local location and not the roaming one, on Microsoft's own words rather than on a preference**: `FOLDERID_LocalAppData` is *"The file system directory that serves as a data repository for local (nonroaming) applications"*, and `FOLDERID_RoamingAppData` carries no such statement. **A roamed copy of this record would be one installation's state arriving on another computer**. **[W2](#8-where-a-download-is-proposed-to-land)'s refusal holds here too and for the same reason**: the flags argument is `0` and `KF_FLAG_DEFAULT_PATH` is never passed, so a relocated folder is followed rather than defeated.
- **Linux is read and never assembled, which is [W3](#8-where-a-download-is-proposed-to-land)'s discipline applied to a location that does have a specification.** The specification's absolute-path rule is kept as it is written — *"All paths set in these environment variables must be absolute. If an implementation encounters a relative path in any of these variables it should consider the path invalid and ignore it."* — so a relative value is ignored and the default is taken instead.
- **Where a route returns nothing the adapter says so and composes nothing**, which is [W5](#8-where-a-download-is-proposed-to-land)'s rule and not a second one. It does not fall back to a path built out of a name, and [F11](#62-what-the-adapter-is-for) already closes every other route to a location.

**The user home directory, per system.**

| | macOS | Windows | Linux |
|---|---|---|---|
| The route | the password-database entry for the **effective** user id, field `pw_dir` | `SHGetKnownFolderPath(FOLDERID_Profile, 0, NULL, &p)`, then `CoTaskMemFree` | the password-database entry for the **effective** user id, field `pw_dir` |
| Governed by | macOS's own manual page for `getpwuid`, filed under `getpwent(3)` | Microsoft's Known Folders | the Linux man-pages project's `getpwnam(3)` |
| Standing | the route [I3](#10-the-application-identity-adapter) already takes on this system for the login account name, one field over | documentation alone, and there is no Windows computer | documentation alone, and there is no Linux desktop computer |

- **It is the password-database entry on both systems that have one, and that is the same route [I3](#10-the-application-identity-adapter) takes for the login account name.** Its field is documented as the *"home directory"* on each, and macOS's own page names where the entry comes from — *"These functions obtain information from `opendirectoryd`(8), including records in `/etc/master.passwd`"*. **One reading, two fields, and no second mechanism for the second field.**
- **Not `NSHomeDirectory()` on macOS, and the reason is on Apple's own page.** It answers two different questions behind one call — *"In macOS, it's the application's sandbox directory, or the current user's home directory if the application isn't in a sandbox"* — and says nothing about whether it consults `$HOME`. What [L3a](#51-one-store-and-its-contents-are-another-documents-list) asks for is the directory `ssh` and `ssh-keygen` themselves resolve, which Fact 33 observed on this system to be the real account home; the password-database entry is the one route whose publication names its own source. **And never `NSHomeDirectoryForUser`**, which takes an account name and is [L3a](#51-one-store-and-its-contents-are-another-documents-list)'s first closed way in a different spelling.
- **The `$HOME` in the Linux data location above is not a home-directory reading and is not read across to one.** It is the specification's own stated default, exactly as [W3](#8-where-a-download-is-proposed-to-land) reads `$HOME` because the downloads tool documents it as that tool's own fallback. **The home-directory service on Linux is the password-database entry**, like macOS's, and [L3a](#51-one-store-and-its-contents-are-another-documents-list)'s ban on an environment value binds it with no exception.
- **Windows names one call and nothing else moves.** Microsoft's own entry for `FOLDERID_Profile` gives its default path as `%USERPROFILE%`, and its older equivalent's description carries the instruction this document already obeys — *"Applications should not create files or folders at this level"* — which is why the reading is a reading and Smoozy writes nothing there. **Whether that call agrees with what Windows' own `ssh` resolves is `Keys` L1a's question and no page settles it**; it wants a Windows computer and is permanently unresolved ([Section 22](#22-unresolved) items 2 and 59).

**L4. No field of the record is ever placed in a filename**, which is `Security and licence` §9's second binding rule and which [Section 7](#7-the-receiving-filesystem-collision-judgement) makes concrete from the other side: a name is a thing with rules, and a field put into one is a field subject to them.

### 5.2 Migrations

**L5. The record carries its own schema version, in the record.** Nothing infers a version from which fields are present, because that is reading a store's shape as state.

**L6. A migration runs at application start, before any module reads the record, and one at a time in order.** Forward only. No module sees a partly migrated record, because none has read it yet.

**L7. A migration's partition unit is the whole migration** — [§9.2](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#92-three-tests-applied-in-order-and-the-answer-is-declared-rather-than-inferred) test 1: nothing promises an outcome about half a migration, so the action is **atomic entire**. Test 2 then applies with force, because a half-migrated record is precisely a half-finished unit that could be mistaken for a finished one: **the migration is staged and published in one step, by the platform file adapter's publication operation** ([6.5](#65-the-publication-operation-and-what-would-demonstrate-it)), never by ordering and never by care.

**L8. A copy of the record is taken before a migration runs and kept until the new version has started successfully.** This is not caution, it is forced by [Section 20](#20-update-verification-and-replacement): macOS's supported replacement API carries a **named backup for rollback**, the Windows update is **side-by-side**, and the Linux update retains the immediately previous verified package for Z21's one permitted reinstall (Z11) — so on all three systems a previous binary can be run again after a migration has happened. Without the copy, a rollback would meet a record it cannot read.

**L8a. What a screen does where an ordinary read of the local record fails at run time. Added 2026-08-22 at the Architecture seam close, routed by the runner from [Keys](Keys.md#13-unresolved) §13 item 15.** L9 covers a record whose schema is newer than the binary's, which is a refusal to open at all. **This is the other case: the store opened, and one read of it did not answer.** **The rule is that a screen never draws a field from a read that did not answer, and never draws an absence in its place.** Where the read a screen's contents are made of fails, **that screen shows the failure rather than an emptied version of itself**, by `Preparation/Specs/Whole app.md`'s *How a failure reaches the user* — so there is no state in which a list is drawn short, a count reads zero, or a *used by nothing* line appears because a read failed. **Where a read that fills part of an already-drawn screen fails**, that part shows as unread rather than as answered; nothing substitutes a default, a blank or a last-known value. **This is one rule with one home**, and the screens that lean on it — `Keys` W11a is the worked case — cite it rather than restating it.

**L9. A binary that meets a record newer than its own schema version does not open it and says so.** It does not read the fields it recognises and ignore the rest. **This is the direct consequence of L8's rollback routes**, and a partial read would be the app being wrong on screen about its own state, which [Whole app](../Specs/Whole%20app.md) says is never past the line.

**L10. A migration writes no secret value, into no table, no backup and no crash record** (`Security and licence` §9). That is what makes the record and its copies **safe to write, copy and back up**, and it is the reason L8 costs nothing to the six held things.

**L11. A migration never removes a suppression row and never rewrites a warning identity.** Row 290's failure is invisible until a user presses the control and one warning does not come back, and a migration is the one place that failure could be introduced silently ([15.2](#152-the-warning-identity)).

**L12. A migration never removes a history tombstone.** [§10.1](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#101-the-history-tombstone) keeps a tombstone *"for the life of the local record"* and it is cleared by nothing; a migration is part of that life.

**L12a. A migration never empties the local job record.** Added 2026-09-18, on the build runner's ruling under the Creator's standing delegation, when step 10b piece 6 put the job record down at record version 23 and found this list still naming only the two tables that existed when it was written. **[L12](#52-migrations)'s own reasoning reaches it unchanged**: a job record is kept for the life of the local record, cleared by the user's own delete, by a resolution and by a server's removal ([`Queue & History`](Queue%20&%20History.md) X1, and the removal's own reading settled the same day), and by nothing else — **a migration is not one of those acts and must not become one by accident.** **What it guards against is silent, and worse than [L11](#52-migrations)'s**: nothing is pressed, nothing fails, and a person opens History to find their work gone. **It is not a new promise** — nothing about what Smoozy does changes; this says a thing the user already has cannot be taken away by machinery they never invoked. Its cost is that a migration rebuilding that table moves rows rather than emptying and refilling, **which is what L11 and L12 already require of the other two.**

**L13. The copy is spent by being restored, and the act is named here rather than assumed.** **Restoring L8's copy over the record is the first act of any rollback to a previous binary, and it happens before that binary reads the record.** A rollback that put a previous binary in front of a migrated record would meet a newer schema and L9 would refuse to open it — **so L9's refusal is what the user meets only where the copy is gone**, and it is not the ordinary outcome of a rollback. **The copy is discarded only after the new version's own start has been recorded as successful**, which is what L8 means by *started successfully* and which [20.1](#201-what-the-application-does-and-what-the-user-does) Z21 defines, along with what detects a failed start and what performs the rollback on each of the three.

### 5.3 The diary service

**Constraint:** [Reporting a problem](Reporting%20a%20problem.md#1-the-diary) R4 — *"The diary has one writer — the diary service"* — routed here by that document's §12 item 5, because A1's list of services is closed and a diary write outside it is a broken boundary that fails invisibly. Added 2026-08-25 at the tenth sitting. **The diary's own rules — its shape, cap, durability, eviction and what may enter it — are `Reporting a problem` §1, §3 and §4's, implemented here and re-decided nowhere.**

**L14. The diary service is a named service of [Section 21](#21-what-this-document-supplies-and-to-which-document) and the diary's sole writer.** A module never opens the diary, never writes a line itself and never holds a diary file handle; it hands the service an entry — one of its own declared acts under A7's fifth declaration, or the material the plumbing hands over under [16.8](#168-what-the-plumbing-hands-the-diary-and-the-report-entry-point) — and the service writes it.
