A1 and A4 govern with no carve-out: every byte the service writes goes through the platform file adapter, and the service is the only caller that writes into the diary area. **Without this row, A1 made every module's diary write a broken boundary** — a module writing nowhere satisfies its own spec and leaves a hole the Creator cannot tell from a thing that did not happen, which is G2's silent-failure shape named at the moment it is priced.

**L15. What the service implements, cited clause by clause rather than restated**: one line-oriented plain-text record in a diary area of its own (R1); a fixed 20 MB cap no free-space reading moves (R3); each entry written and forced down at the moment its fact becomes true, eviction by deleting the **oldest whole segment** of an append-only segment set and never by rewriting a retained byte (R5); the closed set of line kinds and what each carries, the run identity and the session line on every run (`Reporting a problem` §2 U7, §3 J1); and the write boundary of §4 N1 — **nothing enters the diary that may not leave the user's computer in a report**, enforced at the write and filtered nowhere. **The dying-process handlers that write the last line are the service's own** (J8): a raw write forced to the device — `F_FULLFSYNC` on macOS, `FlushFileBuffers` on Windows, `fsync` on Linux — and never a buffered writer, because a buffered line in a dying process vanished to zero bytes on every run while the exit status proved the handler ran (observed on macOS and reproduced by the check; the Windows and Linux calls documentation only — the input document §1, via `Reporting a problem` J8).

**L16. What the service does when the operating system refuses a write, and what the calling module learns.** The service answers every hand-over with its outcome, so a builder cannot half-use it — there is no fire-and-forget entry point whose failure is indistinguishable from success. **On a refusal the entry is lost and that is the whole of the consequence**: nothing retries, no failure record is raised and no surface says anything, because the user never meets the diary (R7) and a failure record about the diary would put it in front of them; and **the act the entry recorded proceeds untouched** — no operation anywhere is contingent on its diary line landing, the line being beside the ordinary conduct and never instead of it (J7's discipline, generalised). The cost of a lost entry is the contents of a future report and nothing else, which is R2's own pricing of the store. **The caller learns the outcome and branches on nothing**: there is nothing for it to do differently, and a module that invented a fallback record of its own would be A2's fault.

---

## 6. The platform file adapter

**Constraint:** the scope line's *"platform adapters for Mac, Windows and Linux"* and *"receiving-filesystem collision judgement … through the platform file adapter used by every local writer"*; [Server protocol and transfers](Server%20protocol%20and%20transfers.md#75-publication-is-the-last-step-and-it-is-one-step) S18's four requirements; and Fact 23, answered for Mac by observation on 2026-08-18 and the most dangerous finding in that batch.

### 6.1 The one rule the whole adapter is built on

**F1. No return value is an outcome. The destination is re-checked by path after closing, and that re-check is what is reported.** Observed on the Mac: a **directory deleted underneath an open descriptor reports success at every call** — write, `fsync` and `close` alike — while the bytes go to an unlinked inode that vanishes at close. **Without this rule Smoozy reports a frame written that does not exist**, which is the plainest possible breach of [Whole app](../Specs/Whole%20app.md)'s promise that Smoozy always tells the truth about what happened.

**F2. The outcome of a write sequence is the first error any call in it returned, and a later success never clears it.** Observed: a detached volume returns `EIO` (5) **once, at the write**, and `fsync` and `close` then return 0. **An arrangement that inspects only the last call sees a clean finish.**

**F3. Two causes are not claimed apart where the system does not separate them.** A removed directory and a detached volume **both give `ENOENT` (2)** where the mount point went with the volume. The adapter reports *the destination is no longer there* and names neither. **The test the findings first proposed — telling them apart by whether the mount point survived — is struck rather than carried**, because in half the observed runs it survived.

**F4. A refusal is classified by what the system returned, and never by an amount.** At `open()` on the Mac: full volume `ENOSPC` (28); permission refused `EACCES` (13); destination gone `ENOENT` (2) per F3. At `write()`: full volume `ENOSPC`; **a detached volume `EIO` (5), classified by F4a**; a permission refusal cannot arise, because refusal happens at `open()`; and a removed directory returns **no error at all**, which is F1's case rather than a refusal to classify.

**F4a. The class for `EIO` at `write()`, declared here rather than left for a builder to discover. Added 2026-08-23, routed in from [`Download`](Download.md#15-unresolved) §15 item 23.** F2 has recorded the value since this document was written — a detached volume returns `EIO` (5) once, at the write, with `fsync` and `close` then returning 0 — and F4's classifier named no class for it, so the one value F2 exists to catch reached `Download` D1 as a failed write with no named cause. **The class is a fourth and not a spelling of `ENOENT`: the adapter reports *the write failed at the device*, and it names no cause behind it.**

Two things force the fourth rather than folding it into F3's *the destination is no longer there*. **`EIO` is a different value from `ENOENT`**, and F4's own rule is that a refusal is classified by what the system returned. And **a detached volume is the only cause observed for it, while nothing read establishes that it is the only cause that returns it** — so reporting *no longer there* would claim a cause the system did not give, which is the thing F3 exists to forbid. **What the class asserts is bounded:** the write failed, the failure is real and is F2's first error, and no cause is named. F1 still governs the outcome and the destination is re-checked by path after closing, exactly as for every other write.

**The fourth class is routed to `Download` and not written there.** D1 lists three classes and consumes them without re-deriving, so the line that adds this one is that document's; the gap it names in its own item 23 is closed on this side by this clause. **Nothing is claimed for Windows or Linux**, whose detached-volume values are unestablished on both ([Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 9), and the class is therefore macOS-observed and per-system like every other value in F4.

**F5. No local decision is taken from a free-space reading, so there is no full-volume test here at all.** Observed: a genuinely full volume reported **1,068 KB free, not zero**, so *available equals zero* never fires — and that observation is **the reason no threshold exists rather than a rule to derive one from**. Naming a threshold here would invent a quantity out of the very reading that warns against quantities, and two builders picking two thresholds would build two applications that disagree about when a local volume is full. **F4 is the only classifier of a refusal and F6 the only signal that a refusal is over**, and neither consults an amount. **A free-space reading is supplied only where a module asks to show one**, and it is then presented **as of the moment it was taken** and never re-read to keep a message fresh, which is the rule [16.4](#164-row-287-presented-the-disk-full-carve-out) M15 already applies on the machine side. Where one is taken, free space on this side is the same thing it is on the rented side — *"a choice of which mount to read"* ([Server protocol and transfers](Server%20protocol%20and%20transfers.md#91-a-reading-is-a-choice-of-mount-and-the-mount-is-the-one-the-write-was-attempted-on) §9.1) — so it is taken against the destination path's own volume and never against the computer.

**F6. Only a write attempt establishes that a refusal is over.** A recovered free-space reading is not that signal, which is the same split [§8.3](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#83-the-condition-identity-and-who-supplies-each-observed-value) already makes for the machine side, applied here.

**F7. Two error numbers that differ between systems are never shared between them.** `EDQUOT` is **69 on macOS and 122 on Linux**, each from its own header. **The adapter maps by symbol per system and never by number**, which is the same discipline `Security and licence` §3.1 requires of the keychain statuses.

**F8. One observed contradiction with a vendor's own manual is carried rather than smoothed.** A write to the sealed macOS system volume returned **`EPERM` (1), not the `EROFS` (30) Apple's own `open(2)` documents**. Observed, unadjudicated, and recorded because a builder reading the manual page alone would get it wrong.

**F9. *A full volume leaves no file behind* is true only where the volume was already full at open time.** Where it filled **during** the write, a truncated file remained on every run of the byte loop and an empty file on every run of the copy tool. **So no local writer ever reads the absence of a file as evidence that nothing was written**, and F1's re-check is what says what is there.

### 6.2 What the adapter is for

**F10. Every local write in the application goes through it**: a download's staged bytes and its publication, the local record and its migrations, the fetched-derivative area, the staged-transfer area's cleanup, and anything a module writes at all. **There is no second route to the filesystem.**

**F10a. Three things F10's wording did not reach, named 2026-08-22 at the Architecture seam close, routed by the runner from [Keys](Keys.md#13-unresolved) §13 items 10 and 11 and [Dependency Upload & Remap](Dependency%20Upload%20&%20Remap.md#12-unresolved) §12 item 9.**

- **A local *deletion* is a local write for this rule.** Removing a file, removing a directory and clearing a staged area all go through the adapter, and F10's own list already assumed it for the staged-transfer area's cleanup. `Keys` W8's delete is the same act; there is no second route for removal either.
- **A file a tool writes is not the application writing, and the adapter still owns the ground it writes on.** Where a module runs an outside program that writes a file — `ssh-keygen` creating a key pair is the standing case — **the destination is composed and checked through the adapter before the run, and the outcome is established by re-reading that path through the adapter after it.** The adapter does not claim to have performed the write; it owns the name and the verdict, which is F1's discipline applied to somebody else's write.
- **Reading a name back as the filesystem stores it is the adapter's, and it is a read rather than a write.** `Dependency Upload & Remap` F13 asks for the on-disk capitalisation of a path; that reading lives here, beside the collision judgement, because both are questions asked of the receiving filesystem about how it holds names. **It is not a second route to the filesystem and it is never remembered as state** — the same distinction F13 below draws for the collision judgement.

**F10b. What unit a path reaches this adapter in is ruled once, and not here. Added 2026-09-06; no auditor of this document has seen it.** `Server protocol and transfers` [§2](Server%20protocol%20and%20transfers.md#2-request-framing-and-versioning) **P7** settles it for the whole transfer seam — bytes on the machine side, the receiving system's own path unit on the desktop side, text only at the point a name is shown — and it names [C3](#72-the-name-check-before-the-transfer)'s name check as the single place a machine-side path's bytes become a local one. **Two things follow for this section and neither is a new rule.** Every call on this adapter's surface takes and returns a local path in the system's own unit and never as text, which is what makes [C15](#74-which-name-is-in-the-way)'s *"comparing those bytes against the bytes asked for is the whole test"* and [C16](#74-which-name-is-in-the-way)'s *"the bytes asked for **are** the stored name"* performable rather than aspirational. And [F10a](#62-what-the-adapter-is-for)'s third bullet — reading a name back as the filesystem stores it — answers in that same unit, never in a decoded spelling of it. **This document restates none of P7 and holds no second version of it.**

**F11. The adapter is the only place a location this document names is resolved to a path**, and no path is ever synthesised from an identity by anything else ([§2.1](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#21-the-rules-that-bind-every-identity) rule 7). *(**Corrected 2026-08-29 at the stage-close repair.** This read **"any of the four names above"**, which pointed at F10's list of **five** and, one section earlier, at [L2](#51-one-store-and-its-contents-are-another-documents-list)'s different four — a phrase no reader could follow. [`Keys`](Keys.md#13-unresolved) §13 item 20 read it as a closed list of what may be resolved and found the user's home directory absent from it, which is not what the clause said: as written it fixed **where** resolution happens and nothing about **what** may be resolved. The enumeration it means is written out below, and the half it was missing is stated with it.)*

**The locations this document names, and the adapter resolves these and no others:** the per-user application-data location ([L3](#51-one-store-and-its-contents-are-another-documents-list)) and, under it, the local record ([Section 5](#5-the-local-records-database-and-its-migrations)), the diary area ([L2a](#51-one-store-and-its-contents-are-another-documents-list)), the throwaway known-hosts area (`Security and licence` [§7](Security%20and%20licence.md#7-host-trust)) and Smoozy's own temporary area ([L3a](#51-one-store-and-its-contents-are-another-documents-list)); the fetched-derivative area and the staged-transfer area; the downloads folder ([Section 8](#8-where-a-download-is-proposed-to-land)); and the user home directory ([L3a](#51-one-store-and-its-contents-are-another-documents-list)). **The operating system's secret store is not among them**, being reached through [Section 9](#9-the-secret-adapter-and-its-helper)'s adapter and never resolved to a path at all. **A path the user themselves chose is not resolved from a name**: it arrives as a path and is judged by [Section 7](#7-the-receiving-filesystem-collision-judgement) like any other. *(Added 2026-10-25 by step 11's Mac update piece, Rule 7: **on macOS the list also holds Z8's installed path — the complete bundle the running program sits in, read from the system's own report of the running program and never composed from a name — and, beside it and composed from it alone, E18's staging folder and Z7's named backup of the previous bundle.** Z5, Z7, Z8 and E18 name all three and this list did not, so the update would have had to compose a path the adapter does not resolve, which this section's missing half calls a gap. On Windows and Linux none is named. No auditor has seen it.)* *(Added 2026-10-26 by step 11's Windows update piece, Rule 7: **on Windows the list also holds Z15's new per-user folders** — one folder per version Smoozy installs for itself, named by that version, inside one builds folder under the per-user application-data location (`windows-update`). Z15 names *a new per-user folder* and this list did not. That piece's sentence above, *On Windows and Linux none is named*, is narrowed by this one for Windows. No auditor has seen it.)*

**The missing half: a module that needs a location this list does not hold has found a gap in this section, and never a licence to compose one.** [A1](#4-module-api-boundaries) already closes the route — a service [Section 21](#21-what-this-document-supplies-and-to-which-document) does not name has no route at all — and this states the set that route resolves, so the two closures are the same closure read from either end. `Keys` §13 item 20 was exactly that gap, and L3a closes it.

### 6.3 What it may not read as state

**F12. Nothing here reads a file's presence, its name or its size as state** ([§1.3](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#13-rules-that-bind-every-store) rule 5, which binds this document with no carve-out of its own). Every state fact comes from a record Smoozy wrote.

**F13. The collision judgement of [Section 7](#7-the-receiving-filesystem-collision-judgement) is not an exception to F12, and the distinction is the rule's own.** That rule already draws it for the re-run's already-there test, which is *"a rule about what to render, never a reading of state"*. **The collision judgement is a rule about what name to write.** It asks the receiving filesystem a question whose answer is about the world rather than about anything Smoozy did, it is never remembered as state, and no durable record is written from it.

**And F1's re-check stands on the same distinction, which is said here because F12 has no carve-out and F1 is the strongest rule in this section.** The re-check is a question about the world at the moment of the write — whether these bytes are at this path now — and **its answer is never held as a fact about a file**. What is written durably is not the re-check but **the write's own outcome under [§9.4](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#94-the-outcome-record)**, which is a record Smoozy wrote about what Smoozy did, exactly as F12 requires of every state fact.

### 6.4 The case no error reports, named because it is not defended

**F14. Where a mount-point directory survives a detach, `open()` succeeds and the file lands on the underlying volume, with no error anywhere.** Reproduced twice, and neither behaviour adjudicated as the normal case. **F1's re-check by path does not catch it**, because the file genuinely is at that path — on the wrong disk. **What is lost is which volume the file is on, and not the file**: the path the user chose still shows it, and it is whole. It is named here rather than defended, and [Section 22](#22-unresolved) carries it.

### 6.5 The publication operation, and what would demonstrate it

**F15. The operation is the platform's atomic rename of the staged file onto the final path, and staging is on the same filesystem as the final name** ([Server protocol and transfers](Server%20protocol%20and%20transfers.md#75-publication-is-the-last-step-and-it-is-one-step) S18's four requirements: one step, no part-arrived bytes at the final name, same filesystem, and the staged and final objects' stable file identity supplied where the platform has one — F15a). **This is the choice Fact 79 was waiting on**; the register records the operation as unchosen and that is what changes here.

**F15a. The fourth requirement — the staged and final objects' stable file identity — and what supplies it per system. Added 2026-08-22, with S18's fourth requirement.** S15's publication pre-state, its crash-recovery proof and S28's removal match read it. **Where the supplier cannot answer, S15's own rule is taken rather than restated: the record says the identity is unavailable and publication proceeds on the byte length and digest; removal alone keeps the requirement absolute.**

- **macOS and Linux: the `st_dev`/`st_ino` pair, read with the `stat` family** — on the open descriptor where the adapter holds one, by path otherwise. The pair rule is the standard's own: *"The st_ino and st_dev fields taken together uniquely identify the file within the system."* (The Open Group Base Specifications Issue 7, 2018 edition, IEEE Std 1003.1-2017, `<sys/stat.h>`, `https://pubs.opengroup.org/onlinepubs/9699919799/basedefs/sys_stat.h.html`, read 2026-08-22). Apple's own page carries both fields — `st_ino` *"inode's number"*, `st_dev` *"device inode resides on"* (*Mac OS X Manual Page For stat(2)*, `https://developer.apple.com/library/archive/documentation/System/Conceptual/ManPages_iPhoneOS/man2/stat.2.html`, read 2026-08-22) — and the Linux man-pages project's own inode page is why the pair and never the inode alone is the identity: *"Each file in a filesystem has a unique inode number. Inode numbers are guaranteed to be unique only within a filesystem"* (`https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man7/inode.7.html`, read 2026-08-22). **Neither publication promises the pair is never reused after a delete**, and nothing here reads an identity match as proof against reuse: S15's recovery proof compares it beside the byte length and digest, never alone, and S28's removal match runs only against material a record of Smoozy's names.
- **Windows: the 128-bit file identifier with the volume serial number, `GetFileInformationByHandleEx` with `FileIdInfo`** — *"The 128-bit file identifier for the file. The file identifier and the volume serial number uniquely identify a file on a single computer."* (`FILE_ID_INFO`, `https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/api/winbase/ns-winbase-file_id_info`, read 2026-08-22). **Microsoft's own remarks are what make the identity-unavailable branch real on this system rather than a formality — and every one of them is published on `BY_HANDLE_FILE_INFORMATION`, about the 64-bit `nFileIndexHigh`/`nFileIndexLow` file ID, not about the 128-bit identifier, whose own page carries no such caveat**: *"Support for file IDs is file system-specific. File IDs are not guaranteed to be unique over time, because file systems are free to reuse them. In some cases, the file ID for a file can change over time."*, for FAT *"Thus, a FAT file ID can change over time."*, for NTFS *"In the NTFS file system, a file keeps the same file ID until it is deleted."*, and of the 64-bit form against the newer filesystem *"The 64-bit identifier in this structure is not guaranteed to be unique on ReFS."* (`BY_HANDLE_FILE_INFORMATION`, `https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/api/fileapi/ns-fileapi-by_handle_file_information`, read 2026-08-22). **The caution is carried across to the 128-bit identifier as this document's own caution rather than as that page's claim about it.** **So the adapter answers with the identity where the call succeeds on a filesystem whose identifiers Microsoft documents as stable, and reports identity unavailable where the call refuses or the volume's own identifiers are documented changeable** — a changeable value passed off as stable would defeat the one consumer for which the requirement stays absolute, S28's removal match. Documentation alone, on a system with no computer ([Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 2).
- **No reading of any of this has been run on any system**, the requirement adds nothing to F18's throwaway, and the collision judgement of [Section 7](#7-the-receiving-filesystem-collision-judgement) takes no identity from it — C10 asks the filesystem by name, deliberately, and stays that way.

**F16. Per system.** **macOS: `rename(2)`**, on the vendor's own crash guarantee — documented, macOS 26.5, page dated June 3, 2021, read 2026-08-17: *"The rename() system call guarantees that an instance of new will always exist, even if the system should crash in the middle of the operation."* (S19). **Linux: `rename(2)`**, on the same operating system's own manual quoted in Fact 78's answer — *"If newpath already exists, it will be atomically replaced, so that there is no point at which another process attempting to access newpath will find it missing."* **Windows: the documentation route is answered negatively and the throwaway route is unrun.** Microsoft documents `MoveFileExW` with `MOVEFILE_REPLACE_EXISTING` and `ReplaceFileW`, but no crash guarantee for either; `ReplaceFileW` documents a failure state in which the final name is absent and the replacement remains under its original name (Fact 79). [Section 22](#22-unresolved) carries the unrun throwaway with what breaks.

**Both addresses were recorded nowhere in this project — not here and not in Fact 79 — and are supplied 2026-08-23 by reading the two pages.** `MoveFileExW`, `https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/api/winbase/nf-winbase-movefileexw`, page dated 2018-12-05, read 2026-08-23; `ReplaceFileW`, `https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/api/winbase/nf-winbase-replacefilew`, page dated 2018-12-05, read 2026-08-23. These two addresses serve every use of either call in this document — F20's backup parameter, C22, and [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 4.

**The negative is confirmed by the re-reading rather than assumed from the earlier one:** neither page contains the word *atomic*, *crash*, *power* or *interrupt* anywhere. `ReplaceFileW`'s summary stands word for word — *"Replaces one file with another file, with the option of creating a backup copy of the original file. The replacement file assumes the name of the replaced file and its identity."* — and so does its named failure state, `ERROR_UNABLE_TO_MOVE_REPLACEMENT` 1176 (0x498): *"The replacement file could not be renamed. If lpBackupFileName was specified, the replaced and replacement files retain their original file names. Otherwise, the replaced file no longer exists and the replacement file exists under its original name."*

**One quotation is corrected against its own page, and the trimmed half is a condition rather than a flourish.** `MOVEFILE_REPLACE_EXISTING` is carried in this project as *"If a file named lpNewFileName exists, the function replaces its contents with the contents of the lpExistingFileName file"*, and the page's sentence does not end there: *"If a file named lpNewFileName exists, the function replaces its contents with the contents of the lpExistingFileName file, provided that security requirements regarding access control lists (ACLs) are met."* **Nothing that rests on it moves.** The Windows third of F16 is the **absence** of a crash guarantee, which the full sentence does not supply either; the ACL clause is a condition on the call succeeding at all, which arrives as a refusal the adapter classifies, and it is not a statement about what survives an interruption.

**F17. The Windows publication question and the Windows update question are not assumed to have one answer.** The update shape there is **side-by-side rather than in-place** ([20.4](#204-windows-side-by-side-and-never-in-place)), which is a statement about replacing a **running executable** and says nothing about replacing an ordinary data file. **Neither is read across to the other.**

**F18. What would demonstrate the operation, per system, and it is not claimed demonstrated.** A throwaway on a computer running that system, writing a staged file of known contents beside its final path on one filesystem, then **forcibly killing the process at the publication boundary, repeatedly**, and after each kill reading the final path and the staging path. **The claim holds if, on every run, the final name holds either the whole previous file or the whole new one and never a mixture, and the staging path's absence is the operation's own result.** **A negative result is any one of:** a final name holding fewer than the whole staged bytes; a final name absent with the staging path also gone; a rename returning success where the file at the final name is not the staged file. **Nothing has been run on any computer**, and Fact 79's own *Machine* line means the Linux run wants a **desktop** Linux computer, which this project does not have.

**F19. What holds without F18.** [§9.6](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#96-where-a-unit-must-be-atomic-and-the-ground-for-it-is-not-established) — the unit is written as atomic and this document says so, rather than the boundary being softened to whatever a platform happens to give — and S18's two requirements that do not depend on the platform: staging on the same filesystem, and no route to the final name but the publication step.

**F20. The adapter carries the update publication operations, which is why F10's *no second route* stands unqualified.** F15's ordinary-data operation is the plain atomic rename. Updates are three different publication units:

- **macOS: the supported replacement API's own operation, which carries a named backup for rollback** (Z5). **The directory form of that operation is unread** ([Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 43). This is the variant in full.
- **Linux: the verified package is handed to the package mechanism**, which retains the immediately previous package through Z21's probe. The adapter does not perform a file-level rename or maintain a second package database.
- **Windows: a documented file-level backup exists and is not wanted** — **this bullet said *no file-level backup either* until 2026-08-21, and that was false.** `ReplaceFileW` takes one as a named parameter: *"Replaces one file with another file, with the option of creating a backup copy of the original file."*, with `lpBackupFileName` — *"The name of the file that will serve as a backup copy of the lpReplacedFileName file. If this parameter is NULL, no backup file is created."* — and the constraint *"The backup file, replaced file, and replacement file must all reside on the same volume."* **Address at [F16](#65-the-publication-operation-and-what-would-demonstrate-it), supplied 2026-08-23; all three quotations were re-read there and stand word for word.** **The conclusion does not move and the reason is unchanged:** the update shape here is side-by-side, so no file is replaced for a backup to be of, and what stands in for one is [Z15](#204-windows-side-by-side-and-never-in-place)'s kept previous build. **What the correction buys is that a later reader does not reach for `ReplaceFileW` believing this document ruled it out on the ground that it did not exist.**

**Each system now has one bounded previous-unit rollback route in Z21.** Nothing but the updater uses these operations and every ordinary data write publishes with F15's plain operation.

### 6.6 The folder-identity comparison

**Constraint:** Row 484 — the searched-folder list's *"A folder already on the list is not added twice and is not refused either: the list flashes the row it is already on"*, labelled **settled at Design** (2026-08-23) — and **Fact 140, whose answer of record is [The three desktop systems read again](The%20three%20desktop%20systems%20read%20again%20—%20dying,%20revealing,%20resolving,%20and%20what%20the%20shell%20does%20not%20expose.md) §8.** Added 2026-08-27 at the twelfth sitting. *(Constraint line supplied 2026-08-29 at the stage-close repair: this was the one row-answering subsection in the document with none, and the row it was written for was in neither the head count nor the head table.)* **One comparison, one answer, serving every module that must decide sameness of two folder paths.**

**F21. Whether two paths name one folder is answered by comparing identity numbers the filesystem assigns, live, at the moment the answer is needed — never by comparing text.** The comparison is a service of this adapter, used by any module that must decide sameness of two folder paths. Fact 140, answered on all three systems in [The three desktop systems read again](The%20three%20desktop%20systems%20read%20again%20—%20dying,%20revealing,%20resolving,%20and%20what%20the%20shell%20does%20not%20expose.md) §8.

- **macOS: `stat` / `fstat`, comparing the `st_dev` and `st_ino` pair.** The same pair F15a already uses for file identity. **Symbolic links are resolved before the call**, because Apple's own file-identity value disagrees with `stat` through a symbolic link — observed and reproduced (Fact 140). The resolution makes them agree.
- **Windows: `GetFileInformationByHandleEx` with `FileIdInfo`, comparing the volume serial number and the 128-bit file identifier** (documentation alone, [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 2). The same call F15a already uses for file identity. **Where the call refuses or the volume's identifiers are documented changeable, the comparison reports *cannot determine*** — the same identity-unavailable branch F15a takes, for the same reason. **Symbolic links and junctions are resolved before opening the handle**, on the same trap Windows documents for its file-identity call.
- **Linux: `stat` / `statx`, comparing `st_dev` and `st_ino`** (documentation alone, [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 3). **Symbolic links are resolved before the call.** **Bind mounts are the one case where two paths are one folder with no text relationship at all**, and on a bind mount the `st_dev` / `st_ino` pair is the only route that can see it — whether `st_dev` is preserved across a bind mount is documented nowhere read and is unresolved ([Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 3).

**F21a. Where a folder does not exist — a path not yet created — no system identifies it.** The comparison falls back to the resolved canonical text, and **the interface says so rather than pretending** the answer is certain. Fact 140's four rules.

**F21b. The result is never stored as a durable key.** Both POSIX and Microsoft state that these values are reused over time. The comparison is taken live and discarded.

**F21c. No reading of the folder-identity comparison has been run on Windows or Linux**, and on the Mac the symbolic-link trap was observed and reproduced but no network-filesystem or case-sensitive-volume run was made. [Section 22](#22-unresolved) items 2 and 3 carry the unrun halves. **The network-filesystem half is not merely unrun — it is documented against on all three systems, and F21d states it** *(added 2026-08-29 at the stage-close repair, because this sentence presented a published negative as a test nobody had performed and pointed at two items that say nothing about network volumes on any system, macOS included)*.

**F21d. The comparison is a *local* answer, and on a network volume it takes F21a's *cannot determine* branch and says so. Added 2026-08-29 at the stage-close repair, per Row 484 and Fact 140.** F21 states the comparison as the answer for *"any module that must decide sameness of two folder paths"* with no restriction to local volumes; **Fact 140's own register entry says the opposite in as many words:** *"**And no vendor publishes identity across network filesystems**, so the answer is a local one."* *(The register's own emphasis restored 2026-08-29 at the re-gate repair; the quotation was word-perfect and dropped the bold.)* Row 484's subject is the searched-folder list and a search folder on a network share is ordinary, so this is not a hypothetical case. **The three vendor positions, from the answer of record's *The published limits, per vendor* ([The three desktop systems read again](The%20three%20desktop%20systems%20read%20again%20—%20dying,%20revealing,%20resolving,%20and%20what%20the%20shell%20does%20not%20expose.md) §8):**

- **Windows: the information call *"may fail, return partial information, or full information"* depending on the server**, and normalized-path queries are not supported by the file-sharing protocol. *(Documentation alone, [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 2.)*
- **Linux: the client's own documentation refuses it both ways** — using the server's inode numbers, *"the server does not guarantee that the inode numbers are unique if multiple server side mounts are exported under a single share"*; generating them client-side, *"These inode numbers will vary after unmount or reboot"*. *(Documentation alone, item 3.)*
- **macOS: Apple publishes nothing found on identity over network filesystems**, across five sources read on the one machine this project has. *(A gap in the vendor's documentation rather than in the reading, and the answer of record labels it not-independently-verified.)*

**F21e. How the adapter tells a network volume from a local one, per system. Added 2026-09-03 at Build step 2**, because F21d states an answer the adapter gives *wherever the path resolves onto a network volume* and nothing anywhere said how it would know — [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 56's own second half, in its own words: *nothing establishes how the adapter tells a network volume from a local one in the first place*. **macOS publishes the test and it is one flag**: `statfs(2)`'s `MNT_LOCAL`, whose whole description is *"File system is stored locally"* (macOS's own manual page shipped on the machine, `statfs(2)`, macOS 26.6, page dated August 14, 2008, read 2026-09-03). **So on macOS F21d is kept for real**: a path whose volume does not carry that flag takes the *cannot determine* branch before any identity call is made. **On Windows and Linux nothing read establishes a counterpart, so the branch is reached only where the identity call itself refuses**, which is a narrower net than F21d describes — item 56 carries what that costs and this clause does not soften it. **The reading is not carried across**: `MNT_LOCAL` is Apple's own flag on Apple's own call and says nothing about either other system.

**So the adapter answers *cannot determine* wherever the path resolves onto a network volume**, on all three systems and whatever the call returns there — **the branch F21's own Windows bullet already names**, taken here for the same reason F15a takes it where a volume's identifiers are documented changeable: a value the vendor will not stand behind, passed off as an identity, is worse than no answer. **And the interface says so rather than pretending, which is F21a's rule applied to a second cause**; no text comparison stands in for it, because F21's whole ground is that text is wrong in documented ways on all three. [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 56 carries what it costs.

---

## 7. The receiving-filesystem collision judgement

**Constraint:** Row 303, quoted at the head of this document; [The shape](The%20shape.md#17-download) — `Download`'s *"local collision handling is the user-facing response to that adapter's collision judgement, not a second collision judgement"*; and `Job files and outputs` N14, observed 2026-08-17: **seventeen names written on the rental came back as fourteen files on the Mac**, three destroyed on arrival, with **every fetch reporting success**.

### 7.1 Two judgements at two moments

**C1. There are two judgements and they answer two different questions.** *Can this name be written on this volume at all* is answered **before the transfer is arranged** ([7.2](#72-the-name-check-before-the-transfer)). *Is this name already taken* is answered **at publication and not before** ([7.3](#73-the-taken-check-is-an-exclusive-create-and-the-filesystem-answers-it)). Merging them into one check would either move bytes for a name that can never be written, or judge takenness at a moment that is not the moment it matters.

**C2. Both are the adapter's and neither is `Download`'s.** `Download` is handed the outcome and builds the user-facing response on it ([7.5](#75-what-is-handed-to-download)).

### 7.2 The name check before the transfer

**C3. It runs against the receiving system and the receiving volume, before any byte moves.** Fact 24's own conclusion forces the moment: **macOS refuses invalid UTF-8 outright with `EILSEQ` while Linux permits any byte but `/` and NUL**, so a frame can come back carrying a name that cannot be written to an APFS volume **at all**, *"and that must be caught before the transfer rather than during it."*

**C4. Four outcomes, and the set is closed.** *Writable as given*; *not representable on this system*; *too long in this system's own unit*; *silently transformed by this system*. **The last three are all *not acceptable as given*** and are handed on together with which of the three it was.

**C5. The length unit is two units and not three, and the binding one is not the receiving system's.** **Linux is 255 bytes** of UTF-8, from ext4's own kernel documentation and POSIX's own definition; **Windows and macOS are 255 UTF-16 code units**, the macOS figure **measured rather than documented**. A name is produced on a rented **Linux** machine, so it can arrive at up to 255 bytes and be refused by neither, one, or both of the other two depending on its content. **The check is made in the receiving system's own unit, per component**, and never once in characters. **Apple's own two manual pages contradict each other here and both are wrong** — `pathconf(2)` says bytes, `getattrlist(2)` says characters — with a 765-byte name accepted and a 128-character name refused; the adapter follows the measurement and not either page.

**C6. A path length ceiling limits the pathname handed to one call and not the file's depth.** Both observed runs hit the refusal at 1,041 bytes. **So a fetched tree may go deeper than any absolute path macOS will accept while staying reachable by relative descent**, and the adapter's own writes descend rather than composing one long absolute path.

**C7. The three silent failures are treated as refusals rather than as successes, and only one of them is on a Mac.** **Windows silently strips trailing periods and spaces** from any path not prefixed with exactly `\\?\` — documented by Microsoft, no error, the file lands under a different name — **and this is the one that will bite, because Linux produces such names happily**. **Windows silently collapses a reserved stem**, so `NUL.png` *"is equivalent to NUL"*, a device rather than a file, and the frame is discarded. **A Linux case-folding directory silently loses case-insensitive lookup** for a name not valid in the volume's encoding. **A name that would be silently transformed is refused by C4 rather than written**, because a silent transformation is exactly what Row 303 forbids. **The Windows half of this clause is documentation alone and is permanently unresolved — [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 62 carries it, with what breaks.**

**C8. Everything else measured on the Mac is a visible hard error and visible errors are safe** — `ENAMETOOLONG`, `EILSEQ`, `EEXIST`. **Nothing is claimed about what Windows returns**: no page read states it and nothing was measured. **[Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 63 carries that as permanently unresolved, with what breaks and what the check keeps without it.**

**C9. A base character carries at most 31 combining marks before `EILSEQ`.** Measured, undocumented, on one volume format, and carried as a limit of the check rather than as a rule about the system.

### 7.3 The taken check is an exclusive create, and the filesystem answers it

**C10. The question *is this name already taken* is put to the filesystem as an exclusive create of the final name — create if absent, fail if present — and the created entry is the reservation publication renames over.** The answer and the reservation are one act, and they are adjacent to publication rather than early.

**C11. That is what makes the judgement independent of every folding rule, including the ones nobody documents.** A filesystem's own answer to *create this name if absent* already embodies its own case and normalisation rules. **macOS folding is Unicode *full* folding** — tested against the folding table and predicting all fifteen observed pairs where simple folding mispredicts three — so `ß` and `ss` are one file and **a one-character name can collide with a two-character one**; a check that compared lengths or characters would miss it and the filesystem does not. **NTFS's folding rule is undocumented on every page read**, and the filesystem still answers. **This is the difference between asking and modelling**, and modelling is what the input document warns against in the plainest terms: *"any canonicaliser that manipulates strings rather than calling the system"* is the trap, and *"a resolver that rejoins the strings it was handed cannot detect a near-miss whatever it is called."*

**C12. Where the exclusive create fails because the name is present, no bytes are published and nothing is overwritten.** That is Row 303 kept, on all three systems, by one mechanism.

**C13. The reservation's window is declared rather than discovered.** Between the exclusive create and the rename there is a moment at which a **zero-byte** file stands at the final name. It is not part-arrived bytes and S18's second requirement is untouched, but it is a file the user could meet. **It is never unrecorded:** [Server protocol and transfers](Server%20protocol%20and%20transfers.md#75-publication-is-the-last-step-and-it-is-one-step) S15 already requires the transfer record to be in **publication pending**, naming both the staging path and the final path, and durable **before the first step of publication runs**. So a crash inside the window leaves a file the record names, and the next run either completes the publication or removes the reservation. **What the reservation departs from is S15's own words and not a bare reading of S18**: *"Publication is a rename of the staging path onto the final path, and it is the only moment the final name changes. Nothing else in a transfer touches the final name — not a create, not a truncate, not a touch, not a permission change."* **C10 creates the final name**, which is the first of the four acts that sentence excludes by name. **The difference is declared here in the same sense S17's degradation is declared, and it is stated rather than met at Build**; the disagreement with S15 itself is not repaired here and is carried as a seam ([Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 32). **The file the user could meet was put through [Section 23](#23-moments-that-reached-the-creator)'s own question and the Creator ruled it acceptable on 2026-08-18**, so C10 stands as written; [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 30 carries the window and what it costs.

**C14. A deliberate replacement is not a silent one.** Where the user has answered `Download`'s question with *replace*, the adapter publishes with the replacing rename of [6.5](#65-the-publication-operation-and-what-would-demonstrate-it) and no reservation is taken. **Row 303 forbids silence, not replacement.**

**C22. Because the reservation stands at the final name, every ordinary publication is now a rename onto an existing entry.** Where the name was absent, C10's exclusive create is what makes it present, so the publishing rename **always replaces and never lands on an absent name** — the weaker operation is replaced by the stronger one in **every** case rather than only in C14's. **That makes Windows's documented negative answer load-bearing for every download, and the throwaway that would observe the boundary remains unrun.** [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 4 carries it in those terms.

### 7.4 Which name is in the way

**C15. On macOS the stored name is readable in one call and the near-miss is separable from the exact match.** `getattrlist(ATTR_CMN_NAME)`, `fcntl(F_GETPATH)` and the C `realpath(3)` all return the name **as stored on disk**, and Apple's own `realpath(3)` documents `F_GETPATH` as the mechanism; **comparing those bytes against the bytes asked for is the whole test**. **APFS stores names byte for byte as given** and normalises only its lookup hash, so names round-trip; **HFS+ rewrites them** to a decomposed form, which is documentation plus inference and was never measured.

**C16. On Linux the ordinary case needs no read at all, and ext4 supplies the folding case.** On a case-sensitive volume a lookup that finds an entry at exactly the bytes asked for is the whole test, and nothing has to ask what the stored name is because the bytes asked for **are** the stored name.

**In a case-folding ext4 directory, the stored name is the byte-for-byte name userspace supplied and the folded form is not exposed to userspace or stored on disk** (Fact 122). Directory enumeration therefore supplies the stored name rather than a folded spelling.

**What remains unresolved is every folding filesystem that is not ext4, which filesystem a distribution presents, and the unrun Linux throwaway.** On such an unestablished folding filesystem the outcome handed on says *taken* without saying which stored name took it, while **Row 303 is still kept by C10**, because the exclusive create is answered by the filesystem's own folding rule whatever that rule is.

**C17. On Windows, `FileNormalizedNameInformation` returns the exact casing stored on disk on NTFS and ReFS.** The Win32 surface is `GetFileInformationByHandleEx` with `FileNormalizedNameInfo`; other filesystems refuse with `STATUS_INVALID_DEVICE_REQUEST` (Fact 121). **Directory-enumeration casing and NTFS's folding rule remain undocumented**, and nobody has run the call on a Windows computer.

**C18. What remains costs wording and not the promise.** C10 detects the collision on Windows exactly as on the other two, because the exclusive create fails whatever the volume's folding rule is. **On NTFS and ReFS, C17 supplies which stored name is in the way. On every other Windows filesystem, the call refuses loudly**, so the outcome says *taken* without saying *taken by a name that differs only in capitals*. **Row 303 is kept on all three; the distinction remains unresolved on Windows filesystems other than NTFS and ReFS, and the Windows throwaway remains unrun.**

### 7.5 What is handed to Download

**C19. The adapter hands on an outcome and never a prompt, a rename or a decision.** The outcome carries: which of C4's four the name check gave, and for a refusal which of the three; whether the name was taken; and, where the system supplied it, the stored name that took it. **`Download`'s Replace and Keep rules are the user-facing response built on this, never a second collision judgement**, which is the shape's own wording.

**C20. Appending a non-colliding name is `Download`'s act and is judged again here.** A name it composes is a name, so it goes through C3 and C10 like any other; **nothing composed is assumed writable because it was composed**.

**C21. The inventory's names are the machine's.** `Job files and outputs` supplies them and says outright that this judgement *"is what stands between them and the loss observed on 2026-08-17"* — seventeen names, fourteen files, every fetch reporting success. **The count of what arrived is therefore never the count of what was fetched**, and F1's re-check by path is what says which of the two a caller has.

---

## 8. Where a download is proposed to land

**Constraint:** Fact 22, answered on all three systems 2026-08-18. `Download`'s destination rules are that document's under Rows 202 to 220; what is supplied here is the folder and the rules the folder comes with.

**W1. The three systems are three different mechanisms, not three spellings of one**, and the adapter holds three routes rather than one with branches.

| | macOS | Windows | Linux |
|---|---|---|---|
| The route | `FileManager.url(for: .downloadsDirectory, in: .userDomainMask, appropriateFor: nil, create:)` | `SHGetKnownFolderPath(FOLDERID_Downloads, 0, NULL, &p)`, then `CoTaskMemFree` | read `XDG_DOWNLOAD_DIR` from `${XDG_CONFIG_HOME:-$HOME/.config}/user-dirs.dirs` |
| Governed by | Apple's Foundation reference | Microsoft's Known Folders | **no specification at all** — the `xdg-user-dirs` tool and its manual pages |
| Can return nothing | the array may be empty | `E_FAIL` or `E_INVALIDARG` | yes; the tool's own helper returns `$HOME` |

**W2. The Windows default-path flag is never passed.** Windows is the one system with a real relocation API whose default read **follows** the relocation, and `KF_FLAG_DEFAULT_PATH` turns that off. **Asking for the default path defeats the user's own choice**, which is the one thing a destination rule must not do.

**W3. On Linux the path is read and never assembled.** The XDG Base Directory Specification contains the string `download` **zero times** and freedesktop's specification index carries no `user-dirs` entry; what governs it is a **tool**. **The documented fallback is `$HOME`, not `$HOME/Downloads`**, and **Linux is the only system where the on-disk name is genuinely translated**, so a path built from the English word is wrong there and only there. **Nothing caches it**, because the tool resets a missing downloads folder to `$HOME` at next login and a cached path would outlive that.

**W4. Nothing is written into the folder unasked, and that is Apple's own instruction rather than a preference.** Documented: an app *"must not write files to the preceding directories unless explicitly directed to do so by the user"*. **So the adapter proposes the folder and never writes into it on its own**; what the user is asked and when is `Download`'s and Design's.

**W5. Where the route returns nothing, the adapter says so and proposes nothing.** It does not fall back to a composed path, and it does not create a folder the system did not name. **One trap in the managed Windows route:** `.NET`'s `Environment.SpecialFolder` has **no Downloads member** and Microsoft's own Known Folder table records `CSIDL Equivalent: None`, so there is no older equivalent to fall back on.

**W6. One thing is unresolved and it blocks no route. Repaired 2026-08-23: this said two, and the second closed on 2026-08-22.** Whether the macOS route **looks the location up or composes it** — observation suggests composed — which wants a compiled Foundation call. [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 20 carries it.

**The struck half, and it is struck because it was answered rather than abandoned.** This clause read *"and **whether Windows localises the on-disk name**, which wants a Windows computer"*. **The input document withdrew exactly that on 2026-08-22**, in its own correction: *"Microsoft documents it and it wanted a page rather than a computer — `KNOWNFOLDER_DEFINITION` and the `FOLDERID_Downloads` entry, both read 2026-08-20"*, and it dropped the item from its own closing list of what only a Windows computer can close. **The answer is negative and first-party:** `KNOWNFOLDER_DEFINITION`'s `pszRelativePath` is *"a null-terminated Unicode string, refers to the physical file system path, and is not localized"*, while `pszLocalizedName` is a resource reference held in the folder's `Desktop.ini` and `SHGetLocalizedName` hands back a resource module and an identifier, never a path. **Address supplied 2026-08-23 with the reading confirmed word for word:** `https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/api/shobjidl_core/ns-shobjidl_core-knownfolder_definition`, page dated 2018-12-05, read 2026-08-23. **So Windows does not translate the on-disk name**, which is what [W3](#8-where-a-download-is-proposed-to-land) already states from the other side in calling Linux the only system where it is translated — the two clauses now agree. **Nothing in W1's Windows row moves:** the path is still read through `SHGetKnownFolderPath` and never composed from the English word, and W2's refusal to pass `KF_FLAG_DEFAULT_PATH` is what protects a relocated folder, which is a different question from a translated name.

**W7. Whatever the folder turns out to be, a name written into it is judged by [Section 7](#7-the-receiving-filesystem-collision-judgement) like any other.** A folder the system named is not a folder whose names are safe.

---

## 9. The secret adapter and its helper

**Constraint:** [Security and licence](Security%20and%20licence.md#3-the-secret-store-on-each-system) §3 decides the store on each system and it is **not re-decided here**; §1.4 fixes the three hops and states that *"`Desktop app and delivery` owns the adapter, the helper and the module API boundaries."*

**S1. The store per system is taken as decided.** macOS: the **file-based keychain through the `SecItem` API**, with the data-protection keychain explicitly not used. Windows: **Credential Manager, `CRED_TYPE_GENERIC`, through `CredWriteW`, `CredReadW` and `CredDeleteW`**. Linux: **the Secret Service on the session bus, through libsecret**. The adapter implements the five-operation contract of §3.0 — put, get, erase, probe, enumerate — and adds no sixth.

**S2. The adapter is one interface with three implementations and one shared classification.** A get that fails is classified into *not there*, *locked or refused*, or *store unavailable*, and **only *not there* means the user is asked again as though nothing was remembered**. **An unmapped status is *store unavailable* and never *not there***, because an unknown status must never be read as permission to ask the user for a secret the store may still hold. **The macOS mapping is by symbol and never by number**, since the numeric values appear on no Apple page.

**S3. No store call is waited on without a deadline**, and a call that does not return within it is **store unavailable**. This is a rule about how long Smoozy waits and not a claim about what any store does when it blocks.

**S4. Every store call runs off the interface thread** (P4, P5).

**S5. The Linux probe is the adapter's and is taken at first use in a run, not at start.** *Is there somewhere to remember at all* is answered by writing a throwaway item under a reserved subject, reading it back, and erasing it. **A probe that has never succeeded in any run is what drives the promise's one-sentence notice; a probe that succeeded once and later fails is a failure and not a change of arrangement.** The result is re-taken once per application run and not cached across runs. **What is durable is not the result but the standing** — added 2026-08-21, per `Security and licence` §3.3: each probe's outcome writes the local record's three-reading `store's standing` value, which is what *never succeeded in any run* is evaluated against and what makes the notice once-per-installation rather than once-per-run. **The adapter still takes a fresh probe every run and never answers from the standing.**

**S6. The module boundary is the thing this document builds, and it has exactly two shapes.** A module receives either **a use of the secret carried out on its behalf** — the adapter opens the channel, the endpoint consumes the plaintext, and the module is handed the outcome — or **a handle it can present again**, which names a kind and a subject and carries no value. **No module receives a plaintext**, and no interface of this document returns one.

**S7. The askpass helper is started by the one-use broker and lives for one delivery.** It holds the plaintext for the length of that delivery and nothing beyond it; it is P3's first helper; it binds no listener; and **no secret is ever a process argument or an environment value**, which is `Security and licence` §13 rules 1 and 2 and is not softened here.

**S8. The three plaintext hops are the complete list and this document adds no fourth.** The adapter for the length of its call, the one-use broker for the length of one delivery, and the final endpoint that consumes it. **A builder that needs a fourth has found a fault in this section.**

**S9. The post-update keychain dialog on macOS is an ordinary outcome of this document's own updater, and not a failure path.** Apple documents that *"You must sometimes reauthorize updated apps"* and that the dialog *"appears if you recently updated your system software or the app, or if the app has been modified"* — **sometimes**, with no source saying which updates trigger it. **So nothing here predicts it, nothing schedules around it, and nothing reads it as a broken store**: it presents as *locked or refused* under S2, which means Smoozy reports that it could not reach what it remembered, offers the action again, **does not ask the user for the secret and does not forget it**.

**S10. Erasure is confirmed where the system confirms it and inferred nowhere.** macOS: `SecItemDelete`'s status plus a following query returning `errSecItemNotFound`, observed end to end. Windows: the `CredDelete` return is the confirmation, because the read-back claim is **inference rather than citation**. Linux: an erase that could not unlock is reported as an erase that **did not happen**.

**S11. The recorded erase debt is `Security and licence` §5.1's and this document performs it rather than defining it.** Its sweep runs after the first successful store operation of a run and acts on marks and on nothing else.

---

## 10. The application-identity adapter

**Constraint:** `Keys` G11’s default key name and N1’s recorded-at-entry comparison use the computer and login account names. This adapter is their only reader; no other feature reads either name.

**I1. This adapter is the only place the computer name or the login account name is read, and one source is named per system.** Nothing else in the application reads either, and no path, identity, filename or record field is composed from either ([L3](#51-one-store-and-its-contents-are-another-documents-list), [§2.1](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#21-the-rules-that-bind-every-identity) rules 3 and 6).

**I2. The computer name, per system, with the trap each avoids.**

- **macOS: `SCDynamicStoreCopyComputerName`**, which Apple documents as returning *"the current computer name"*. **Never `gethostname(3)`**: observed, with `HostName` unset, it returned a **fourth** string that is neither of the two names actually set — `LocalHostName` plus `.local`. Apple documents **three** distinct names in `scutil(8)`, and reading the wrong one is silent.
- **Windows: `GetComputerNameExW` with `ComputerNameDnsHostname`.** Microsoft documents the host-name portion as local and stable — *"The names are established at system startup, when the system reads them from the registry"* — while the **domain and fully-qualified formats are network-dependent**: *"If group policy is set, the function returns the primary domain name set by group policy"*, and *"If the local computer is not configured to use DNS names, **GetComputerNameEx** will not return DNS information."* **Neither of those two formats is read.** Documentation alone.
- **Linux: the static hostname**, which systemd's own page describes as *"the "static" hostname configured in /etc/hostname"* and which *"is not always in sync with the current hostname as returned by the gethostname(3) system call"*, falling back to the kernel's current hostname only where the static one is *"the empty string"*. Documentation alone.

**I3. The login account name, per system, and it is read for one purpose only.**

- **macOS: the password-database entry for the *effective* user id.** **Never `getlogin()` or `logname`** — **observed on the Mac**: both returned the literal `root` while the real account name at effective uid 501 was something else.
- **Linux: the same password-database entry, and this is documentation alone.** Neither of the two routes macOS's observation rules out is safe there either, and the Linux man-pages project's own page says why. Documented — `getlogin(3)`, read 2026-08-18 at `https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man3/getlogin.3.html`: *"getlogin() returns a pointer to a string containing the name of the user logged in on the controlling terminal of the process, or a null pointer if this information cannot be determined"* — **the controlling terminal rather than the process's own user id**, which is a different question and answers nothing for a windowed application that may have no terminal at all. The same page says of the obvious substitute that *"This is more flexible precisely because the user can set LOGNAME arbitrarily"*, so **`LOGNAME` is not read**, and its BUGS section records that *"it is often rather easy to fool getlogin()"*. **No Linux desktop computer exists to observe any of it**, so the route is documented and unobserved.
- **Windows: `GetUserName`**, whose documented impersonation caveat does not arise because nothing here impersonates.

**I4. The login account name is never used to build a location.** The per-user application-data location comes from each system's own call for it (L3). The name is read where a module needs a default to show or to pre-fill and for nothing else, and **it is never an identity**.

**I5. A computer name is a display label, never an identity or key.** Nothing here derives an identity from a name.

---

## 11. The two rate budgets and the one session-admission point

**Constraint:** Row 289, presented here and answered in [Server protocol and transfers](Server%20protocol%20and%20transfers.md#8-per-channel-rate-enforcement) §8; R1 — *"There is one upload budget and one download budget for the whole application, and neither is in this document. They are `Desktop app and delivery`'s single desktop-side scheduler's"*; T5's requirement that admission be one desktop-side point beside them; and the shape's own clause, added 2026-08-18, that every per-server session-admission point is admitted through it **so admission knows about every server at once**.

### 11.1 Two budgets, one unit, and where they live

**R1. There is one upload budget and one download budget, and never one budget for both.** Two, because the promise is *"the **total** across every transfer running at that moment"* in each direction and the two directions are measured against different lines; **11 to 12 times** separates them in the one measurement this project holds (Fact 25).

**R2. The unit is bytes per second**, fixed by [Server protocol and transfers](Server%20protocol%20and%20transfers.md#83-a-cap-reaches-transfers-already-running-which-is-row-306) R11, because every quantity the enforcement point holds is already a byte count. **Three units are in play across the evidence and the tools** — the standard tool's flag in Kbit/s, Fact 25's figures in MB/s, Fact 75's in KB/s — so **nothing here carries a rate without its unit**, and the one conversion to whatever the control shows the user happens **once, at the settings edge**, and nowhere else.

**R3. Each budget is one object in process memory for the life of the application run, and it is not state.** Nothing durable is written from it. **The setting that sets it is durable and is user-held** ([§11.1](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#111-five-classes-and-the-reason-there-is-no-sixth)); the object is not.

**R4. This document holds the budgets and does not enforce them.** The enforcement is per-channel, in Smoozy's own byte loop, and is `Server protocol and transfers`' R2: each channel draws from the shared budget at each accounting interval and moves no more than it drew. **This document does not divide a budget into per-transfer caps, and it accepts none from anywhere** — Fact 75 measured what that costs: a two-way 400+400 split against an 800 KB/s budget achieved **421.9 KB/s** while one shared budget on the same work achieved **820** and cut 21.85 seconds to 11.24.

**R5. A channel that finished returns its unused share at once**, which is the half Fact 75 measured as forfeited. That is the budget holder's act rather than the enforcer's, and without it the budget shrinks over the life of a batch of uneven files.

### 11.2 How the budget is divided, which is the constraint that was handed over

**R6. Every admitted channel's draw is bounded below by a non-zero share.** That is `Server protocol and transfers`' requirement of this document, and it exists so that *waiting on the budget* is a **bounded** wait and C19's inactivity deadline has something to sit against.

**R7. So a floor is fixed per direction, and admission is what keeps it true.** A channel is admitted only where the budget still affords the floor after it. **One channel is always admissible whatever the budget is**, and it takes the whole budget; beyond the first, the count of admitted channels is bounded by the budget divided by the floor. **That is why the admission point sits beside the budget rather than anywhere else**: with them apart, admission would let in channels the budget cannot feed, and the floor would be a wish.

**R8. Where the user has set no cap the budget is unlimited, the floor binds nothing, and admission is bounded only by the concurrency numbers Rows 188 and 206 pick.** The measured range those rows choose within is supplied by T6 — the knee is at **two streams** for download and around **four** for upload — and it is not narrowed here.

**R9. The division is by the floor and by nothing else.** No priority class buys a larger share of the budget; priority governs **admission order** ([Server protocol and transfers](Server%20protocol%20and%20transfers.md#63-priority-and-what-is-owned-here-is-the-mechanism) T8 to T10) and not rate. Two mechanisms shaping one number is how a total cap stops being a total.

### 11.3 The one desktop-side admission point

**R10. Every per-server admission point is admitted through one desktop-side point, so admission knows about every server at once.** The measurement forced it rather than suggesting it: a whole second rented machine bought about **20%, not 100%**, *"the slowest set the finish time"* with the line idle for 80 of 112 seconds, and **uncoordinated upload fan-out was actively worse than one machine at a time** — 1.54, 1.24, 0.99 MB/s across one, two and three boxes (Fact 25). **A pool sized per server and admitted per server tunes the wrong thing.**

**R11. What the desktop point admits is transfer channels across every server, and the update fetch beside them.** It does **not** gate the command channel or the reading channel. T5 already reserves at least one session per server for the command channel above a learned cap of one, and holds the primary connection's sole session as an alternating reading/command lease at a cap of one, so that a machine at a low cap keeps correlation; **a desktop-wide transfer queue that could delay a command would put correlation on every server at once behind transfer load**, which is the opposite of what that reserve, or that lease at a cap of one, exists for. **The update fetch is a session on no connection and reaches no server**, but it is bytes over the user's own line that Smoozy causes, so it draws from the download budget and is admitted here like any other draw — [20.1](#201-what-the-application-does-and-what-the-user-does) Z22 states it and gives the reason.

**R12. The per-server point is unchanged and remains `Server protocol and transfers`'.** Every session on a connection — a transfer channel, a command invocation, the one long-lived reading channel — is counted against one count there, because `MaxSessions` is one pool per connection and *"the pool size is a request rather than a guarantee"*. **The desktop point asks nothing of that count and never overrides it**: a channel needs both points to admit it, and either may refuse.

**R13. A refused channel is a reason to open fewer and never a reason to fail the transfer** (T2), and that holds at both points.

**R14. This document names no number.** The pool sizes are Rows 188's and 206's, the class order is Rows 189's and 207's, and the floor of R7 is a quantity Build picks inside R6's constraint. **What is fixed here is that there is one budget per direction, one floor per direction, and one point that knows every server.**

**R17. Admission at this point never starves a class, and the guarantee is this document's own at its own point.** R7's admissible count is the budget divided by the floor rather than a pool, and at a low user-set cap that quotient is **one** — so R9's class order is **a preference within each admission decision and never a gate**, and **every class is guaranteed a share of the desktop point's admissible count**, that share being Rows 189's and 207's to declare against this mechanism, which is what [Server protocol and transfers](Server%20protocol%20and%20transfers.md#63-priority-and-what-is-owned-here-is-the-mechanism) T10 already says at the per-server point and for the same reason. **Where the admissible count is one, the guarantee is turn-taking between the classes with work waiting** rather than a share, and saying so is what closes it. Without this the point would starve silently: an auto-download arrangement that *"keeps trying, and never switches itself off"* (Row 219) could hold the single admissible slot indefinitely, the user's own manual download would never be admitted, and **R13 makes a refusal not a failure — so nothing would be reported and the presentation would show *underway* for ever.**

**R18. How T5's *every session* is read here, said because this reading narrows two closed documents' plain wording.** T5's *"Every session, and not only the transfer channels"* is read as binding the **per-server** point, which counts all three session kinds — a transfer channel, a command invocation, the one long-lived reading channel — against one `MaxSessions` count (R12). **The desktop point above it takes transfer channels alone**, for R11's reason. **That narrows T5 and [The shape](The%20shape.md#7-desktop-app-and-delivery) section 7, neither of which is cited as agreeing**, so it is recorded as a seam ([Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 31) and repaired in neither of them here.

**R19. The update fetch's admission class, declared here and ranked against `File Browser` O4's list. Added 2026-08-29 at the stage-close repair, and it reverses that morning's routing of the same question:** [Z30](#201-what-the-application-does-and-what-the-user-does)'s third bullet and [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 57 had it *routed rather than written here* under R14, and the `File Browser` gate ruled that wrong on that document's own words — **O4's closing sentence hands the rank to the declaring document**, *"a class a later document declares takes its rank by that document's declaration against this list"*, and [Rendering](Rendering.md#7-declarations--atomic-or-partial-and-arbitration) §7 has already walked exactly this route for its **submission class**, ranked *"below manual download, above dependency-upload"*, without editing O4's list.

- **It is a class of the one order, not a desktop-point-only class**, and that is load-bearing rather than tidy. O5 defines N as *"the count of declared classes"* and O8 applies the same share rule at this point *"with the same N and the same rank tie-breaker"*; a class declared at this point alone would make the two points' N differ, which falsifies O8 in its own words. **At a per-server point it simply never has work waiting** — the update fetch *"is a session on no connection and reaches no server"* (R11) — so it counts into N there and takes no grant there, and nothing about the per-server point changes.
- **The rank: below O4's `upload` class and above the preview fetch.** Stated as a relation and not as an ordinal, because an ordinal ages out the moment a later document declares against O4 — the fault `Queue & History` P6 was corrected for on this same day. **Below `upload`:** the update fetch is not user-asked in the ordinary case — Z4 fixes that an update the application never had a chance to notice is not a promise broken, and Row 280's whole content is that the user does not have to remember — while O4 ranks `upload` as *"user-asked bulk"*, and its artifact draw is a whole build that blocks nothing the user is doing. **Above the preview fetch:** P6 declares that class *"below every user-asked class and below auto-download, never starved"* and fixes it as the **lowest** — *"**lowest** is what this clause actually fixes and it holds at any count"* — so nothing may be declared beneath it; and the substantive ground runs the same way, since Z26 makes the update fetch the one non-user-asked class a user can be standing in front of, while preview traffic is *"the one transfer nobody asked for by name"*.
- **The one case where a user does stand in front of it is protected by the share rule and not by the rank.** [Z26](#201-what-the-application-does-and-what-the-user-does)'s user-asked check draws here, and O5's exception — a class with work waiting throughout the last N grants takes the next one — reaches it now that it is declared, **which is R17's guarantee arriving at this draw for the first time**. The two draws of this one class cannot contend with each other at the user-asked route either: [Z27](#201-what-the-application-does-and-what-the-user-does) starts no check at all where an update is already past its check.
- **R14 still holds and this is not a number.** A rank against a declared list is not a pool size, a share or a cap; it is taken by the route O4 itself provides. **No edit to `File Browser` is owed** — O5's bound follows the count of declared classes by its own definition and scales with this one, and its own sentence already binds a builder to compute N from the declarations rather than read a worked figure as the bound. With `Rendering`'s submission class this makes N **seven**.

### 11.4 The achieved-rate reading

**R15. The reading presented to the user is `Server protocol and transfers`' R12** — Smoozy's own count of bytes moved over a window, taken at the same accounting point as the enforcement, per channel and summed. **It is never read out of a tool's output and never derived from a file's size and a start time.** It is **period-held** and nothing durable is written from it.

**R15a. The window that reading is averaged over is `Server protocol and transfers`' [R12a](Server%20protocol%20and%20transfers.md#84-the-achieved-rate-reading), and the sum is taken per direction. Added 2026-08-21, per Row 329.** The trailing three seconds, the elapsed-time divisor until that window has filled, and the two publication moments are taken whole; **this document holds no second window and smooths nothing on top of one.**

- **One reading per direction, not one for the application.** A channel belongs to one direction by construction and draws from that direction's budget (R1), so the per-channel counts sum against the budget they were drawn from — which is what puts a reading beside each of the two caps.
- **A published reading is held, never aged.** P10b permits a drawing to tick against a moment already published and to do nothing else; **a rate is not a moment**, so whatever is on screen stands unchanged until the next reading arrives. **R12a's zero at the moment a direction's last channel ends is what makes that safe** — without it a finished batch would leave its last rate on the screen for the rest of the run.
- **The one unit conversion is R2's and the reading passes through it**, at the settings edge, once, beside the cap it is read against.

**R15b. A request's remaining-time estimate is supplied and is a second reading built from the first. Its inputs, its window and what it is taken against are fixed here. Added 2026-08-21, per Row 426.** The mockup draws *about 6 minutes left* on a request's summary line; the two operands existed in two closed documents and nothing paired them.

- **The rule, and both operands are already established.** **Bytes remaining, divided by the request's own achieved rate.** Bytes remaining is the request's byte whole minus bytes staged summed over its units — the whole from `Download` [M16](Download.md#87-the-reading-of-the-request-as-a-whole-row-299) downwards and `File Browser` U11 upwards, both of which exist **before the first transfer starts**, and bytes staged from `Server protocol and transfers` [G4](Server%20protocol%20and%20transfers.md#102-what-each-kind-of-work-has-to-report-and-from-where). Units whose disposition is not to send are in neither term — skipped, refused, left-alone and cannot-land — exactly as they are absent from the bar (M16, `Download` N7).
