- **The rate is R12a's, over R12a's window, summed over this request's channels and no others.** Same accounting point, same trailing three seconds, same elapsed-time divisor before the window has filled; **the only difference from R15's reading is the set summed over.** A per-direction rate must never be used here: it rises when another request's channels end and falls when one is admitted, and neither event is about this request, so the figure would move for reasons the line it sits on does not name.
- **It is never built the naive way.** `Server protocol and transfers` R12 forbids the rate being *"derived from a file's size and a start time"*, and that forbids the average-since-the-request-began route with it. **The estimate is recomputed from the trailing window at every accounting interval** and carries no memory of an earlier estimate — no decay, no smoothing, no floor on how fast it may change.
- **Three states carry no estimate, and in each the absence is stated rather than a number shown.** **No channel of this request is moving bytes** — every unit waiting on admission or on the budget — so the divisor is zero and there is nothing to divide. **The whole is not yet known**, which for a manual request is `Download` M17's still-running enumeration. And **the request has ended**, where a remaining time is not a reading at all.
- **It is a reading, period-held, and it is held rather than aged** (R13, P10b). Nothing durable is written from it, no promise rests on it, and the drawing never runs it down between publications — a rate is not a moment, and neither is a quotient of one.
- **What it assumes is stated, because the figure is confident-looking and the assumption is the whole of its accuracy.** It assumes the request keeps the share of the budget it currently draws. A cap change (R10), a channel admitted or ended, a competing request of a higher class taking admission order (R9, `Server protocol and transfers` T8 to T10), and a start-over returning a unit's staged bytes to zero (`Server protocol and transfers` G4a) all move it — and R10's window means the figure is wrong for up to three seconds after any of them, which is exactly as long as the rate beside it is wrong.
- **Two limits ride in from the operands and are not repaired here.** The byte whole is drawn from enumerated **apparent** sizes, so a sparse file's share of it is its claimed size (`Download` M17). And downwards the count is bytes Smoozy accepted rather than bytes the line carried (R16), so the down estimate inherits that reading's standing and no more.
- **Why `Queue & History` B9's refusal does not carry, worked rather than asserted.** B9 declines a countdown over a render on three grounds, and **all three are that nobody measures the quantity**: no running figure covers a whole animation, the whole-job total is *a plan* adaptive sampling legitimately undershoots, and Blender's own estimate comes and goes. **Here neither operand is modelled.** The whole is an enumeration of exact byte counts, durable before the first byte (`Server protocol and transfers` S8), and it is not a plan — nothing may legitimately finish short of it. The rate is Smoozy's own count at the point that enforces the caps, measured over a fixed window and republished many times a minute, so the figure **converges as the request runs** instead of being a claim made once from a model. B9's own harm — *"a confident figure Smoozy cannot stand behind"* — is what a modelled figure produces; this one Smoozy can say exactly what it is, which is what this clause writes down. **What is refused here is only B9's shape**: nothing in this document offers a remaining time for a render, and nothing composes one out of this estimate.
- **What is Design's:** the wording, the rounding, whether the word *about* is used, and whether the figure is drawn at all. **[Method](Method.md)'s third escalation, checked and declined** — it is a reading and not a place to look, nothing is typed, and nothing can be lost.

**R16. Two limits are carried rather than glossed.** The shared-budget mechanism was **exercised on uploads only**, and the download case differs materially because *"a receiver enforces a cap by not reading, so buffering can let the wire rate overshoot"* — so the down reading is a count of bytes Smoozy accepted rather than of bytes the line carried. And **a budget changed while channels are moving has been run on the upload side alone** — 2026-08-22, four runs under two governors ([`Server protocol and transfers` R16](Server%20protocol%20and%20transfers.md#85-what-is-not-observed-and-it-is-named-here-rather-than-in-a-footnote)) — **while the download half stands unrun, and permanently — labelled 2026-09-02**: running it wants a rented machine and the Creator ruled on **2026-08-31** that there will be no further rented machine for answering a question, so it is not a pending item and nothing here waits on it ([Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 18). Downwards, Row 306's *yes* follows from the mechanism rather than from an observation, **permanently**, and this document does not upgrade it. *(Corrected 2026-08-31: this clause read "has never been run", which `Server protocol and transfers` R16 withdrew on 2026-08-22.)*

---

## 12. The automatic supervisor-unavailable repair trigger

**Constraint:** [The shape](The%20shape.md#each-rented-server) — *"If the supervisor does not answer while SSH is live, command callers report the supervisor as unavailable without marking the server connection down, inspect the package, and the desktop application automatically invokes the bootstrap-and-repair request under the same commit and rollback rules. Server Setup's user-facing re-run is the fallback if automatic repair fails."* The condition's observed value is `Rented-server runtime` V4; the request is `Server protocol and transfers` §5; what the machine does with it is `Rented-server runtime` §2.

**V1. The condition is presented against the server and cleared when the supervisor answers again, and the server connection is never marked down.** Its observed value is **whether the supervisor answered a request on the command path, and is nothing else** — not a reading of a process, not of a file, not of the connection. `Servers` supplies the connection's own condition and **the two are never inferred from one another**.

**V1a. The moment an unanswered command-path request moves that observed value to *the supervisor not answering* is `Server protocol and transfers` [C17a](Server%20protocol%20and%20transfers.md#46-per-waiter-deadlines)'s verdict, never a waiter's expiry alone. Added 2026-08-29, routed from that document's §12 item 22.** The transport's own next report is what shows the line carried the silence; a request left unanswered inside a break `Servers` [K3](Servers.md#21-the-break-reporting-mechanism) then declares moves nothing and raises nothing. V1's *never inferred from one another* stands as written — the transport's report decides only whether the question was carried (C17a).

**V2. The record is [§8.3](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#83-the-condition-identity-and-who-supplies-each-observed-value)'s condition record and this document raises nothing of its own.** It holds over *the supervisor not answering* and is withdrawn on *the supervisor answering*, both from that table. [Section 16](#16-progress-and-failure-presentation-plumbing) presents it.

**V3. The trigger fires at the three moments [§8.4](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#84-nothing-is-polled-to-withdraw-a-record) already permits and at no others.** Concretely: **every command-path request attempted against a server that carries the standing condition, and that finds the supervisor still not answering, sends the bootstrap-and-repair request.** **No timer, no watcher, no schedule, and nothing that reaches out to a machine on its own** — which is that section's rule and P9's.

**V4. One request is outstanding at a time, per server.** A second attempt is not composed while one is in hand. **Repeating is safe rather than merely tolerated**: the request carries an operation identity that is its idempotency key, and its idempotency is answered from the **durable package-phase record** rather than from an operation record (B3), so a repeat never acts twice.

**V5. The trigger is not consumed by a failed attempt.** The condition is withdrawn by the supervisor answering and by nothing else, so a design that fired once per raise would leave a machine unreachable for good after one failure. **Each new command-path attempt is a fresh chance**, which is what makes the trigger able to act in a condition that only ends when something else changes.

**V6. NARROWED 2026-08-31, and the narrowing decides whether this clause answers the case at all.** ~~A rented machine whose disk fills **stops its supervisor**~~ — **that is true of the supervisor that was not yet running when the mount filled, and false of one already running.** `Rented-server runtime` §16 item 25 records the opposite as **observed on 2026-08-25**: a supervisor already running **lives through its mount filling**, every durable write refusing loudly, and S14 holds the ending it could not write pending and re-attempts it. **So in the ordinary shape of this case — a machine rendering when the disk fills — the supervisor does not stop, and this clause's automatic repair trigger never fires.**

**Why that matters more than the wording.** V6 is the sentence that makes the repair trigger an answer to a filled disk at all. Read as written, a builder builds a trigger for the commonest form of the case and it can never fire; **read narrowed, this clause answers the machine that was restarted or came up into a full mount, and the running case is `Rented-server runtime` S14's entirely.**

**What survives unchanged, because it was never about the running case.** After space is freed, **the container keeper heals by itself while the systemd unit does not recover merely because room returned** and needs a plain start, **so the two machine kinds differ here.** That is the observation from Batch E, 2026-08-18, and it stands.

**Found by the 2026-08-31 replay of *The server disk fills up during render*, which is where this clause is actually exercised.** The clause was written before the running-supervisor case was understood, and its neighbours were repaired around it on 2026-08-22 while it was not.

**Three qualifications the input document put on that observation on 2026-08-20, carried here rather than left behind. Added 2026-08-23; the trigger's answer is unchanged.** **What was actually run** is the ballast removed and the box watched three minutes at thirty-second intervals — `is-active=failed`, endpoint answering no, at every one — with a plain start command enough to recover it and nothing on the machine performing one. **The *stays failed across a reboot* clause was never posed:** the one reboot performed was with the disk **still full**, where the unit fails again for the same reason, and the three-minute watch after the space was freed was run **without** a reboot. **Nothing here claims it**, and V6's answer never needed it — what the trigger exists for is the machine that is up and whose unit is `failed`, which is the state that was observed. **The restart-count figure is not repeated anywhere in this document and must not be**: the ten-in-two-seconds reading does not match the documented default, and the input document's own instruction is that *"the raw record wants re-reading before that number is repeated anywhere."* **And the start limiter is not a property of systemd** — `StartLimitIntervalSec=` and `StartLimitBurst=` are unit settings — so *the two machine kinds differ here* is a difference between **the two arrangements as this project builds them**, not a general property of the two machine kinds, and a unit written with the limiter disabled would behave differently. Nothing below is derived from the figure or from the general form of the difference.

**The trigger can act in that condition** because the request rides on the **pre-package surface**, which reaches a machine whose supervisor is dead (B2), and because browsing, file operations, readings and transfers stay available while the supervisor is unavailable — so the user can free the space through Smoozy, and **the next command-path attempt after they do is what sends the repair**.

**V7. What the trigger does not do, said plainly.** It does not fix a full disk; the user frees the space, and ~~the disk-full carve-out `Whole app` grants is the diagnosis they are shown~~ ([16.4](#164-row-287-presented-the-disk-full-carve-out)). It does nothing with the application closed, because there is no daemon. And **it does not decide what the machine does in response** — placing, repairing or replacing, and starting a supervisor whose own restarter has given up, are `Rented-server runtime` §2's. That document's Section 16 item 12 names this trigger as what reaches the machine and deliberately does not answer what performs the start; **[Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 15 records that as the one place this answer needs another document to act, with what breaks.**

**NARROWED 2026-09-02, by the replay of *The server disk fills up during render*, and for the same reason V6 was narrowed: in the branch this clause describes, that promise has no producer.** Where the supervisor was down through the render's ending, **no free-space reading of the failing moment exists.** The ending was never recorded, so route (b)'s reading — which [M15a](#164-row-287-presented-the-disk-full-carve-out) fixes as taken **on the machine, in the same act that writes that render's recorded outcome** — was never taken with it. Reconciliation writes the ending after the user has freed the space, and a reading taken **then** is a reading against a mount that now has room; M15a forbids feeding route (b) with a reading taken when the ending is learned in any case. Route (a) wants a refused write of Smoozy's own, and that write no longer exists. **So the carve-out does not fire in this branch and the machine's own words stand alone** — which is exactly what [M17](#164-row-287-presented-the-disk-full-carve-out) already provides for on the answering document's F17, and what M15a already states from the other side: a recorded outcome carrying no such reading is not one carrying a zero, and nothing infers a full disk from the reading's absence. **Where the carve-out does fire is the branch it was written for** — a render whose supervisor stayed up and whose recorded ending therefore carries route (b)'s reading, or a write of Smoozy's own refused for space. **Nothing else in V7 moves**, and the trigger's own answer is unchanged.

**V8. Package inspection's answer is a reading and never durable state on this side.** It carries the moment it was taken and the clock it was taken on, and where it is remembered at all it carries the installation identity it was read under and is subject-held on the server row (B5). **Inspection compares content and never presence** (B4): the recycle observation is why — the boot hook *"was not deleted but replaced"* by an 82-byte provider default, so *"a check for 'does my hook exist' passes while the hook does nothing Smoozy asked for."*

**V9. A commit deferred because live or held work depends on the runtime is an ordinary result and not a failure** (B6). It does not expire a waiter and it does not license a second attempt under a fresh identity.

**V10. Where automatic repair fails, `Server Setup`'s user-facing re-run is the fallback** and this document offers no second automatic route. **A second automatic restarter would be a second restarter**, which is the arrangement `Rented-server runtime` §3.2 exists to have exactly one of.

**The update of a working, older program, which nothing did until V11. Added 2026-09-28, outside any round, out of step 10c of Build, per Row 519. Label: Creator-stated (2026-09-28): their rule for the application's own update — *"updating only happens with no running jobs"*, with the user able to override — carried across to servers, which is what they asked for when they asked *"How do they know this stuff has to be updated, too?"* No auditor of this document has seen V11 to V16.** Until these clauses the bootstrap-and-repair request was sent only for a supervisor that did not answer (V3) and by the user's own re-run (`Server Setup` F1), and F1 said outright that a program one protocol version behind triggers nothing. **So a fix that lives in the machine's program never reached a machine already set up**, and after two protocol changes the desktop called a running program unavailable.

**V11. At every primary connection on the confirmed branch, a machine whose active release is below the desktop's own is updated to the desktop's own release where nothing there is live, held or uncollected.** It is `Servers` Q1's last step (step 9), after the queue reading and after any remembered setup. **It also runs where the supervisor does not confirm because the program shares no protocol with this build and `Server protocol and transfers` P3b reads it *too old*, deciding *nothing live, held or uncollected* from P3b's own records reading**, a program that cannot be spoken to never confirming; **where that reading cannot answer, the machine is held as one with work on it** — V13 says so and V15 is unchanged. *(Added 2026-09-28 on the build runner's ruling, out of transfer wave piece 8; no auditor of this document has seen it.)* **The comparison is release sequences**: the machine's from package inspection (`Server protocol and transfers` B4), the desktop's own from its own verified release (B8d). **The work reading is the one already taken**: the queue reading of Q1 step 7 where the program can be spoken to, and otherwise the records reading G21 takes, through the surface (`Server protocol and transfers` P3b). **Where it shows nothing live, held, with an uncollected result or a result not acknowledged, the bootstrap-and-repair request is sent for the desktop's own release** — the same request, under the same claim (G1a, G1b) and the same commit, health exchange and rollback (`Rented-server runtime` G3 to G23), under G2's `automatic repair` route. **Where it shows work, nothing is sent and nothing is staged**; V13's condition stands instead. **G21 stays the gate**: a job accepted between the reading and the commit defers the commit on the machine exactly as it always did (G22).

**V12. A waiting update is tried again at each connection, and while connected, at the reading that shows the last of that machine's work ended.** That reading is `State, identity and recovery` F15's third moment — the queue reading a connected period already takes, P10d's sequence included — so **nothing polls, nothing is timed and nothing reaches out**. A deferred phase found on the machine is re-sent under its **recorded** operation identity, read from the phase record, and never a fresh one (B6, G17's *deferred* row). A machine whose work cannot be read live (*too old*, below) is tried again at each connection only. **One request is outstanding per server** (V4).

**V13. What the server carries while this is so, and each is a condition of its own and never V1's.** `Server protocol and transfers` P3b is why none of them is *the supervisor not answering*. Each is presented against the server, withdrawn when the condition ends, and worded by Design:

- **the update waits** — the machine is older, work stands on it, and that work can be controlled from this desktop;
- **too old, and its work cannot be controlled from here** — the protocol intervals do not overlap, work stands, and no retained adapter reaches it (P3a). Nothing is offered that would need one;
- **too new** — V14;
- **the update is under way** — from the request's send to its terminal phase, which is Row 291's *anything that isn't instant shows that it's underway*; the supervisor ended and started again inside G25 raises nothing of V1's while it stands;
- **the update failed** — the transaction ended *rolled back* or *failed before commit*. It is a failure record against the server, carrying the transaction's own words, and it ends when a later update lands. **The machine is on the release it had and goes on working where it could before.**

**V14. Nothing this desktop sends places a release below the machine's own.** V11, V3's repair and `Server Setup` F1 all refuse to send the request where the machine's active release sequence is above the desktop's own (B8d). **The machine is *too new*, and the way out is the desktop's own update** (Z23, Z26), which is what the user is told. Where the protocol intervals still overlap, the desktop goes on using the machine and says nothing, exactly as before; the condition is raised only where they do not, or where the supervisor does not answer and V3 would otherwise have replaced it.

**V15. The user may have a waiting update done anyway, by one act at the entry point Design draws, and is told first what it does. Z32's counterpart on the machine.** The act sends **`Queue & History` L13's stop-and-clear** — the request a removal already sends (`Servers` W18) — and runs V11 again once both outcomes are learned. **Nothing is stored**: the next time a newer release waits, it waits again. **What the user reads before the act is `Servers` W19a's statement, unchanged**: the waiting jobs are cleared, then the render is stopped; the frame being rendered may not arrive; the frames already written stay on the server. **Where the result is anything but *cleared and stopped*** (`Servers` W21), the update stays waiting and the result is said as W21 says it for a removal; nothing is retried on its own. **It is offered only where the update waits and the connection is live**; on *too old* the stop cannot reach the work, so it is not offered (V13).

**V16. What was weighed and not taken.** **Updating over live work and letting the new program take the waiting jobs over.** A render running under the old program would be ended by G25's ending, and its worker would report to a supervisor of another release, which is exactly the crossing P3 exists to prevent. **An override that stops only the render and keeps the line.** The admission point starts the next waiting job the moment the stop's outcome is written (`Queue & History` Q4), so the update would wait again; `Queue & History` L14 is the one act that empties the line and stops the render with no window between. **A second, lighter trigger for the update.** V11 reuses the repair's request and every rule under it, so the only new thing is when it is sent.

---

## 13. Notifications

**Constraint:** Row 284, quoted at the head of this document, with its three clauses; Fact 73, answered 2026-08-18; Fact 57 for the connection kind; and the bound Row 284's own spec text states — *"A notification therefore reaches the user while Smoozy is running behind their work, and never once they have quit it or shut the laptop. That gap is not closed here — it is what **Telegram** is for"*. **Nothing here tries to close that gap.**

### 13.1 What a plain downloaded binary must carry before it can post at all

**T1. A plain downloaded binary cannot post a notification on any of the three systems without extra machinery.** That is the clause most likely to break this row, it bites on all three, and in each case the machinery is ordinary. **It is a build output rather than run-time work**, which is why [Section 19](#19-build-outputs-signing-and-hosting) carries it.

**T2. macOS: a bundle identity, and the application ships as a bundle with an identifier.** Apple documents that precondition **nowhere** and it is decisive: an unbundled executable is killed by an uncaught exception on the **very first call, exit 134, with nothing to catch**; inside a bundle with an identifier it works; remove the identifier and it dies with a different exception. **An ad-hoc signature was enough** — no Developer ID and no notarization — so the precondition is the bundle, not the certificate. Reproduced exactly by the citation check, including the distinct failure paths for *no bundle* and *bundle with no identifier*. **The identity is necessary and not sufficient, and this is new rather than a correction — added 2026-09-09 at Build step 10a piece G, the first time anything in Smoozy posted.** The notification daemon validates the client, and a bundle it will not validate is refused with **no prompt, no post, and no error the application can act on**: the same ad-hoc-signed bundle with the same identifier was refused from a scratch path under `/private/tmp` and delivered from an ordinary one, by direct execution and by `open` alike, so the launch route was not the difference. **What holds:** T2's own claim is unchanged for an ordinarily installed application, and T5's shape already covers the refusal without raising a failure record — but **the application cannot tell that case from a refusal**, and item 11 has nothing about it.

**T3. Windows: a Start-menu shortcut carrying an application identifier, and not the runtime-package route.** Microsoft's words: *"Without a valid shortcut installed in the Start screen or in All Programs, you cannot raise a toast notification from a desktop app."* The recommended alternative depends on a runtime package an unpackaged app must deploy through its installer, and Microsoft's own page says this *"can compromise the simplicity of self-contained deployment"* and offers a runtime probe rather than a guarantee. **Smoozy is a downloaded binary, so the shortcut route is taken and the runtime package is declined.**

**T4. Two further Windows facts bind the same clause.** **An elevated app cannot post at all** — so **nothing in this application ever runs elevated**. **Corrected 2026-08-22**, out of the third sanity sweep's verification round: this clause used to add *"which is consistent with the whole of [Section 20](#20-update-verification-and-replacement), where no route asks for administrator"*, and that was false of Linux from the day [Z10](#203-linux-a-package-transaction) chose the package mechanism. **The distinction that saves the Windows argument is real and is the one to hold**: the application itself still never runs elevated anywhere. On Linux it **asks the platform's own package mechanism to perform a privileged transaction**, and the platform — not Smoozy — authenticates the user ([Z10a](#203-linux-a-package-transaction)). So no Smoozy process is ever elevated, which is what this clause needs, and Section 20's Linux third does ask the user for their computer password, which it did not admit. And **the post fails silently**, so **a Windows post is never treated as delivered** (T16).

**T5. Linux: no identity precondition at all, and the one thing Smoozy cannot fix.** The freedesktop Desktop Notifications Specification v1.3 requires no identity — the application name *"can be blank"* and the desktop entry is only a hint — but it needs a **session message bus and a process owning the notification name**, and the specification says outright that clients *"must not assume that it is available."*

**The specification's address and date read were recorded nowhere in `Preparation/` — the input document names it by title and version alone — and are supplied 2026-08-23: `https://specifications.freedesktop.org/notification-spec/latest/`, publication date 18 August 2024, Version 1.3, read 2026-08-23.** It is a multi-page document and the three readings sit on three of its pages; all three stand word for word. `app_name` — *"The optional name of the application sending the notification. Can be blank."* (`protocol.html`, `org.freedesktop.Notifications.Notify`). `desktop-entry` — one entry in the hints table, *"This specifies the name of the desktop filename representing the calling program."* (`hints.html`). And the availability sentence in full (`basic-design.html`), which is also where the session-bus precondition comes from: *"take the org.freedesktop.Notifications service on the session bus … It can optionally be activated automatically by the bus process, however this is not required and notification server clients must not assume that it is available."* **The same publication serves T16, T24 — whose Linux atomicity is `protocol.html`'s `replaces_id`, *"The server must atomically (ie with no flicker or other visual cues) replace the given notification with this one"* — and [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 12.** **So on a computer with no notification service the four kinds do not arrive, and that is written as a case rather than as a failure**: no failure record is raised, because a failure record about the user's own desktop is not a fact about Smoozy, and the four kinds' in-app records are unaffected. **[Section 22](#22-unresolved) carries it with what breaks.**

### 13.2 Permission, and what a refusal is

**T6. Three different models rather than one, and the adapter holds three.** macOS: the app **must request authorization**, the system draws the prompt, **a denial is silent** — the system *"ignores any attempts to schedule local notifications"* — and the state is **readable back**. Windows: **no prompt and no request interface found**, only a read-only setting whose every non-enabled value is a way of being switched off. Linux: **no permission model in the specification at all**, and therefore no denied state to read.

**T7. The macOS request is made at the first moment a notification would actually be posted, and never at application start.** At start nothing is happening and nothing is being told, so a prompt there is a question about nothing. **This is the same shape as the Linux store probe of S5 and for the same reason**, and it keeps the prompt attached to the event that needs it.

**T8. A denied state is read back where the system offers one, and it is treated exactly as T5's Linux case**: the kinds do not arrive, no failure record is raised, and nothing else changes. **The per-kind switches in Settings remain the user's own record of what they want**, whether or not the system will deliver any of it; nothing here adds a notice about it, and whether Design offers one is Design's.

### 13.3 The four kinds, and what composes each

**T9. The four kinds are the spec's and no fifth is added.** A job finishing; a job failing or being given up; a connection Smoozy opened and could not restore; a machine that has had nothing to do for half an hour, which `Queue & History` owns and states in full.

**T10. A job the user stopped is deliberately not among the four**, so a job whose recorded outcome is *left stopped* posts nothing.

**T11. A batch of scene-jobs speaks once, and the state that knows when the last one ended is already there.** A batch is the **group identity** [§2.2](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#22-the-register-of-identities) issues when an all-scenes submission is composed, and every member job's local record carries it. **So *have all of this group's jobs reached a recorded ending* is answered from the job records this desktop already holds** — no group record, no count kept beside them, and nothing read from a machine. **The finishing notification for a group is posted once, when the last member reaches a recorded ending, and no member posts its own.**

**T12. A job that fails still speaks for itself**, group or not, at the moment its failure is learned. That is the spec's own split and it is what stops a batch's one message hiding a failure inside it.

**T27. The group's finishing notification needs a member that finished, and *left stopped* still counts towards the last member.** Two things follow from T11 that T11 does not say, and both are decided here. **The finishing notification is posted when the last member reaches a recorded ending *and at least one member finished*** — a recorded ending is the class that includes failed and left-stopped, so **a group all of whose members failed or were given up posts no finishing notification at all**, its members' own T12 posts being the whole of what is said. Without that, an all-scenes submission whose five members all fail would post five failure notifications and then tell the user, over T15's picture, that the batch finished, for a batch in which nothing finished. And **a member whose recorded outcome is *left stopped* satisfies the last-member test**: T10 removes that member's **own** post and says nothing about the group's completion, and the spec's *"when the whole batch is done"* is not served by a batch that is done and silent.

**T13. The connection kind is raised from the condition record and not from a reading.** `Servers` supplies the observed value under [§8.5](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#85-the-connection-condition-needs-a-break-reported-without-using-the-connection)'s constraint — a break reported **without the caller having to use the connection first** — and Fact 57 is why that constraint exists: a caller's own line pulled went **undetected for 21 minutes 45 seconds** with keepalives off. **The notification is posted when the condition is raised and not when a request happens to fail.**

**T14. The idle kind is `Queue & History`'s reading and its half hour**, and nothing here re-derives either.

### 13.4 The frame preview, and it is three answers

**T15. What is attached is the fetched derivative already on the user's computer, and nothing is made here.** `Job files and outputs` settles that **every viewable derivative is produced once on the rented machine** and that a fetched derivative is a **viewing-format picture**; its B12 fixes that a viewing format is **PNG or JPEG**; and [§11.3](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#113-the-two-rulings-those-documents-were-promised) makes the fetched copy **subject-held on its job record**, so it is a file on disk for as long as that record exists. **That closes the one decision left open under Fact 71 — where a frame preview's small copy is made — without re-opening where derivatives are made: it is not made on the user's computer at all.**

**T16. The three systems take it three different ways, and one of them is unresolved.**

- **macOS: an attachment, and it must be a file on disk** — **JPEG, GIF or PNG only, 10 MB maximum**, with **no in-memory supply**. The fetched derivative is already a file and already PNG or JPEG, so the format holds by construction; **the size is checked against the ceiling at the moment of posting** and the notification is posted **without a picture** where it does not fit.
- **Linux: pixels supplied in memory**, which is the only route the specification documents, with no stated size limit — **but there is no capability string for it**, so a client cannot ask in advance whether the server will render it and hints may be ignored silently. **So the picture may simply not appear and nothing detects that it did not.** **The route is named here and is not built. Added 2026-09-12, Build step 10a round 1 upstream must-do U-2.** Supplying pixels in memory means decoding the fetched derivative, which `Job files and outputs` B12 admits as **PNG or JPEG**, so it wants a decoder of each declared for the Linux artifact and neither is in that tree. **The weight would be spent on an artifact nobody on this project can observe**, for the reason in the sentence above and in [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 13: on that system nothing can detect that the picture did not arrive. **So on Linux the picture never goes**, and that is a fact about Smoozy rather than about the system — [T17a](#134-the-frame-preview-and-it-is-three-answers)'s fifth entry. **What closes it is a picture decoder of each of the two kinds declared for the Linux artifact**; until then the notification still says the job finished, which is the kind's own content.
- **Windows: two Microsoft pages disagree and neither acknowledges the other**, one listing three image sources of which two need a package identity an unpackaged app lacks, another documenting a local file path *"Supported only for desktop apps"*. **The contradiction is inherited unresolved.** The local-file route is what would be attempted, and **the notification still says the job finished with no picture where it does not work**.

**T17. A finished-job notification never waits on a fetch.** Where the derivative is not yet on disk at the moment of posting, the notification is posted **without a picture** rather than delayed, suppressed, or re-posted later. The spec's own wording is *"where the job has one"*.

**T17a. What the finished-job switch may claim about the picture is bounded here, and *it carries the frame* is more than this document will promise. Added 2026-08-21, per Row 332.** [Design](../Design/Settings.md) has that switch *"says it carries the frame"* — a claim made in the interface, at the one moment the user decides whether to keep the kind switched on.

- **The picture is a common case rather than a property, and it is absent five ways that are none of them faults.** *(Four until 2026-09-12, when Build step 10a round 1's upstream must-do U-2 added the fifth. The first four are all facts about a system; the fifth is a fact about Smoozy, and without it this closed list and the application disagreed about what Smoozy does on one of the three systems.)* **macOS:** the fetched derivative exceeds the **10 MB** attachment ceiling and the notification is posted without it, and **nothing establishes that a derivative is by construction inside that ceiling** (T16, [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 25). **Linux:** there is no capability string, so it may be dropped and nothing detects that it was (T16, item 13). **Windows:** two Microsoft pages disagree, the local-file route is what is attempted, and the contradiction is inherited unresolved (T16, item 13). **On all three:** the derivative is not yet on disk at the moment of posting, where [T17](#134-the-frame-preview-and-it-is-three-answers) refuses to delay, suppress or re-post. **And on Linux the route is not built at all** — T16's Linux third names it and says why, item 13 being the reason nothing on that system could observe what building it bought.
- **So a label may say what the kind is and not what it carries.** The kind's own content is that the job finished, which T16 keeps in every one of those cases; the picture is either stated as the spec states it — *"where the job has one"* — or not stated at all. **A flat claim is wrong in ordinary use rather than in an edge case**, which is what makes this a bound rather than a caution.
- **The same bound reaches the switch's other implied claim.** A switch governs whether Smoozy **attempts** the post and never whether one arrives: delivery is never confirmed (T23), a Windows post fails silently (T4), a computer with no notification service gets none of the four (T5), and a job ending inside any reconnection window posts nothing at all (item 26).
- **What is left to Design is the wording of an on-and-off switch**, which [Section 24](#24-what-this-document-does-not-own) already leaves it as *"how the per-kind switches are offered"* — bounded by this clause and by nothing else. **The design sentence is a drafted one and is parked rather than repaired here**, this stage not owning that document. **[Method](Method.md)'s third escalation, checked and declined**: the answer is a bound on a label's wording — nothing new is put on a screen, there is no place to look, nothing to type, and nothing that can be lost.

### 13.5 Telling now from catching up, without a clock

**T18. The boundary is the desktop's own reconciliation read, and not an age.** Events applied at [§7](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#7-reconciliation-on-the-desktop) step 3 — read forward from the cursor on a connection — **are the catch-up account**, and they produce `Queue & History`'s one catch-up notice and **no notifications**. Events that arrive **after** that step has completed for that server, while the connected period is live, are *now* and produce notifications under the four kinds. **What makes an event arrive then is [3.3](#33-ui-event-delivery) P10d's live-reading sequence** — its recurring read-forward is the only *now* route, there being no push (`Rented-server runtime` U26); without it every ending would be catch-up and this boundary would classify nothing.

**T18a. An ending learned from a landing of pending writes is classified by when its events arrive, not by when it happened. Added 2026-09-02, scenario replay *The server disk fills up during render* finding N-f.** Where a machine's mount fills, `Rented-server runtime` S14 holds the ending's journal events **pending** — the write is refused and re-attempted — so at the moment the job fails **there is no event to read forward** and T18's boundary has nothing to classify. **The landing is not a second route and gets no rule of its own.** When those writes land, the events they carry are read forward from the cursor like any others and the same boundary decides: **arriving while the connected period is live, after [§7](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#7-reconciliation-on-the-desktop) step 3 has completed for that server, they are *now* and the failed job speaks for itself under [T12](#133-the-four-kinds-and-what-composes-each)**; arriving at a later reconciliation's step 3 they are **catch-up**, producing `Queue & History`'s one catch-up notice and no notification. **No clock is involved and none is needed** — the event's own moment on the machine may be hours older than its arrival and decides nothing here, which is T19 applied to a case T19 was not written for.

- **What it costs, stated rather than left to be found.** A user who quits Smoozy before the space is freed gets **no notification of that ending at all**, because the landing then arrives as catch-up. That is T18's ordinary consequence rather than a new one, and [T22](#136-a-notification-is-never-relied-on) is what makes it survivable: the ending's own durable record is what the user meets, and the notification's promise was reaching a user looking elsewhere.
- **Nothing here is advanced by the landing.** The events are applied at step 3 as usual, the cursor moves as usual, and a notification that does fire is attempted and forgotten (T23). **Item 26 is untouched**: a job ending inside a reconnection window still posts nothing, and this clause is about a live connected period rather than a broken one.

**T19. No clock is involved, deliberately.** D5 forbids subtracting readings taken on different clocks; **the rented machine's clock stability over the length of a render is Fact 92, answered 2026-08-18 with a qualifier that leaves the reasoning here unchanged — the one clock watched across a real 25-minute render held a 0.0129 ms band with nothing disciplining it, and a container has no clock of its own** *(this clause said Fact 92 was open; corrected in place 2026-08-19, no conclusion of T19 changed)*; and the user's computer's clock is undefended, Fact 13 being negative on the half that matters. **An age comparison would be arithmetic across two clocks neither of which is trusted.** The reader's own cursor is a record Smoozy wrote, and that is what the boundary is made of.

**T20. Two consequences, both correct rather than tolerated.** An event that happened seconds before a connection completed step 3 is still **catch-up**, and the notice names it, so the user is not told twice. And a **reconnection after a drop re-runs step 3**, so everything that happened during the outage is catch-up — which is the burst Row 284 exists to forbid.

**T21. Nothing about presentation is written into the journal.** E7 forbids it and gives the reason: a single flag cannot serve two independent readers, and an event the Telegram worker presented would read as presented to the desktop. **What is supplied to the presenter is the event moment and the cursor, and the decision is made here.**

### 13.6 A notification is never relied on

**T22. Nothing in the application is advanced, cleared, acknowledged or recorded by a notification being posted.** No cursor moves, no failure record clears, no outcome record changes. **Every one of the four kinds also leaves its own durable in-app record** — a job's ending in its history record, a failure record on the thing that failed, the catch-up notice, `Queue & History`'s idle reading — so the promise the notification serves is *reaching a user who is looking elsewhere*, and losing one costs the reaching and nothing else.

**T23. Delivery is never confirmed.** On Windows the post **fails silently**; on Linux the picture may be dropped with no signal; on macOS nothing was ever posted, so even the success path is unobserved. **A post is therefore attempted and forgotten**, and no state depends on the attempt's result.

**T24. Replace-by-identity is the mechanism a repeating fact is said once with**, and it works on all three and is **atomic on Linux by specification**. [Section 14](#14-one-repeating-failure-is-said-once) is what uses it.

**T24a. A posted notification raised from a condition record is taken down when that record is withdrawn. Added 2026-09-01, scenario replay *The provider reboots the machine* finding 2.** The removal is attempted **on the same identity [Q10](#14-one-repeating-failure-is-said-once) replaces on**, at the moment [16.2](#162-row-285-presented-a-live-condition-and-a-recorded-ending) M4's value change withdraws the in-app record, and it is **attempted and forgotten exactly as a post is ([T23](#136-a-notification-is-never-relied-on))** — nothing waits on it, nothing records it, and nothing is advanced or cleared by it succeeding or failing.

- **It reaches the connection kind and any later kind raised from a condition record, and nothing else.** The other three of the four are endings, which [Q3](#14-one-repeating-failure-is-said-once) already keeps out of this machinery: an ending happened once and has nothing to withdraw.
- **The requirement is the spec's rather than this document's invention.** `Whole app` ends the message on *"the condition the message describes plainly ending — the connection restored, however it came back"*, and gives the reason in the same breath: *"Leaving it would have Smoozy saying the connection is down on a server that reads as connected."* Until this clause, [Section 13](#13-notifications), [Section 14](#14-one-repeating-failure-is-said-once) and [Section 16](#16-progress-and-failure-presentation-plumbing) applied that promise to the in-app surface alone and left it unapplied on the one surface the four kinds exist to reach.
- **What made it certain rather than occasional is `Servers` N2a**, which raises the condition at 105 seconds after the declaration: every provider reboot longer than about two and a half minutes now posts, and this scenario's post fires about twenty-five seconds before the machine comes back.
- **M4's in-app withdrawal is what carries the promise, and this removal is the courtesy.** [Q12](#14-one-repeating-failure-is-said-once) governs what happens afterwards unchanged — a condition that returns is a fresh identity against the same key and is said once again.
- **The mechanism's availability is not established on any of the three systems** and this clause does not assume it: [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 61 states what breaks where the removal cannot be made.

**T24b. The trigger is every ending of the record, not the withdrawal alone. Added 2026-09-02, scenario replay *The machine is destroyed* finding 2.** T24a fires at [16.2](#162-row-285-presented-a-live-condition-and-a-recorded-ending) M4's value change, and M4 defines withdrawal as one thing — the observed value moving outside the set the record was raised over. **[State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#8-failure-records--a-live-condition-and-a-recorded-ending) F25 gives the record three endings**, and the other two end it with the observed fact never having moved: **its subject is removed**, and **the user clears the record themselves**. On a machine that is destroyed the value never leaves the set, so T24a's trigger cannot fire and the record ends at the removal of the server row — leaving a posted *could not be restored* naming a server that no longer exists in Smoozy, on a surface Smoozy has already stopped owning.

- **So the removal is attempted at whichever of F25's three endings ends the record**, on the same identity [Q10](#14-one-repeating-failure-is-said-once) replaces on, and **attempted and forgotten on exactly the terms T24a already sets** ([T23](#136-a-notification-is-never-relied-on)) — nothing waits on it, nothing records it, nothing is advanced or cleared by it either way.
- **Nothing else about T24a moves.** The kinds it reaches are unchanged — the connection kind and any later kind raised from a condition record, and never the three endings [Q3](#14-one-repeating-failure-is-said-once) keeps out. [Q12](#14-one-repeating-failure-is-said-once) still governs what happens afterwards: a condition that returns is a fresh identity against the same key and is said once again.
- **The subject-removed ending needs no new knowledge here.** F25 puts that ending in the removing act itself and [§11.1](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#111-five-classes-and-the-reason-there-is-no-sixth)'s subject-held class carries it, so this clause adds a call at a moment that already exists and adds no record, no reading and no reach to a machine.
- **[Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 61 covers this the same way** — where the removal cannot be made on a given system, the post stays and nothing else changes.

**T25. Nothing is assumed to be coalesced for Smoozy.** **No rate limit or coalescing is documented on any of the three**, so every suppression the application needs is its own.

**T26. Grouping is documented on macOS and Windows and absent on Linux**, and nothing here rests on it: T11's *speaks once* is composed from Smoozy's own records rather than from a platform grouping facility, which is what makes it hold on all three.

### 13.7 What clicking a notification does

**Constraint:** Row 480 — *"Clicking brings Smoozy forward and goes to the thing it is about — a job's entry, a server's row"*, settled at Design (2026-08-23) — and the spec bound already at this section's head: a notification reaches the user *"while Smoozy is running behind their work, and never once they have quit it"*. **Fact 138's answer of record is [The three desktop systems read again](The%20three%20desktop%20systems%20read%20again%20—%20dying,%20revealing,%20resolving,%20and%20what%20the%20shell%20does%20not%20expose.md) §7.** Added 2026-08-25 at the tenth sitting.

**T28. The activation reaches a running application on all three systems, and it is platform code of this document's own, beside the shell.** *(**Corrected 2026-09-09 at Build step 10a piece G.** This read *"and the shell exposes it in its current released version"*, which was a reading of Wails taken on 2026-08-25 at the input document §7 and carried onto Tauri when the shell changed on 2026-09-02 without being re-derived — the same fault §19 E4 already records against itself. The shell publishes a notification plugin whose desktop half cannot keep T6, T7, T24, T24a or T29, so the activation route is this document's own platform code, exactly as K20 already says of the folder-reveal. **Nothing else in T28 moves**: the per-system mechanisms below are each vendor's own publication and stand as written.)* *(Documented, the input document §7; nothing was posted on any system — [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 11 — so even the success path is unobserved.)* Per system: **macOS** calls a notification-centre delegate with a response object, the one hard timing requirement being that the delegate is assigned **before the application finishes launching** — the shell installs one and refuses to initialise without a bundle identifier, which T2's bundle already supplies; **Windows** activates the registered component server the shell itself registers at startup, per-user and unelevated, consistent with T3 and T4; **Linux** emits an action-invoked signal on the session bus to the client that posted, and the shell **always prepends the default action pair**, so a plain body click comes back as an action exactly as the specification's reserved name describes. **A bare click on the notification body is itself an action on all three**, normalised by the shell to one identifier.

**T29. What travels with the post is the durable subject, and the destination is the in-app record T22 already guarantees.** Each of the four kinds posts carrying, in the notification's own carried data, the identities of what it is about and nothing more — the job identity or group identity for the two job kinds, the server row for the connection kind and the idle kind — **identities only, never state, never a path and never a message re-derived at click time**. On activation the application brings its window forward and navigates to that subject's own record: the job's history entry, the server's row. **The destination exists whatever became of the notification**, because every kind also leaves its own durable in-app record (T22) — which is what makes this a routing question rather than a storage one. T22 and T23 stand untouched: nothing is advanced, cleared, acknowledged or recorded by the activation, and no state depends on it — **a subject that is gone by the time of the click gets the bringing-forward and nothing else**, no error and no invented screen, because the click's promise is the going and a gone subject has nowhere to go.

**T30. The width is the running application, and the promise needs nothing more.** The spec's own bound is that a notification reaches the user while Smoozy runs behind their work and never once they have quit it, so **nothing here is built on a cold start**. What the systems offer beyond that is carried at its true width *(the input document §7)*: **macOS documents delivery to an exited application** — the system launches it in the background and calls the delegate — and since the shell assigns its delegate during launch, such an activation is met as an ordinary launch and routed under T29; **Windows** has the mechanism by publication, but the shell's own path from a click on a closed application to its callback **is established by no publication** and is treated as unproven; **Linux is undefined by publication** — the specification describes a signal to a connected client and nothing else, so a click is assumed to reach a running application and nothing else. [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 51 carries the residue. **[Method](Method.md)'s third escalation, checked and declined**: the click adds nothing — it goes where the user would have had to go anyway, which is the row's own sentence.

---

## 14. One repeating failure is said once

**Constraint:** Row 288, quoted at the head of this document. **The key is supplied by [State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#89-the-repeat-key-supplied-rather-than-applied) §8.9 and the application is this document's**; that section answers nothing of this row and this section invents no second key.

**Q1. The presentation key is the condition identity, everywhere a failure is presented.** Two failures share a reason when they share a condition identity — *"not when they share their words, and not when they share the unit that met them."* **That is the whole of the application**: keying presentation on the identity rather than on the event is what makes *said once* fall out instead of being enforced.

**Q2. The floor is drawn where §8.9 draws it and this section moves it in neither direction.** *"A hundred files refused by one full mount on one machine are one condition on one subject"* — so one presentation. *"The same words from two servers are two conditions, because the subject differs"* — so two presentations. *"A disk-full and a permission refusal on the same mount are two, because the kind differs"* — so two. **Getting it wrong is not cosmetic in either direction:** a hundred messages buries the one that matters, and a suppression drawn too wide silences a second, unrelated failure and breaks the promise that Smoozy always tells the truth about what happened.

**Q3. Row 288 applies to condition records and not to endings.** A record of kind *ending* names a subject outcome that happened once ([§8.1](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#81-two-kinds-and-the-kind-is-declared)); there is one per subject and there is nothing to repeat. **The row's own example is a condition** — every file in a job failing to land in a folder that is full — and the mechanism follows the example rather than being widened past it.

### 14.1 The presentation key is the condition identity

**Q4. There is already only one record.** [§8.3](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#83-the-condition-identity-and-who-supplies-each-observed-value) composes the condition key first and looks it up: where the key already has an identity, **that identity is used, no second record is raised**, and the existing record is the one there is to withdraw. **So the suppression this row asks for is not about the record at all** — it is about how many times that one record is put in front of the user.

**Q5. And the key already normalises the thing that would otherwise split one condition into many.** A condition key carries *"the location it was read against, in one normalised form"*, and `Server protocol and transfers` F11 says what that means in the case this row names: a pool of channels refused on one full mount resolves to **one** condition key, because the key carries the **mount** the path resolves to rather than the path. **Without that, *said once* would be unimplementable at the only layer that can implement it.**

### 14.2 What is not suppressed

**Q6. Unit outcomes are never suppressed.** [§9.4](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#94-the-outcome-record)'s outcome record holds **each unit's outcome, written durably at the moment it becomes true**, and the action's own outcome is derived from that list. **Saying one thing once never reduces the count**: a hundred files that failed are a hundred failed units and the user is told the count, which is Row 292's *"it does not stay quiet about the piece that failed"*. **What is said once is the reason, not the tally.**

**Q7. Nothing suppressed here is ever a second, unrelated failure**, because a different subject or a different kind is a different condition identity by construction and never resolves to the same one.

**Q8. Whether the arrangement that keeps hitting the condition also switches itself off is the owning module's**, as the row itself says. **Nothing here switches anything off**, and in particular an auto-download arrangement *"keeps trying, and never switches itself off"* (Row 219) whatever this section suppresses.

### 14.3 What said once means on each surface

**Q9. In the interface: one entry per condition identity**, against the durable subject the record names, for as long as the record stands. A later failure resolving to the same identity changes nothing on screen.

**Q10. In the operating system's notifications: one post per condition identity, and a later failure replaces it rather than adding one.** **Replace-by-identity is what carries this**, and it is documented on all three systems and **atomic on Linux by specification** — which is exactly the property that lets one repeating fact be said once rather than said once and then said again.

**Q11. Nothing is assumed coalesced by the operating system** (T25), so Q10 is the whole of the suppression and there is no second layer behind it.

**Q12. A withdrawn record is spent and is never presented again**, whatever the value does afterwards ([§8.2](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#82-a-condition-record-is-a-derivation-never-a-copy)). So a condition that returns is a **fresh identity against the same key** and is said once again, which is correct: the reason for keeping the first was served when it was withdrawn.

**Q13. A later failure resolving to a standing identity needs no post at all.** Q4 already refuses to raise a second record and Q9 already has the entry on screen, so **the repeat's correct treatment on the notification surface is silence**, and nothing about Row 288 depends on what the operating system does with a replacement. **Replace-by-identity is therefore the guarantee that any post which does happen adds no second, rather than the thing that makes *said once* true.** That matters because the property Q10 leans on is unestablished on every system ([Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 27): written this way the answer holds whichever way that fact closes, and it takes nothing away from Q10's mechanism, which still carries the posts that are made — a first raise, and a fresh identity after a withdrawal under Q12.

---

## 15. One control restores every suppressed warning

**Constraint:** Row 290, quoted at the head of this document, and [State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#103-considered-and-not-filed) §10.3 — *"Every suppression is one row in one durable table, carrying the module and the warning identity, and the single control of Row 290 is one delete over that table. … Row 290 is `Desktop app and delivery`'s; what is supplied here is the one table."*

### 15.1 There is nowhere else to put one

**G1. The answer is the rule that nothing else may hold a suppression, and it is kept structurally rather than by remembering it.** A2 gives no feature module a durable store of its own; there is **one** local record and this document builds it; the suppression table is the only row in [§1.1](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#11-on-the-users-computer)'s inventory that holds a suppressed warning. **So a module that wanted to remember its own in its own way has nowhere to write it.**

**G2. That matters because the failure is invisible.** A module remembering its own satisfies its own spec and breaks this one, and **nobody finds out until a user presses the control and one warning does not come back**. A rule a builder must remember would be discovered at that moment; a store that does not exist is discovered at the first attempt to use it.

**G3. It binds documents that have not been written yet.** Every dismissible warning in every module, present and future, records its suppression here. **A module document that does not declare its warnings' identities has not finished** (A7).

### 15.2 The warning identity

**G4. A row is the module and the warning identity, and nothing else.** No wording, no timestamp, no count, no per-subject qualifier — a suppression is *the user switched this warning off*, and a row carrying anything more would be a suppression two rows could disagree about.

**G5. The identity is declared in the owning module's own document and is stable for the life of the application.** A changed identity is either a warning that silently comes back for a user who switched it off, or a row that names nothing; **both are the invisible failure of G2 arriving by a different door.**

**G6. No migration ever rewrites or drops one** (L11). A retired warning's row is left where it is: it names nothing, it costs one row, and removing it is the one act that could take a live suppression with it by mistake.

### 15.3 The control is one delete, and it is atomic

**G7. The control is one delete over the table and it walks no modules.** It does not ask each module to restore its own, it does not enumerate warnings, and it does not need to know which modules exist. **A warning belonging to a module that is no longer in the application is restored by the same act as every other**, because the act is over the table.

**G8. Its partition unit is the whole table.** [§9.2](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#92-three-tests-applied-in-order-and-the-answer-is-declared-rather-than-inferred) test 1: the smallest thing the spec promises an outcome about is *every warning the user has switched off, all at once*, so the action is **atomic entire** — it restores all of them or it leaves the table as it was, and a partly-restored table is never a state the user can be left in.

**G9. Suppressions are user-held** ([§11.1](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#111-five-classes-and-the-reason-there-is-no-sixth)): they end when the user removes them and never otherwise. **No age, no cap, no sweep**, and nothing here restores a warning because time passed.

**G10. Nothing needs a tombstone.** §10.3 already ruled it: nothing is being undone by a later reading, so a restored warning stays restored because there is no row left to find.

---

## 16. Progress and failure presentation plumbing

**Constraint:** the scope line's *"progress and failure presentation plumbing"*; Rows 285, 286, 287 and 291, each presented here and answered elsewhere; and the shape's own sentence that this document *"presents atomic and partial outcomes and does not define that boundary."*

### 16.1 What presenting means here

**M1. Presentation reads records and never sources.** Every surface below is drawn from a durable record another document defines — a failure record, an outcome record, a capture record, a transfer record, an event read forward from the cursor. **Nothing on a presentation path opens a connection, takes a reading, or reaches out to a machine**, which is [§8.4](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#84-nothing-is-polled-to-withdraw-a-record)'s rule and P9's.

**M2. A record is kept against its durable subject and shown on whatever surface is in front of the user.** [§8.7](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#87-every-failure-record-names-a-durable-subject) is explicit: *"The record is kept against the durable subject; the volatile surface is a place it is shown."* **So closing a panel, changing screens, or the application run ending takes away a place a record is shown and never the record.**

**M3. Nothing is presented as finished before its outcome is durable** (P7), and nothing is presented as a whole account where a gap is known — a connection that cannot complete [§7](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#7-reconciliation-on-the-desktop) step 1 or step 3 **says so rather than showing a partial account as a whole one**, and E5's gap is stated rather than smoothed.

**M3a. Every reading presented anywhere is presented as of the moment it was taken, on the clock it was taken on. Added 2026-09-02, scenario replay *The server disk fills up during render* finding N-l.** **Placement is fixed here rather than at 16.5, which the replay marked tentative**, because the rule binds every subsection below and not only the one Row 291 answers. It already stood in three places and nowhere generally — [M15](#164-row-287-presented-the-disk-full-carve-out) for the disk-full free-space reading, [M28](#167-row-347-presented-a-repair-that-says-when-it-next-tries) and [M31](#167-row-347-presented-a-repair-that-says-when-it-next-tries) for a repairing server's last-known work reading, and [F5](#61-the-one-rule-the-whole-adapter-is-built-on) for the local side, in that clause's own words: *"presented as of the moment it was taken and never re-read to keep a message fresh."* **What was missing is the general case**, and it is not a rare one: a live connection whose worker has died declares no break, so `Servers` raises nothing and `Queue & History` C7's not-updating mark is never keyed, and a figure sits on screen beside an honest state with nothing saying it is frozen.

- **What it requires.** Every reading this section hands to a surface carries its own moment and the clock that moment is on, and the surface presents it **as of that moment**. A reading is never re-drawn as current because it is still on screen, and it is never aged into a newer figure.
- **What it does not require, because M1 and P9 already forbid it.** Nothing takes a fresh reading to keep a figure current, nothing polls to discover that readings have stopped arriving, and no watcher is added anywhere. **This fixes what a presented reading claims, not how often it is refreshed** — which is why it costs no reading of its own.
- **It composes nothing.** Where two readings sit beside each other with different moments, both moments stand; M31's refusal of a third reading composed from two is the same rule and is not widened here.
- **The words, the format of the moment and whether it is drawn at all are Design's** ([Section 24](#24-what-this-document-does-not-own)); what is fixed here is that a moment exists for every reading and that nothing may present the reading without it. **[Method](Method.md)'s third escalation, checked and declined**: the moment is already carried with every reading this section draws, no new place to look is added, nothing is typed, and nothing can be lost.

### 16.2 Row 285 presented: a live condition and a recorded ending

**M4. Every failure record carries its kind and the presentation reads it rather than guessing.** A **condition** record is shown *while, and only while,* the condition's current observed value is one of the values it was raised over; when the value moves outside that set the record is **withdrawn by that change alone** and is never shown again. An **ending** record is the mark raised against an immutable subject outcome; **it carries no clearing gesture and goes with its subject** ([§8.8](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#88-when-a-failure-record-ends)). *(Changed 2026-09-19 from a code round — the Gemini catch-up of step 10a, Area 4, upstream must-do U-1. No auditor of this document has seen it. This sentence called the ending record *"the clearable mark"*; [Whole app](../Specs/Whole%20app.md) grants clearing a failure *"only where it describes something still true"*, and an ending does not.)*

**M5. Clearing takes the mark and never the fact, and it reaches a condition record alone.** The user's own clearing gesture is Design's by that concern's own marker and this document presents it; what it does is fixed by §8.8 — **it ends the record and nothing else**, and an immutable subject outcome is not the user's to clear. **An ending's mark carries no clearing gesture**: it goes with its subject, so a failed job's entry says the job failed for as long as the entry stands. *(Changed 2026-09-19 from a code round — the Gemini catch-up of step 10a, Area 4, upstream must-do U-1. No auditor of this document has seen it. This read *"A user who clears a failed job's mark still has an entry that says the job failed"*.)*

**M6. Nothing here holds a second copy of a condition's current value.** The record is a derivation; presenting a copy is what would let the mark and the world drift apart, which is the harm [Whole app](../Specs/Whole%20app.md) names — *"the app being wrong on screen about its own state, which is never past the line."*

**M7. The narrowing that follows is carried and not hidden.** Where nothing has refreshed a condition, the record stands until the user clears it. That is §13 item 8's declared narrowing, it is the ordinary route that concern already grants, and **nothing here adds a watcher to close it.**

**M7a. Two conditions standing at the same moment are presented as two, whatever set them off.** A connection condition on one server and a folder-write condition on a transfer are each recorded against their own durable subject, shown and withdrawn independently under M4. Neither is hidden or reworded because the other stands. No statement about a shared cause is composed: no supplier reports one and no withdrawal column supports one. This is Q7’s different-subject or different-kind condition identity, M31’s refusal of a third reading and M6’s prohibition on state no record carries. Notifications remain limited to T9’s four kinds.

### 16.3 Row 286 presented: the plain sentence and the machine's own words

**M8. The plain sentence is first and the raw record is behind it, never the first thing in front of the user.** Both streams are `Rented-server runtime`'s: **two, kept apart, unedited, retained, and readable with nobody connected.**

**M9. Nothing here edits either stream.** Not a redaction, not a filter, not a prefix, not a trim — M8 of that document, and it is what [17.3](#173-what-it-must-never-touch) exempts the raw record from the typography point for. **The launch material's marker is part of the raw record and is not an edit of it.**

**M10. An empty panel is never what the user meets, and the four outcomes are what makes that possible.** M13's closed set is *collected*; *collected, and the stream was empty*; *collection incomplete*; *never established* — **one per stream per attempt**. **Each is presented as its own sentence and none of them is an empty panel**:

- *collected* — the record is there to open.
- *collected, and the stream was empty* — the plain sentence says the render printed nothing **on that stream** *(narrowed 2026-10-11, the Creator's eighth notes, note 6: it said there is nothing further to read, which speaks for the other stream; M13's corrected premise is why, and the spec's figure quoted next is narrowed the same day to the complaints stream alone)*. **On the complaints stream that is the spec's own *"it complained about nothing"*, and the two wordings are one claim worded to the stream it is about** *(reworded 2026-08-29, per Row 504, which found this bullet and `Preparation/Specs/Queue & History.md` wording the same act two ways. The bullet was per stream already — M13's set is one outcome per stream per attempt — and what made the old generic wording read as a claim about the render is the spec's narrowing of 2026-08-25, which established that on the out-of-memory ending there are words on the other stream.)* **It asserts nothing about the other stream**, which on that ending carries the render's own lines up to a sampling line — `Preparation/Specs/Queue & History.md`'s own account, labelled **observed** there on one rented Linux machine, and not re-derived here.
- *collection incomplete* — the plain sentence says what was collected is not the whole of what was printed. **It never collapses into *printed nothing***.
- *never established* — the plain sentence says nothing was collected, which is a different sentence from the second one and is presented as one.

**M10a. An outcome not yet written is not one of the four, and it never presents as *collected*. Added 2026-09-02, routed here from the disk-fill replay's finding N-g, whose own repair is `Rented-server runtime` S14's.** On a mount the render has filled, S14 holds the collection outcomes pending with the rest of that ending's writes, so between the fill and the landing there is a capture record to which M13's set has not yet given an outcome. **That state is presented as its own sentence and as none of M10's four.** Never as *collected*, which would claim a record is there to open when none is; never as *collected, and the stream was empty*, which asserts what the render printed; and never as *never established*, which is M11's different claim and rests on the **absence** of a capture record written before the worker started, not on an outcome that has not landed yet. **Whether the pending outcomes travel to the user beside the job's state or are withheld until the landing is `Rented-server runtime` S14's to decide and is not decided here** — this clause binds either answer, because under both the outcome is unwritten and under neither may it read as collected.

**M11. The two the row exists to separate are separated by two different records and this document presents them apart.** *Collected and empty* rests on what Smoozy observed passing its own loop; *never established* rests on the absence of a capture record written before the worker started. **Neither is derived from a sink**, and nothing here re-derives either.

**M12. The exit status is carried verbatim and is never presented as a claim about what was written** (M22). **Twenty endings report success, six of them ending at 0 with an empty output folder** *(corrected 2026-08-31; this read **"Twelve endings report success"**, which was the figure the Blender research carried before its repair of 2026-08-30. **The correction is to the figure and to nothing else** — the decision this clause states is unharmed and strengthened by it. The companion sources file's pointer is the runner's to correct.)*, and three of them are in this stage's own evidence from outside Blender — a full disk, a purged driver and a mismatched driver each finishing at 0. **For anything that must decide whether a job is complete, the six that end at 0 with an empty output folder are the sharper fact and the count is not** — a completeness gate reading only the status would call every one of those six a finished job with its frames written. **So no presentation says a job succeeded because its status was 0**, and *"a job that stops with nothing to read is failed, not still rendering."*

**M13. Both streams are presented apart and are never merged into one panel**, because each stream carries some of Blender's own words and which one a line came from differs by version. **Anything mixing the two loses which one a line came from.** *(Premise corrected 2026-10-11, the Creator's eighth notes, note 6: this clause said the complaints stream carries the failure text. It does not, in general: almost everything Blender writes goes to the ordinary stream in every version, and in 5.0, 5.1 and 5.2 a failure such as a save error is two lines on the ordinary stream and nothing on the complaints stream. Label: **observed in a test** — see [Sources](Sources/Desktop%20app%20and%20delivery.sources.md), Section 16. So an empty complaints stream says nothing about whether the render reported errors.)*

**M13a. The ordinary stream's record stands beside a failure that said nothing about why, and it stands as that stream's record. Added 2026-08-29, per Row 504.** On the ending the spec names, the two streams take **two different outcomes of the one four-outcome set M10 names** (`Rented-server runtime` M13's): the complaints stream is *collected, and the stream was empty* and the ordinary stream is *collected*. **E4's per-stream-per-attempt rule is what makes that expressible** and is taken entire (`Queue & History` [E4](Queue%20&%20History.md#8-the-ending-and-the-words)), so M10's four bullets apply to each stream on its own and the user meets the plain sentence for one and a record to open for the other. **That is the promise's positive half and it needs no new mechanism.** What this clause adds is that the ordinary stream's record is presented **under the name of the stream it came from** and **never occupies the place the complaints stream's record occupies** — a substitution there would make M13's separation a wording rather than a structure, and would put words in the one place on the surface that means *this is what the machine complained of*.

**M13b. And nothing presents the ordinary stream's words as the reason. Added 2026-08-29, per Row 504; a prohibition rather than an outcome, which is why the three negatives already here did not carry it.** M9 forbids editing either stream, M12 forbids reading the exit status as a claim about what was written, M13 forbids merging the two, and **none of them forbids laying the ordinary output out where a cause would go**. E10 already refuses to read the words — *"Not sorted, not grouped, not mined for a cause"* *(the source's own capital restored 2026-08-29 at the re-gate repair; the quotation was word-perfect and lower-cased it to fit)* — so nothing here derives a cause; **what is fixed here is where the words may be put.** Three things follow and they are the complete list.
