- **No line of that stream is lifted out of it.** No excerpt, no last line, no highlight, no leading summary. **The last line is a sampling line** (`Preparation/Specs/Queue & History.md`, observed), and setting it beside a plain sentence about a failure asserts a cause **by placement** that no record carries — M6's harm, in the one ending where the words are guaranteed not to name one.

  ~~**One narrowing, and only one.**~~ **Two narrowings** *(heading changed 2026-10-18 with the second, below)*. **The first added 2026-10-03, out of step 10c's map of the Creator's notes on the test copy (note 12), on the build runner's ruling under the Creator's standing delegation; no auditor has seen it.** *Creator-stated, note 12: "The little window this button lives in should also be a little output window — just the last 3 lines really."* **A rendering entry, while its job renders, may show the ordinary stream's newest three complete lines as they arrive.** They are a period-held reading (`Queue & History` E8), never on an entry whose job has ended, never from the complaints stream, and never beside a sentence about a failure — so the harm above, which is placement beside a failure, cannot arise: a rendering entry has no failure sentence. Taking the lines at their line ends is not reading them for a cause (`Queue & History` E10), and what is supplied is still E8's bytes: the page keeps the newest three lines of them, so the third bullet's *not supplied* holds. History entries and every failed, stopped or given-up entry show none, and the rest of this clause stands for them unchanged.

  **A second narrowing, added 2026-10-18, Creator-stated, the thirteenth notes, note 7: a setup run, while it runs, may show the ordinary stream's newest three complete lines ~~of the step under way~~ its steps have printed, kept until another step prints or the run ends, as they arrive** *(changed 2026-10-18, the Creator's fourteenth notes, note 1: "Server setup output doesn't actually output anything." The lines went when their step ended. Keeping them past it puts them beside no failure: a failed step ends the run, and the window goes with it)*, on the rendering entry's terms — ~~never from the complaints stream and~~ never beside a sentence about a failure. **From 2026-10-24 the setup window's three lines come from either stream, in the order the pieces arrived, each line drawn so that which stream it came from is never lost (M13's reason), and the last of them may be a line not yet ended, shown as it stands; a line a program rewrites in place is shown in its newest form, here and in the whole record's panel, as a terminal shows it, while the record keeps every byte as printed (M9).** *Changed by the design pass for the eighteenth notes, ruling 4, Creator-stated, note 5: "I want this output to show everything the server outputs." Many programs print their progress on the complaints stream, and the window never showed it. The rendering entry's narrowing above is unchanged. No auditor has seen it.* *Creator-stated: "Can we add a blender output-style window to server setup? … 3 lines with an option to open the whole thing and copy to clipboard."* The supply is `Server Setup` Y7a. The whole of what the run printed is offered to open, each stream as that stream's record (M13), as a rendering entry offers its job's. A failed step's words stay behind *What the server said* (`Server Setup` S2a). No auditor has seen it.
- **Nothing composed from that stream reaches the surface where the reason is said.** The plain sentence is composed from E4's outcome for **its own** stream alone; the only diagnosis permitted anywhere beside the machine's words is [16.4](#164-row-287-presented-the-disk-full-carve-out)'s disk-full carve-out, **which this clause does not widen** and which reaches no further than F17 already lets it; and M32's captured sentence is the composed plain sentence and never a line of either stream.
- **What is supplied and what is not.** Supplied: both records, each with the name of the stream it came from, and each stream's outcome — the same supply M10 and M13 already make. **Not supplied: any excerpt, any candidate cause, any ordering or weighting of the words, and any mark distinguishing one line of a stream from another.** So a drawing that presented the ordinary output as the reason would have to compose it out of nothing this document hands over. **The words the user reads are Design's** ([Section 24](#24-what-this-document-does-not-own)); this clause bounds what may be put where and not how it reads.

**[Method](Method.md)'s third escalation, checked and declined**: nothing is put in front of the user and nothing is taken from them — the ordinary output the spec promises beside the sentence is there to open under M13a, there is no new place to look, nothing to type, and nothing that can be lost.

### 16.4 Row 287 presented: the disk-full carve-out

**M14. The diagnosis is shown *alongside* the machine's own words and never instead of them**, which is the row's own wording and F14 of the answering document.

**M15. Its two observed values are supplied and neither is re-derived here** — the free-space reading and the outcome of the next real write to that place (F11). The reading is presented **as of the moment it was taken, on the machine's own clock**, and **is never re-read to keep a message fresh**.

**M15a. Route (b)'s reading arrives carried and is never taken at the moment the desktop learns the ending. Added 2026-08-22, scenario replays routed item 6.** The free-space reading the carve-out's route (b) fires on is taken **on the machine, in the same act that writes that render's recorded outcome**, and reaches this side carried with that outcome and with its moment on the machine's own clock ([Server protocol and transfers](Server%20protocol%20and%20transfers.md#92-the-two-routes-to-the-cause-and-the-moment-each-is-fixed-at) F6a, [Rented-server runtime](Rented-server%20runtime.md#12-health-readings) V12). **No reading taken when the ending is learned feeds route (b)**, and nothing on a presentation path takes one in any case (M1, P9, and [P10d](#33-ui-event-delivery)'s closed set, which excludes a free-space re-read). M15's *as of the moment it was taken … never re-read to keep a message fresh* is what already governs it. Two things follow for the presentation. **A recorded outcome carrying no such reading is not one carrying a zero** — route (b) does not fire, the machine's own words stand alone as M17 already has them do where no reading is available, and nothing infers a full disk from the absence. **And the carried reading is never presented as the live disk-full condition**, whose two current suppliers stay M15's (`Server protocol and transfers` F11a): it is an observation on an immutable recorded ending, so presenting it as a condition would put up a record the next refresh withdraws, which is M6's harm.

**M16. It never claims the reading is current, never claims the amount is the amount the rental may use, and never says a render failed because the disk was full.** It says the place a write was refused for space is out of space.

**M17. It reaches no further than `File Browser`'s reading does**, which is the row's own ceiling; **the refusal-driven message where a machine offers no reading at all is the spec's general rule rather than this carve-out** (F17), and this document presents it as such.

### 16.5 Row 291 presented: anything that isn't instant shows that it's underway

**M18. The indicator can appear at the instant of the click, and that is a property of the protocol rather than of the screen.** A request is **recorded locally before it is sent** (C3) and **acknowledged as recorded before its work completes** (C7), so *it has started* and *it has finished* are two separate correlated answers. **So the presentation shows *underway* from the local write, before anything has left the computer.**

**M19. The three reporting routes are `Server protocol and transfers`' G4 and this document reads them rather than inventing a fourth**: a transfer reports bytes staged against bytes expected at its own accounting interval, bounded above in **time** as well as in bytes; a request the machine took as an operation reports through the event journal read forward from the cursor; a reading reports its own result and the fact that it was submitted, bounded by its waiter's deadline.

**M20. Progress is never derived from the far end's own view of a file** (G5), and a batch reports both levels — the per-transfer count and the enumeration — which is what Rows 190 and 299 are written against.

**M21. A waiter's expired deadline is presented as the waiter giving up and never as the work ending** (G9). **A stalled channel is presented as a stalled channel and never as a broken connection** (G8), and the connection's own condition comes from `Servers`.

**M22. Nothing here detects a hang and nothing here makes one undetectable later.** Row 291 promises *underway* and no limit on how long *underway* may last; **the arrangement still distinguishes a slow thing from a stopped one**, because every transfer's progress is its own byte count with a bounded reporting interval and every waiter has a deadline.

### 16.6 Atomic and partial outcomes presented

**M23. The outcome record is read and its boundary is not re-decided here.** [§9.4](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#94-the-outcome-record) holds the enumeration of units, each unit's outcome, and **the action's own outcome derived from the list and never a separate field**. **The presentation derives nothing of its own** and shows what that record says.

**M24. *Not attempted* is presented as *not attempted*, naming the unit that stopped it**, and never as *failed*. Row 292 requires the user to be told what Smoozy could not do, and a unit reported as failed when it was never tried is not that.

**M25. An interruption loses the saying and never the fact**, because each unit's outcome is durable **before** it is presented; the next presentation reads the record.

**M26. A failure record raised against a failed unit hangs off the outcome record as its durable subject**; it is the mark, held apart from the unit outcome, carries no clearing gesture and goes with its subject. The unit outcome itself is immutable and is not the user's to clear. *(Changed 2026-09-19 from a code round — the Gemini catch-up of step 10a, Area 4, upstream must-do U-1. No auditor of this document has seen it. This called that record *"the clearable half"*.)*

**M27. Where an action's partition unit is the whole action, there is no unit list to show**, and the presentation says the one outcome. **Which kind an action is comes from its own document's declaration** (A7) and is never decided at the moment of drawing it.

### 16.7 Row 347 presented: a repair that says when it next tries

**Constraint:** Row 347, and `Servers` [N10a](Servers.md#32-the-standing-repair-which-never-ends-while-the-app-is-open), which publishes the value this subsection presents. Added 2026-08-21.

**M28. Presentation is handed three things about a repairing server and never four.** The **connection condition's observed value** (K6's three, T13's existing supply); the ***moment the next attempt is due***, run-held, absent while an attempt is in flight and absent for good under N8b; and, where the server was working when the break was declared, the **last-known work reading with its own moment**, which `Servers` S12 keeps alive through a drop and whose marking — the stamp and the not-updating statement — `Queue & History` [C7](Queue%20&%20History.md#11-one-job-two-records-two-lifetimes) governs, Row 323's answer's home for this reading *(citation corrected 2026-08-22, scenario replays Pile 1 item R2: this pointed at [16.1](#161-what-presenting-means-here), which carries nothing about the marking)*. **All three are readings this document draws and does not compose.**

**M29. The word the work column says is not the connection's observed value, and conflating them is what made this look unanswerable.** K6's three values are the **condition's**, supplied to T13's notification and to whatever draws a server's state. **The work column's vocabulary is Design's own** — *Rendering* with a job's name, *Idle*, *Reconnecting…*, or nothing — and none of those four is one of K6's three. **What the column reads is whether a repair is under way at all**, which is true from the declared break until the period ends, **runs and standing pauses alike**. So the column says *Reconnecting…* for the whole repair while K6's value goes on moving between *reconnecting* and *disconnected* underneath, **where it is supply and nothing draws it.**

**M29a. The spec was changed to say this, 2026-08-22, on the Creator's ruling.** M29 used to end *"neither contradicts the other because they are answering different questions"* — written when `Preparation/Specs/Servers.md` promised the user a **two-state flicker**, *disconnected* between one run of attempts and the next and *reconnecting* again the next time Smoozy tries. **Both documents knew they differed and each declared its own answer**, which is not a thing this stage may leave standing: a spec sentence is the Creator's and only they can move it. **The scenario replay stopped on it (S1) and the ruling changed the spec**, which now says the server reads as reconnecting for the whole repair. **Nothing in this document moves.** The reason the steady word won is the one M29 already gives: with attempts fifteen seconds apart a label that alternates cannot be read. **The cost, stated by the Creator and accepted with the ruling:** the user can no longer tell at a glance whether an attempt is in flight at this second. **What is not lost** is the saying — a failed run still raises `Servers` N5's record and T13's could-not-restore notification, because that fires on the condition's value and never on the column's word.

**M30. The countdown is a drawing and is bounded by P10b.** It ticks against M28's moment, takes no reading, makes no request, posts nothing on P6's queue, and ceases to exist when the thing it draws is off screen. **Where the moment is absent the line has no countdown and the word stands alone**, which is the honest reading of *an attempt is running right now*.

**M31. Two of the column's four contents can be true at once and this document supplies both rather than choosing.** A server that was rendering when the connection went owes the column *Rendering* with its job's name **and** *Reconnecting…* at the same moment. **Both are supplied, each with its own moment; which occupies the one slot Design drew is Design's** and is [Section 24](#24-what-this-document-does-not-own)'s. **What is not Design's to invent is a third reading composed from the two**, because the last-known work reading is stale by construction and saying so is Row 323's requirement, whose marking rule for this reading is `Queue & History` [C7](Queue%20&%20History.md#11-one-job-two-records-two-lifetimes)'s *(citation given its home 2026-08-22, scenario replays Pile 1 item R2)*.

**M31a. [Method](Method.md)'s third escalation for Row 347, checked and declined. Written 2026-08-29 at the stage-close repair, because [Section 23](#23-moments-that-reached-the-creator) had been pointing at this subsection for it since 2026-08-21 and no such statement stood here.** Nothing new is put in front of the user: M28 hands over three readings the design already draws, M30's countdown is a drawing bounded by P10b that ticks against a moment `Servers` N10a already publishes and ceases to exist off screen, and M31 supplies both of the column's contents rather than inventing a third. **There is no place to look, nothing to type, and nothing that can be lost** — where the moment is absent the line simply carries no countdown, which M30 already states as the honest reading. **What the user could lose is a second of precision about which attempt is in flight**, and that is the Creator's own ruling of 2026-08-22 recorded at M29a rather than a cost this document introduces.

### 16.8 What the plumbing hands the diary and the report entry point

**Constraint:** [Reporting a problem](Reporting%20a%20problem.md#3-what-goes-into-the-diary) J6 and [§8](Reporting%20a%20problem.md#8-the-two-entry-points) X4, routed here by that document's §12 item 5 because the plumbing is this section's and the sentences the user meets are composed here. Added 2026-08-25 at the tenth sitting. Rows 454 and 468 are answered there; these two clauses are the plumbing halves their answers stand on.

**M32. At the first presentation per condition identity or ending record, the plumbing hands the diary service the composed plain sentence verbatim**, with the record's kind ([§8.1](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#81-two-kinds-and-the-kind-is-declared)), its condition identity or the subject outcome it marks, its durable subject and the moment — [5.3](#53-the-diary-service)'s service writing the failure line J6 defines. **Once, keyed exactly as Q1 keys the presentation**, which is also what the user actually met once. The words are captured where they exist — at presentation — because [§8.2](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#82-a-condition-record-is-a-derivation-never-a-copy) keeps the record a derivation holding no prose; **nothing composes the words a second time at report time**, so a presentation that has changed since cannot make the diary lie about what was said, which is the whole of how *in the same words* is kept. The captured string is the composed sentence as handed to the presentation, **before** X7's two substitutions — the typography point is *"on the path to the screen and on no other path"*, and the diary is not a screen, the same parity [Reporting a problem](Reporting%20a%20problem.md#7-writing-the-file-and-putting-it-in-front-of-the-user) V1 already applies to the user's own sentence — so the diary holds the same words with none of the screen's spacing characters, and no richer sentence is derived for anyone. **The capture belongs to whatever composes the sentence the user reads, and a path that composes a stand-in sentence captures nothing** — where this section's own plumbing composes a sentence because the owning module has supplied none, that path leaves the record's one capture unspent, so the composer the user actually met is the one that spends it. *(Sentence added 2026-09-12 from a code round. Without it the clause's own last six words — "which is also what the user actually met once" — are carried by nothing: where two paths can present one record, the diary keeps whichever sentence ran first, which may be one no person read.)* **Second limb: a failure sentence the owning module composes and shows without raising a record is handed to the diary service verbatim, once per presentation** — once for each time the user asked for the act whose ending it states, a read of a `.blend` or a dependency check being the two that reach it ([Render Settings](Render%20Settings.md#12-what-this-document-supplies-and-to-which-document) §12's declaration and [Dependency Upload & Remap](Dependency%20Upload%20%26%20Remap.md#11-what-this-document-supplies-and-to-which-document) §11's). It carries the sentence, the server row's local identifier with the user's own label for the machine beside it and never in place of it, the file's recorded path as its subject, and the moment on this computer's clock. **It is captured before X7's two substitutions, exactly as the first limb is**, and it is still `Reporting a problem` J6's failure line, so J1's closed set of kinds does not grow. **The first limb's rule holds here unchanged: a path that composes a stand-in sentence captures nothing** — only a real ending of a real attempt is captured, and an answer saying the act cannot be performed yet is not. **No once-mark is written anywhere**, because there is no record to hold one: an ending that stores nothing (`Render Settings` Q9) has nothing to key a second capture against, and a second request is a second presentation the user met. *(Limb added 2026-09-17 from a code round — Build step 10a, round 1, *a server's files, setting up and render settings*, must-do U1. Without it the two declarations named above promised a failure line the plumbing had no route to write, the first limb capturing only per condition identity or ending record and these endings storing none. No auditor of this document has seen it.)*

**M33. Every expanded failure presentation supplies the report entry point the failure handover, and the supply is structural rather than per-module.** The handover is X4's closed set: the record's **kind**, its **condition identity or subject outcome mark**, its **durable subject** ([§8.7](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#87-every-failure-record-names-a-durable-subject)'s closed list), the **presented sentence** — the same string M32 captured, never recomposed — and the **moment**. **The *Report a problem…* control is part of the one failure shape this plumbing draws**, so a module that presents a failure through this section carries the handover by construction, present and future modules alike — which is what makes Row 468's *every failure carries a route to report it* bind structurally rather than as a control added in one module. The collapsed row does not carry the control (settled at Design, by the shape's own rule), and the handover exists wherever the control is drawn. What the entry point does with it — the slice, the box's naming line, the file — is [Reporting a problem](Reporting%20a%20problem.md#8-the-two-entry-points) §8's and is not restated here.

## 17. Text rendering and the typography application point

**Constraint:** Row 304, quoted at the head of this document. The two rules are [Design](../Design/Whole%20app.md)'s and are **Creator-stated** (2026-08-10): *"**A non-breaking space goes before every em dash**, so a dash never begins a line"*, and *"**No orphans**: the last two words of any piece of prose are bound together, so a sentence never ends with one word alone on a line."* **Both hold everywhere the user reads, tooltips and button labels included.**

### 17.1 One application point and two substitutions

**X1. Every string of prose that will become displayable text passes through one function before it reaches anything that draws it, and the interface accepts prose from nowhere else** (A6). **That is the typography application point.** The strings X7 forbids it to touch reach the interface by the verbatim route X9 names, and there is no third way in.

**X2. It performs two substitutions and nothing else.** The space preceding an em dash becomes a **non-breaking space**; the last inter-word space of a piece of prose becomes a **non-breaking space**. **A piece of prose is one string as handed in** — the point does not parse sentences out of a paragraph and does not join two strings.

**X3. It is a property of what draws the text and not an instruction to whoever writes a screen.** The Design document says outright that **neither rule can be held by remembering it** and that both have already been broken by the person applying them. **So no screen, no module and no document is asked to apply either rule by hand**, and a string that arrives without them is corrected rather than rejected.

**X10. Where the orphan rule must hold inside a multi-sentence block, the caller hands each sentence as its own string.** X2 binds the last inter-word space of the string handed in, and *a piece of prose is one string as handed in* — so a paragraph handed over whole has every interior sentence ending unbound, which is the ordinary shape of a failure's plain sentence at [16.3](#163-row-286-presented-the-plain-sentence-and-the-machines-own-words). **The point does not learn to parse.** Finding sentence ends inside prose is modelling, and it binds the wrong pair of words wherever the model is wrong — the same trap C11 names on the filesystem side and X8 names on this one. **Handing each sentence separately gets the whole rule with no model inside the point**, and X2 already accommodates it without changing. **The narrowing that remains where a caller does not is carried at [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 28** rather than closed by a parser.

### 17.2 Why a substitution and not a layout rule

**X4. Both rules are pure character substitutions, and that is what makes them survive [Section 2](#2-the-shell)'s cost.** ~~Wails~~ **the selected shell — Tauri v2 from 2026-09-02, and Wails before it; the argument never depended on which, and the name is corrected rather than the reasoning** — puts **three different engines** under the application — one self-updating, one frozen to the operating-system version, one whose own project calls its feature picture *"very hard to compile accurate information about"* (H11). **A stylesheet or layout rule would hold only where a stylesheet applies and only where that engine implements it. A substituted character is in the string.**

**X5. And a great deal of what the user reads is drawn by no engine at all.** Menu item labels ([Section 18](#18-menus-and-pickers)), the system file picker's own chrome, and **notification text, which the operating system draws** ([Section 13](#13-notifications)) — none of them is reachable by a rule about how the application's own view lays out. **One transform at the point strings are produced covers every surface**; nothing else does.

**X6. Nothing here claims what a renderer does with the character.** The substitution puts a non-breaking space where the rules require one, which is what the rules ask for; **how each of the three engines and each of the three operating systems breaks lines around it is not observed and is not claimed** ([Section 22](#22-unresolved)).

### 17.3 What it must never touch

**X7. The point is applied to prose meant for the user and to nothing else.** The closed list of what it never touches:

- **The machine's own words.** Row 286's raw record is unedited — *"not a redaction, not a filter, not a prefix, not a trim"* — and a typography substitution is an edit. **The raw record is exempt, and the exemption is mandatory rather than optional** (M9).
- **Any path, file name, host, port or address.** A non-breaking space inserted into a path produces a path that does not exist, and it would be compared, byte for byte, against a name by [Section 7](#7-the-receiving-filesystem-collision-judgement)'s judgement — which is exactly the class of string manipulation that judgement refuses.
- **Anything the user can copy as data.** The Edit menu's cut, copy and paste act on whatever has focus ([18.2](#182-what-that-means-for-the-four-menus)), and a substituted character travelling out of the application in a value would be a value Smoozy quietly changed.
- **A fingerprint or a host key**, neither of which is ever put on a screen at all (`Security and licence` §5.4).
- **Any string that is about to be written anywhere** — the local record, a filename, a request. **The point is on the path to the screen and on no other path.**

**X8. So the boundary is drawn once: a module hands the point *text for a person* and hands every other string somewhere else.** A module that hands a path to the point has made a category error, and a builder meeting an ambiguous case treats the string as data rather than as prose, because the cost of missing a dash is a line break and the cost of substituting into data is a broken value.

**X9. *Somewhere else* is named here, because a route that is not named is a route a builder invents.** Every string in X7's list unarguably appears on screen — the raw record behind a plain sentence, file names, a destination path, a host, a port, the stored name that took a collision at C19, anything the user can copy — so X7 and A6 together would otherwise permit two readings, and they build two different applications: the names go through the point and a substituted character travels in a value Smoozy quietly changed and that no longer matches the bytes [Section 7](#7-the-receiving-filesystem-collision-judgement) judges, or a builder invents an unnamed second route and X7's boundary has nothing behind it — which is precisely the failure X3 says cannot be held by remembering.

**So: the interface accepts prose only from the typography point, and data only from a verbatim route that performs no substitution of any kind.** A module chooses one at the moment it hands the string over, by X8's rule. **The two routes are the complete set and a third is a fault in this section rather than a case it does not cover**, and [Section 21](#21-what-this-document-supplies-and-to-which-document)'s table carries the verbatim route beside the typography point so that the closed list of what this document supplies actually holds one. **A6's enforcement property survives whole**: it reads *every string of prose* rather than *every string*, and the boundary is still kept by there being no other way in.

**Drawn in the order its bytes run. Added 2026-09-28, on the build runner's ruling that a path shown in another order is not the real path.** A value on the verbatim route is drawn so that it reads in the order it is stored. A path is laid out left to right, one segment after another, and each segment — and any name drawn alone — is kept apart from its neighbours, so that right-to-left letters inside it read in their own direction and never carry a separator or a neighbouring segment with them. **This is done by what draws the value and never by putting anything into it**: what is copied, compared or written is the value as handed over, byte for byte, which is X7's second and third bullets unchanged. How the three engines lay this out is looked at on the Mac's alone and claimed nowhere (X6's shape).

---

## 18. Menus and pickers

**Constraint:** Row 305, quoted at the head of this document, which the Design document labels **an assumption** and hands here to confirm from each platform's own documentation. **Four menus are named and no fifth**, and this document confirms the provision rather than re-taking the design. **Row 305's own text still reads *five*, because it is the Design sentence as quoted when the row was opened**; the Creator ruled on 2026-08-25 that *Report a problem…* sits in the Smoozy menu, per `Preparation/Specs/Reporting a problem.md`, which left the Help menu holding nothing and removed it. Row 481 carries that ruling.

### 18.1 What each platform provides, read from its own documentation

**K1. macOS provides it and the assumption is confirmed.** Documented — Apple, *macOS User Guide*, "What's in the menu bar on Mac?", `https://support.apple.com/guide/mac-help/menu-bar-mchlp1446/mac`, read 2026-08-18: *"The menu bar runs along the top of the screen on your Mac. Use the menus and icons in the menu bar to choose commands, perform tasks, and check status."* **So the bar is the system's and belongs to the screen rather than to a window**, and the only real question is what goes in it, exactly as Design says. **The register does not hold this reading**, which is why it is quoted here in full.

**K2. Windows provides a menu bar, and it belongs to a window rather than to the screen.** Documented — Microsoft, *About Menus - Win32 apps*, `https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/menurc/about-menus`, page dated 2018-05-31 and updated 2025-05-19, read 2026-08-18: *"Only an overlapped or pop-up window can contain a menu bar; a child window cannot contain one. If the window has a title bar, the system positions the menu bar just below it. A menu bar is always visible."* And: *"Each menu must have an owner window."* **So there is no screen-level bar on Windows and the Design's one-button arrangement is an application choice rather than a platform necessity** — the platform's own alternative is a window-owned bar the system positions just below the title bar. **The assumption is confirmed in its conclusion and corrected in its reason**, and nothing about the menu list changes.

**K3. Linux provides nothing for it, and that is the same shape as its downloads folder.** freedesktop's specification index lists 33 specifications, read 2026-08-18 at `https://specifications.freedesktop.org/`, and **none of them is an application menu bar.** The one named `menu` is the **Desktop Menu Specification**, whose own introduction reads *"This document defines how to construct a user-visible hierarchy of applications, typically displayed as a menu"* (`http://specifications.freedesktop.org/menu/latest/`, read 2026-08-18) — **the desktop's list of applications, not an application's own bar.** So on Linux the menus are the application's own to draw and **no platform provision is being relied on**, which is what Design already assumed. **Corrected 2026-08-20: *provides nothing for it* is too strong, and the survey it rests on could never have seen the counter-evidence.** freedesktop publishes no specification for one, and that clause stands. But the toolkits an application would actually be built on ship a menu bar, and one of them documents a published desktop-level route to a **global** bar on Linux by name — The Qt Company's `QMenuBar` reference, read 2026-08-20 at `https://doc.qt.io/qt-6/qmenubar.html`: *"On macOS and on certain Linux desktop environments such as Ubuntu Unity, QMenuBar is a wrapper for using the system-wide menu bar."*, and on `nativeMenuBar` — *"On Linux, if the com.canonical.AppMenu.Registrar service is available on the D-Bus session bus, then Qt will communicate with it to install the application's menus into the global menu bar."*; and the GNOME project's GTK 4 reference for `GtkPopoverMenuBar`, read the same day, *"Presents a horizontal bar of items that pop up menus when clicked."* **That reference's address was recorded nowhere in this project and is supplied 2026-08-23: `https://docs.gtk.org/gtk4/class.PopoverMenuBar.html`, GTK API version 4.0, library version 4.23.3, read 2026-08-23** — the sentence stands word for word, and the page describes an in-window widget built from a `GMenuModel` rather than any screen-level or desktop-level bar. **Nothing about the arrangement changes** — Design already chose one button in the title bar on Windows and Linux — and the same concession this document already makes for Windows is now owed to Linux: **the one-button arrangement is an application choice rather than a platform necessity.**

**And the concession stopped one step short of where the register takes it. Ground repaired 2026-08-23.** `Facts.md` Fact 98 reads the same question from a different page — GTK's own `GtkApplication`, `https://docs.gtk.org/gtk4/class.Application.html`, read 2026-08-20: *"GtkApplication will automatically load menus from the GtkBuilder resource located at 'gtk/menus.ui', relative to the application's resource base path."* and *"The menu with the ID 'menubar' is taken as the application's menubar."*, exported on the session bus as a `GDBusMenuModel` — and concludes that **"that route belongs to GTK and is not available to a system-web-view shell"**. **That is the conclusion this clause is missing, and it is the right shape of it:** each of the two published routes hangs off its own toolkit's application object — Qt's off `QMenuBar` and the AppMenu registrar, GTK's off `GtkApplication` and a resource the toolkit loads — so **neither is a platform provision a shell gets for free**, and *no platform provision is being relied on* is true in the strong sense rather than only for want of a freedesktop specification.

**One thing is left open rather than folded in, because it is a different question.** The chosen shell's Linux build is itself on GTK — its own installation page states *"Linux requires the standard `gcc` build tools plus `libgtk3` and `libwebkit`"* (E4) — and **whether that build can reach its own toolkit's route is unread on every page consulted here.** That is not a platform question and does not belong to K3: it is **what the shell exposes**, which [18.3](#183-what-the-shell-must-expose) holds as a required property of a candidate and K4 records as not established. **Nothing in [18.2](#182-what-that-means-for-the-four-menus) or [18.3](#183-what-the-shell-must-expose) moves either way**, because the arrangement Design chose on Linux is application-drawn on both readings.

**K4. What this closes and what it does not.** Row 305's assumption is **confirmed on all three**, from each platform's own documentation, and no Windows computer was needed because it is a documentation question. **What is not established is what Wails exposes**, which is [18.3](#183-what-the-shell-must-expose) and is a property of a candidate rather than of a platform.

### 18.2 What that means for the four menus

**K5. Four menus and no fifth, and the list is Design's rather than this document's.** Smoozy; Edit; Go; Window. **This document builds the integration and adds no item**, and in particular there is **no File menu**, **no Help menu** and no menu of server actions, because Design settles that a menu bar repeating what already lives on the thing it acts on would be a second copy of the interface. **The list was Smoozy, Edit, Go, Window, Help until 2026-08-25**, when the Creator ruled *Report a problem…* into the Smoozy menu per its spec's Creator-stated placement; that item was the whole of what Help held, so the menu went with it (Row 481).

**K6. One of the four is the operating system's own** — Window — and on a platform that does not provide it, the application supplies only what that platform's own conventions supply. **Nothing is invented to fill a gap a platform leaves.** **This clause named two until 2026-08-25**, Help being the other; with the Help menu gone the clause is narrower and the rule behind it is unchanged.

**K7. Cut, copy and paste act on whatever has focus**, so the Edit menu's three items are one set and the focused thing decides. **The focused thing may be an engine-drawn field or a native surface**, and the integration routes the three to whichever it is; that is the one place the three-engine cost of H11 reaches the menus.

**K8. Menu item labels are prose the user reads and go through the typography point** (X5), even though no engine draws them.

### 18.3 What the shell must expose

**K9. The shell must expose the platform's native menu bar**, which is required property 6 of H6. **On macOS that is not optional**: K1's bar is drawn by the system whether the application fills it or not, so a shell that cannot put items into it leaves a Mac with an empty system menu bar and four menus with nowhere to go. **Corrected 2026-09-06, found at Build by [H16a](#25-the-rulings-stop-run-and-not-reached)'s re-walk.** This opened *"Wails must expose the platform's native menu bar"*, which named the shell that did not win; **the requirement was never that shell's** and no word of it moves with the choice. [H6b](#22-the-route-chosen-and-the-properties-it-is-held-to) property 6 is where the selected shell answers it, from that project's own pages.

**K10. Where it cannot, the choice moves down H7's list — now to Tauri v2 rather than to Qt 6 — rather than the arrangement changing.** **The fallback is not a redesign**: Windows' and Linux's own arrangement — the same menus behind one button in the title bar — is already application-drawn, so a shell without native menu-bar integration would keep Row 305's promise on two systems and lose only the Mac's native placement. **So the promise does not break; the Mac's own convention does**, and that is why this is a property of the route rather than an escalation.

### 18.4 The system file picker

**K11. The system file picker is reached through this document's adapter and by no other route** (A1), and it is one service for every module that wants one.

**K12. Whether a system file picker can take files and folders together is Row 307 and is `File Browser`'s.** It is not answered here. **What is supplied is the picker**, and Design already assumes one control either way, so a negative answer costs a second control rather than a promise.

**K13. Whatever a picker returns is a path from the user and is judged like any other name before anything is written** (C20, W7). A path the user chose is not a path that is safe to write into.

### 18.5 The keyboard shortcuts beside the menu items

**Constraint:** Row 474 — the mockup draws its menu combinations in macOS notation and no document anywhere says what a Windows or Linux user presses. **Fact 137's answer of record is [The three desktop systems read again](The%20three%20desktop%20systems%20read%20again%20—%20dying,%20revealing,%20resolving,%20and%20what%20the%20shell%20does%20not%20expose.md) §6**, and every conventions claim below is that reading's, each vendor's own publication. Added 2026-08-25 at the tenth sitting. **The acts that carry a key are Design's and no key is added to any act here** (K5's discipline); what is decided is the per-system integration the drawing left unposed.

**K14. The shell exposes an accelerator beside a menu item, and it works only with a platform-native menu bar — so the mechanism is split by K2 and K3's own arrangement.** *(Documented, the input document §6: the accelerator is a field on a menu item whose only consumer is native menu construction, and a whole-documentation search at the released version finds no keyboard, shortcut or hotkey facility anywhere else.)* **On macOS the native bar K9 already requires catches the keystroke — the shell's accelerator is the route and the system draws the key beside the item.** **On Windows and Linux, where the four menus are the application's own drawing behind one button, the accelerator beside an item is a label the application draws, and catching the keystroke is the web view's — a key handler in the page.** The shell publishes no route for it there and none is waited for. The shell's accelerator has **no Meta/Super/Windows-key modifier** — deliberately commented out in its source — which lines up with the one rule freedesktop and KDE both publish: applications never use that key.

**K15. The drawn combinations are one drawing of three renderings, and the mapping is the modifier's.** The mockup's `⌘` combinations are macOS's answers as drawn. On Windows and Linux the primary modifier is **Ctrl**, which is the modifier every app-level convention either vendor publishes uses, and each drawn combination maps with it — the clipboard three to Ctrl+X, Ctrl+C, Ctrl+V (published by all three vendors); Select All and the five rail numbers by the same map, as the application's own combinations that no vendor publishes, checked against the reserved lists in K16 and nothing else. **The label drawn beside an item is that system's own notation** — the macOS symbols on macOS, the platform's own spelling on the other two — which is integration, not a redesign of the drawing. **Where a system publishes no app-level convention for an act macOS has one for, the item carries no drawn combination on that system rather than an invented one** — K6's own discipline applied to keys. Per the reading: **Quit** maps to Ctrl+Q on Linux (GNOME and KDE both publish it) and carries **no combination on Windows**, where no app-level quit convention is published and the system's own Alt+F4 needs nothing from Smoozy; **Hide, Minimise and Full screen** carry their combinations on macOS alone, neither Windows nor Linux publishing an app-level convention for any of the three; **Settings** maps to GNOME's Ctrl+Comma on Linux — the one distribution family this stage's Linux evidence already reads is GNOME's — with KDE's Ctrl+Shift+Comma recorded rather than drawn, and carries no combination on Windows, which publishes none. **No Help key is drawn and none is added**, though the conventions exist — F1 on Windows (published in Microsoft's own Win32 reference, a reversal the check found) and on both Linux desktops. **From 2026-08-25 there is no Help menu for one to attach to either** (K5).

**K16. Nothing drawn or mapped collides with anything any vendor reserves.** *(Documented, the input document §6.)* Windows reserves exactly two things — combinations involving the Windows key, and F12; macOS publishes a standards table under guidance rather than the word *reserved*; GNOME's legacy system-reserved list covers window-switching, window-menu, close and interrupt combinations; freedesktop's one hard rule is the Meta/Super key. None of the mapped set touches any of them. One published Windows behaviour is carried because it is K7's rule from the platform's side: **with a text box focused, the copy accelerator copies the selected text and app-defined accelerators are ignored — a control can win**, and the integration routes the clipboard three to whatever has focus either way.

**K17. Three menu-key behaviours the platforms publish are knowingly absent, named so the absence is a decision rather than an accident.** Windows publishes mnemonics (Alt plus an underlined letter), F10 to activate the menu bar and Shift+F10 for a context menu; GNOME publishes F10 for the primary menu. **Those are behaviours of a platform-drawn bar, and on Windows and Linux Smoozy's menus are its own drawing** — nothing anywhere promises them, the mockup draws none of them, and this document adds no item and no behaviour (K5). A later want here enters at Specs, not by a builder imitating the platform unasked.

**K18. The clipboard-keys residue is an assumption and is labelled as one.** On macOS the shell states the dependency outright: the native edit menu is what makes the ordinary clipboard keys work at all, which is one more reason K9's native bar is not optional there. **On Windows and Linux, whether cut, copy and paste keystrokes work in the web view without a native menu is established by no publication read** — the web views presumably handle editable fields themselves, and that is an assumption, not a read fact. [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 52 carries it with what breaks. **This clause permits the engine handling those keys itself**, which is what it was written for. **And the click route carries a second assumption of the same standing, added 2026-09-12 at Build step 10a round 1**: the menu items act through one old engine call whose standing in either web view is established by no publication read either, so *what holds without it* is itself unestablished. Item 52 carries that half too.

**K19. K10's shape holds for the keys as it holds for the bar, and the escalation is declined.** Every act with a drawn key has a control of its own elsewhere in the app, so a shell or an engine that could not carry a keystroke would cost the key and never a promise — a property of the route, not an escalation. **[Method](Method.md)'s third escalation, checked and declined**: labels on menus already drawn, nothing new in front of the user, nothing to type, nothing that can be lost.

### 18.6 The folder-reveal service

**Constraint:** [Reporting a problem](Reporting%20a%20problem.md#7-writing-the-file-and-putting-it-in-front-of-the-user) V7 and V8, routed here by that document's §12 item 5; `Download` [H7](Download.md#11-where-downloads-land-row-204)'s *"One place, and it opens"*, which has carried this gap since it was written, and H8's requirement that an unreachable place be presented as a place Smoozy cannot open rather than dropped; and Row 500's *Files on your computer*. **Fact 132's answer of record is [The three desktop systems read again](The%20three%20desktop%20systems%20read%20again%20—%20dying,%20revealing,%20resolving,%20and%20what%20the%20shell%20does%20not%20expose.md) §3.** Added 2026-08-25 at the tenth sitting. **One service, one answer, serving every caller — a second route anywhere would be two answers to one question.**

**K20. The service performs two acts and no third: reveal a named file selected in its folder, and open a folder, in the system's own file manager.** It is a named service of [Section 21](#21-what-this-document-supplies-and-to-which-document) and A1 admits no other route to either act. **It is platform code of this document's own, beside the shell, the same shape as the title bar (H6a) and the menus** — the chosen shell reaches none of it: its one browser-opening call **refuses the `file` scheme by name**, in a validator byte-identical across every current release checked, **and returns nothing at all on all three platforms**, so even were the scheme permitted the application could not learn that the open failed — which is precisely what it must be able to learn. *(Documented, the input document §3.)*

**K21. The per-system routes, each with the constraint that keeps it honest.** *(The input document §3; observed on macOS, documentation alone on Windows and Linux.)*

- **macOS: `selectFile:inFileViewerRootedAtPath:`, which returns a status, with symbolic links resolved before asking** — Apple's own page says the call *"does not follow symlinks when selecting the file"* and selects the symlink instead of its target, so the path is resolved first or the wrong thing is highlighted. **The no-outcome variant (`activateFileViewerSelectingURLs:`) is refused**: its declared return is nothing, so there is no outcome that could count as failure, and failure has to reach the caller. Folder-open is the same call with no file. No bundle, no entitlements and no Developer ID are preconditions — a plain binary reached the Finder and got answers back (observed, reproduced by the check).
- **Windows: `SHOpenFolderAndSelectItems`, with the component system initialised first** — its page **requires** that rather than suggests it: *"Not doing so causes … to fail."* Folder-open is `ShellExecute` on the folder path, with its documented greater-than-32 success contract. **The `explorer.exe /select` command line is folklore** — Microsoft publishes it in no reference documentation, and a launched process would return no status — and it is not used.
- **Linux: the portal's `OpenURI.OpenDirectory`**, published and versioned (version 3 of the portal API), **whose fallback is the route's own published behaviour rather than this application's error handling**: it is implemented over `org.freedesktop.FileManager1.ShowItems()` and falls back to the plain open on the directory where no file manager owns that name. It returns a handle and answers on a response signal, so failure is learnable in the ordinary way. Folder-open falls to `xdg-open`, which **ships in a separate project and is declared as a package dependency in E4's matrix**, or the call fails; **no file manager is assumed**, which the portal's fallback is what makes survivable. *(An earlier reading said Linux had no published reveal route at all; it was overturned by its own check, and the reversed form is the only one carried here.)*

**The first act may be handed a folder, and whether each system then selects it is labelled per system. Added 2026-10-03, for `Download` H7's opening of a job's own folder (the Creator's note 15 on the test copy); no auditor has seen it.** This is the first act on a different kind of named thing, not a third act. **macOS: unresolved** — Apple's page for `selectFile:inFileViewerRootedAtPath:` speaks of *"the file"* throughout and says nothing of a folder; the builder observes it at the desk and labels what it sees *observed in a test*. **Windows: documented** — `SHOpenFolderAndSelectItems` *"opens the parent folder and selects that item"*, and Microsoft's namespace introduction gives every folder and file in it an item ID, *"the functional equivalent of a file or folder name"*. **Linux: documented, with the portal's own hedge** — `OpenDirectory` opens *"the directory containing a local file or directory … possibly selecting it"*. Windows and Linux are documentation alone, as item 53 already says of this whole section ([Sources](Sources/Desktop%20app%20and%20delivery.sources.md), *K21, the first act on a folder*). **What holds where it does not select:** a refusal falls back, at the caller, to the second act on the folder that holds it, which opens with nothing selected; a reveal that succeeds and highlights nothing is the K24 gap and costs the user a guess, which is the state before this paragraph.

**K22. The outcome reaches the caller, and *could not be opened* is a thing that exists rather than silence.** Every route K21 names returns an outcome, and the service reports it — success, or a refusal with no cause modelled behind it — **and does nothing else: no retry, no failure record of its own, no surface**. The reveal is the pointing, not the act, and the thing pointed at is already written, named and the user's. What a caller does with a refusal is its own document's, and both consumers already say it: the report box **withholds the folder-has-opened sentence** and still names the file and its folder ([Reporting a problem](Reporting%20a%20problem.md#7-writing-the-file-and-putting-it-in-front-of-the-user) V8), and `Download` H8 presents an unreachable place **as a place Smoozy cannot open rather than dropping it from the set** — a distinction that exists only because this service's attempt can fail and say so. **A builder cannot half-use this service**: there is no variant that returns nothing, on any system, by K21's own refusal of the one call that would.

**K23. The callers, and Row 500's control is not a second route.** The report box's reveal of the written report file (`Reporting a problem` §7); the *show a job's downloaded files* control, whose handing — the set, never a folder, and its one place — stays `Download` H7's; and the transfer header's *Files on your computer*, which is that same way-back drawn in the place Stop was and opens its place through this service like the others. **What each caller is handed and shows is its own document's and Design's; the act is this service's alone.**

**K24. What this section does not answer, named so nobody reaches for it here.** Whether the reveal actually brings a window forward with the right row highlighted was never checked, even on the Mac; every Windows and Linux claim is documentation alone ([Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 53). **And telling two paths to be one folder is not this section's question**: the reveal resolves symbolic links and compares nothing — the per-system sameness calls are Fact 140's subject, held for a later batch, and naming them here would answer a row this sitting holds back. **[Method](Method.md)'s third escalation, checked and declined**: the folder that opens is the one the spec and the drawing already promise, and a refusal withholds a sentence rather than adding a surface.

### 18.7 The address-opening service

*Added 2026-10-03, out of step 10c's map of the 38 promises (hole 6), ruled by the build runner. No auditor of this document has seen it.*

**K25. One service opens a build-fixed project `https` address in the user’s own browser.** The caller selects Source code or, only after the Creator chooses an address, Donate by name; it never supplies an address. No page, record or server can change the destinations. This is Section 21’s named service and A1 admits no other route. The shell’s browser-opening call is K20’s; the result says only that it asked, never that the page opened. Licence and third-party texts open inside Smoozy from bundled copies.

---

### About’s free-software notices

**Added 2026-10-26, carrying the Creator’s decision of 2026-10-04.** About reads the bundled GNU General Public License, version 3, and the generated third-party notices locally. The notice grants use under version 3 or any later version, names the Creator as copyright holder as on the website’s About page, states no warranty and the right to share and modify under the licence. Design owns the screen words. Source code and the optional Donate use K25; no donation address means no Donate control. The public source address is fixed in the build after the repository is chosen, never guessed by this plan pass.

Every desktop artifact carries the full licence and the notices for the code it distributes, including its server program; the release carries them too. The server package format is unchanged. Before 0.2.0 the builder reads the retained dependencies’ compatibility with GPL-3.0 from the FSF’s own list, and each artifact format’s own instructions for carrying notices; no compatibility claim is made here. No per-file headers are required by this plan. Release signing, notarization, update trust roots and the desk-placement shortcut are unchanged.

*Build detail, 2026-10-27, wave 4; no auditor has seen it.* Source code is fixed once at `src/the_address_opener.rs` to `https://github.com/a-kislyakov/Smoozy`; the copyright holder is Alexander Kislyakov and the year 2026, as ruled in the wave brief. The existing menu command accepts the closed About source action and calls K25; it never accepts a URL. About carries the two built-in texts as verbatim values, displayed through textContent. Build generates notices from each target's actual normal dependency tree, with Cargo metadata supplying package identities and local licence paths; tests, procedural macros and build-only tools are excluded. The machine program's Linux-musl tree is listed separately in each desktop's notices and alongside the server artifact at release. Licence files below bundled native sources are included. Where a registry package omits its licence text, checked-in supplements retain the upstream text, pinned source revision, source address, date and digest; they must match the package revision. Missing licence material fails the build. The generator lives under `App/Release/`; the existing packaging settings consume its output.

## 19. Build outputs, signing and hosting

**Constraint:** Row 280's second clause; Fact 74, answered on all three systems 2026-08-18; the whole-app *Security* concern's *"no account, no registration and no email anywhere in it"*; and `Preparation/Groundwork/Open Threads.md`, which gives this stage *"where an update comes from, how it is checked and how it lands"* and Build the doing.

### 19.1 What each system is built as

**E1. One codebase, three artifacts, and each carries what its own system requires before the application can keep a promise on it.**

**E2. macOS: a signed, notarized bundle carrying an identifier.** The identifier is the precondition Fact 73 found for posting a notification (T2). A `PrivacyInfo.xcprivacy` declaration remains required for any retained API whose documented notice applies; withdrawing the identity reader grants no blanket manifest deletion.

**D7b correction, 2026-10-26; no auditor has seen it. E3’s signing requirement below is superseded while no Windows certificate exists.** **Creator-stated:** the Creator holds no Microsoft developer licence, will not buy one, and chooses to ship Windows unsigned; Smoozy v1 updated unsigned and users force-opened its first launch. The artifact remains one per-user unpackaged executable, unelevated, with T3’s shortcut. E7’s certificate cost is not a release requirement under this choice. *(2026-11-01, [E7a](#192-signing-and-where-the-cost-lands): Windows now has a certificate route through SignPath Foundation at no cost. A release SignPath signs meets E3’s signing requirement; one it does not sign stays under this correction. No auditor has seen it.)*

**E3. Windows: a per-user unpackaged executable, signed, that never runs elevated**, with **a per-user Start-menu shortcut carrying an application identifier** (T3). The ordinary desktop application creates that shortcut in the user's Programs location at its first successful start; an installer and elevation are not required. Microsoft's worked route — *How to enable desktop toast notifications through an AppUserModelID*, `https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/shell/enable-desktop-toast-with-appusermodelid`, page dated 2018-05-31 and updated 2025-03-11, read 2026-08-22 — creates and installs the shortcut in application code: its step 1 *"composes the full path to the shortcut, determines that a shortcut of that name does not already exist at that location, and passes that information to another method which creates and installs the shortcut"*, with the path composed under the user's own `APPDATA` Start Menu Programs folder, and it says outright *"Note that the shortcut can be deployed per-user or per-app."* **The same page prefers the installer** — *"We strongly recommend that you do this in the Windows Installer rather than in your app's code"* — **which is a recommendation and not a precondition: the code it works is the application's own.** **That first-party answer closes the former unresolved item; it does not by itself move the shortcut to a future side-by-side version, which Z15a answers separately.**

**E4. Linux: a signed, dependency-declaring package for each supported user-computer combination, not an unqualified plain executable.** Each release-matrix row names the distribution and release, CPU architecture, libc family and minimum version, GTK runtime and WebKitGTK ABI; the package declares those as dependencies through that distribution's package mechanism. **The GTK and WebKitGTK boundary is the shell's own published requirement, and it is the provisional shell's — corrected 2026-08-22, out of the third sanity sweep's verification round.** The row's CPU, libc and distribution halves are the platform's and stand whatever [H4](#22-the-route-chosen-and-the-properties-it-is-held-to) settles; **the GTK and WebKitGTK halves are properties of Wails and are re-derived from Tauri's own publications if H5b selects it**, because a package declaring the wrong runtime libraries as dependencies is precisely the failure this shape exists to prevent. **H5b selected Tauri v2 on 2026-09-02 and the re-derivation was owed from that day. It was written on 2026-09-06, found at Build by [H16a](#25-the-rulings-stop-run-and-not-reached)'s re-walk of Row 282's Linux leg, and it is E4a below.** For four days this clause declared the withdrawn shell's dependency pair while its own sentence said it would not. **Nothing else in E4 moves**, and the pair itself turns out to be the same one. The requirement as Wails publishes it:: *"Linux requires the standard `gcc` build tools plus `libgtk3` and `libwebkit`"*, and the ABI split is named by package and build tag — *"If you are using latest Linux version (example: Ubuntu 24.04) and it is not supporting `libwebkit2gtk-4.0-dev`, then you might encounter an issue in `wails doctor`: `libwebkit` not found. To resolve this issue you can install `libwebkit2gtk-4.1-dev` and during your build use the tag `-tags webkit2_41`."* (`https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wailsapp/wails/master/website/docs/gettingstarted/installation.mdx`, read 2026-08-22; the same 4.1-not-4.0 split [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 23 reads off Canonical's own image manifests). **That a distribution's package mechanism enforces a declared dependency is the distribution's own policy**: Debian's, §7.2 on `Depends` — *"This declares an absolute dependency. A package will not be configured unless all of the packages listed in its `Depends` field have been correctly configured (unless there is a circular dependency as described above)"* (`https://www.debian.org/doc/debian-policy/ch-relationships.html`, read 2026-08-22); the parenthetical exception is for circular dependencies and does not reach a leaf application depending on a runtime library. The downloadable artifact and its signed manifest carry the row identifier. **An unlisted combination is not allowed to fail inside the web view or before Smoozy can explain it: the package transaction refuses it before launch.** Which user-computer combinations the product promises is not invented or narrowed here; the signed/package-enforced matrix must exactly implement that boundary once it is ruled. **It was ruled on 2026-10-24, and the rows are [E4b](#191-what-each-system-is-built-as).** Lead 18's rented-server boundary is separate and does not silently answer this desktop boundary. What every Linux artifact carries is the Smoozy signature [19.4](#194-what-is-verified-and-by-what) verifies, because the platform supplies dependency enforcement but not Smoozy's publisher trust anchor.

**E4a. The GTK and WebKitGTK halves re-derived from the selected shell's own publication, which is what E4's own sentence promised. Added 2026-09-06, found at Build. Label: documented, first-party, read 2026-09-06.**

**The selected shell publishes the pair its own bundler declares, as a property of the generated package rather than of a build machine**, which is the stronger of the two things E4 could have got. *Debian*, `https://v2.tauri.app/distribute/debian/`: *"The stock Debian package generated by the Tauri bundler has everything you need to ship your application to Debian-based Linux distributions, defining your application's icons, generating a Desktop file, and specifying the dependencies `libwebkit2gtk-4.1-0` and `libgtk-3-0`, along with `libappindicator3-1` if your app uses the system tray."* The same page states where a row adds to that list: *"If your app depends on additional system dependencies you can specify them in `tauri.conf.json > bundle > linux > deb`."*

**So the pair is `libgtk-3-0` and `libwebkit2gtk-4.1-0`, and it is the same GTK3 and WebKitGTK 4.1 pair every reading in this document already turns on.** Canonical's two manifests at [H16](#25-the-rulings-stop-run-and-not-reached) are read against exactly those package names, so **nothing that rested on the withdrawn shell's pair moves**: H13a's Linux bullet, [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 23's Linux limb and the release matrix's own row all stand as written. **The build requirement is published separately and is a build machine's rather than a user's** — *Prerequisites*, `https://v2.tauri.app/start/prerequisites/`, lists `libwebkit2gtk-4.1-dev` among the Debian system dependencies — and E4 is about what the shipped package declares, not what a build machine installs.

**Two things this does not cover, stated as silence.** **The tray dependency is conditional on a facility this document does not use**, and whether Smoozy's package carries `libappindicator3-1` follows from whether anything here asks for a system tray; nothing in this document does, and no row is decided on it here. **And only the Debian form is published this way** — no page read states the default dependency list for any other packaging format, so **a release-matrix row that is not a Debian-family package has no published pair and its dependencies are established by reading that format's own output rather than by carrying this one across.** **Nothing was rented and no machine was used to obtain either reading.**

**E4b. The release matrix's rows. Ruled 2026-10-24 by the build runner under the Creator's standing delegation, out of the step 11 map's H4; entered at Specs as `Whole app` *Getting the app, and updates*. No auditor has seen it.** E4 left the rows to *"once it is ruled"*. Each row is what the shell and the release machinery already build, at the higher of the shell's and the compiler's published floors; a row that could not be sourced is left out rather than guessed. Citations are in the sources file under E4b.

| Row | Processor (compiler target) | Lowest system | Libc | GTK / WebKitGTK |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **macOS** | Apple silicon only (`aarch64-apple-darwin`) | macOS 11.0 | — | — (system WebKit) |
| **Windows** | x86_64 only (`x86_64-pc-windows-msvc`) | Windows 10, every edition alike | — | — (WebView2, `embedBootstrapper`, item 65) |
| **Ubuntu 24.04 LTS** | x86_64 / `amd64` | that release | glibc ≥ 2.39 (has 2.39) | GTK 3, WebKitGTK 4.1 |
| **Ubuntu 26.04 LTS** | x86_64 / `amd64` | that release | glibc ≥ 2.39 (has 2.43) | GTK 3, WebKitGTK 4.1 |
| **Debian 13** | x86_64 / `amd64` | that release | glibc ≥ 2.39 (has 2.41) | GTK 3, WebKitGTK 4.1 |

- **Processors are the builds that exist.** The Mac artifact is built at the desk with no target named (`Preparation/Groundwork/Templates/Release Runner.md` step 3; `Audits/Build/Step 8a/Decisions record.md` §8 step 3), on a desk whose processor reads `arm64` and whose one toolchain is `stable-aarch64-apple-darwin` (observed 2026-10-24). Windows and Linux come from the hosted runners, x86_64 images both (`Preparation/Build/Shell measurement.md`, observed 2026-09-02). **Intel Macs, ARM Windows and ARM Linux are not rows**, because nothing builds for them; adding one is a build added to the release, not a reading.
- **x86_64 means any 64-bit Intel or AMD processor, with no instruction-set floor — added 2026-10-24, out of the Creator's eighteenth notes, note 3**: until that day the HTTPS client's cryptography (`rustls-graviola`) needed `aes`, `pclmulqdq`, `bmi1`, `adx`, `avx` and `avx2`, so a Windows or Linux PC without them could not fetch or check anything and a rented server without them could not download Blender, and it is now `rustls`'s `ring` provider, which asks for nothing past the SSE and SSE2 every x86-64 build already assumes (documented, its own source; sources file under E4b). No auditor has seen this sentence.
- **macOS 11.0** is the Rust target's published floor (*"ARM64 macOS (11.0+, Big Sur+)"*), above Tauri's *"macOS Catalina (10.15) and later"*. **The bundle declares nothing today and the bundler's default is 10.13** (`Audits/Build/Step 10a/The narrowing mechanism across the three engines.md`), so the artifact must declare 11.0.
- **Windows 10** is the Rust target's published floor (*"64-bit MSVC (Windows 10+, Windows Server 2016+)"*), above Tauri's *"Windows 7 and later"*. **No page read separates editions**, so no row narrows by edition; Windows 11's Smart App Control block on an unsigned file (E8a) is a cost on this row, not a narrowing of it.
- **Linux rows are the releases whose own package pages publish `libwebkit2gtk-4.1-0` for `amd64` and whose `libc6` meets what the built artifact asks for** — `GLIBC_2.39`, read off the released Linux desktop artifact (`Audits/Build/Step 10c/Piece 11 — the sixth release.md`). **Ubuntu 22.04 and Debian 12 publish the web view and fail the C library** (2.35 and 2.36), so they are out while the artifact is built on Ubuntu 24.04. **Interim Ubuntu releases are not rows**: the series list shows 24.10, 25.04 and 25.10 already *Obsolete*, and a row that lapses within a year is a row the matrix would have to drop again. Debian testing and unstable are not releases.
- **One thing the pages show that E4a does not: on all three Linux rows the GTK 3 library is published as `libgtk-3-0t64`, not `libgtk-3-0`**, and Debian's own `libwebkit2gtk-4.1-0` in trixie depends on `libgtk-3-0t64 (>= 3.23.0)`. **Whether a package that declares `libgtk-3-0`, as E4a's bundler default does, installs on these rows is not established** by any page read — Debian's page for `libgtk-3-0t64` shows no relation to the old name. The artifact for these rows declares the name each row publishes, or its declared dependencies are read against each row's package index before it ships.

**E5. The artifacts are compared against Fact 96's figures at their own date and not against a remembered number** (H2). *(Restored 2026-10-24 by the build runner: step 11's plan pass, inserting E4b above it, cut this clause's opening words by mistake.)*

### 19.2 Signing, and where the cost lands

**E6. macOS: signing and notarization are both documented requirements for a download that runs without the system refusing it.** Apple's notarization service explicitly removes the macOS dependency for **that step**, but **no Apple page read documents signing off a Mac** — **so a Mac stays in the pipeline**, and the build arrangement Build writes has to have one.

**E7. Windows is where the cost lands, and it is an identity gate rather than an engineering problem.** Microsoft's managed signing programme is documented as open to individual developers **located in the United States or Canada only**; organizations need *"a verifiable tax history of three or more years"*; a paid subscription is mandatory; validation takes *"from 1 to 20 business days"* with three document-upload attempts before the door closes; **and the two Microsoft country lists genuinely disagree with each other**, reproduced on both runs. **The fallback is an ordinary certificate at "$300–500/year".**

**E7a. The Windows certificate is SignPath Foundation’s, through its free route for open-source projects. Added 2026-11-01, out of the Creator’s twenty-sixth notes (Want 1), entered at Specs as `Whole app` *Getting the app, and updates*. Creator-stated. No auditor has seen it.** E7’s identity gate and its price do not land on the Creator: the certificate is issued to SignPath Foundation and costs nothing. Documented, SignPath’s own terms (citations in the sources file under *E7a*): *"The code signing certificate is issued to SignPath Foundation. This means that SignPath Foundation is the publisher of the OSS project."* **So Windows names SignPath Foundation as the publisher wherever it names one.**

- **Acceptance is SignPath’s call.** Its terms: *"it can only be our decision to accept or reject a project"*, and for a downloadable program *"we require a certain verifiable reputation"*. **Until it accepts, and for every Windows release it does not sign, D7b’s unsigned route stands as written.**
- **The released Windows file is built and signed on GitHub’s hosted runners, from the public source repository, never at the desk.** Origin verification ties each signature to the repository, branch, commit and build job, and for open-source projects the GitHub connector checks that *"All jobs of the GitHub worfklow leading up to the signing request were executed on GitHub-hosted agents"*. So it is built in the public repository the Release Runner exports to, at the release’s tag, after that export — not in the private working repository. The repository is public before SignPath is asked.
- **Every signing is approved by hand** — *"Every release needs manual approval for signing."* The approver is the Creator, so a Windows release waits on that act.
- **E15’s length and digest are the signed file’s.** Signing writes into the executable, so the manifest is composed from the file SignPath returns, never from the unsigned build. E3’s per-user executable, its shortcut and its application identifier do not change.
- **Every signed file carries the product name Smoozy and the release’s version**, which SignPath enforces: *"Set all product name attributes to your project’s name. Set all product version attributes to the same value in each build."*
- **What SignPath asks around the file.** A *"Code signing policy"* on the home page and on download and release pages, carrying *"Free code signing provided by SignPath.io, certificate by SignPath Foundation"*, the team’s roles, and a privacy policy or SignPath’s fixed sentence; instructions for removing software that installs; and multi-factor sign-in on GitHub and SignPath for everyone on the team.
- **Two things to name in the application rather than assume.** `webpki-roots`, the root-certificate list inside the HTTPS client, is under CDLA-Permissive-2.0, which is not OSI-approved (SPDX list 3.29.0), against *"an OSI-approved Open Source license without commercial dual-licensing for all components"*. And the update check sends the version and system to GitHub, a system the user did not name, where the terms ask software that *"collects user data and transfers it to systems not specified by the user"* to show a privacy policy at installation and offer a way to switch it off. Whether either counts is SignPath’s to say.

**What it changes in the update route.** E16’s D7b rule already reads the running build’s own standing, so **from the first signed release a signed copy updates only to a signed build** — the protection E16 exists for. **What that costs:** SignPath may *"pause or terminate the subscription without prior notice"* and *"revoke the certificate effective immediately or retroactively"*. If it stops signing Smoozy, signed copies stay on their version until a release is signed again, by SignPath or under any other certificate the platform call accepts; the call names no publisher. The user keeps a working copy, so this costs the Creator and breaks no promise. Whether a revoked certificate also stops installed copies from starting is not established (`Preparation/Groundwork/Open Threads.md`, *Windows signing through SignPath*).

**E8. And a signature does not buy silence.** Microsoft's own words: *"Even when signed, a newly created binary could still show a SmartScreen warning until its hash or publisher certificate accumulates sufficient evidence of positive reputation"*, taking *"several weeks and hundreds of clean installs from a wide audience"*, with *"no need (or mechanism) to manually submit a file for SmartScreen reputation review for consumer endpoints"*, and **extended-validation certificates no longer bypass it**. **Quotation restored 2026-08-22, out of the first sanity sweep's verification round: this stopped at *"manually submit a file"* and added *for review*, dropping the qualifier that scopes the sentence.** The same page, four lines below, names a route that is real and is not Smoozy's — *"Enterprise IT administrators may optionally submit files for review via the Microsoft Security Intelligence portal. This can accelerate trust for internal or managed deployments."* (read 2026-08-20) — **and Smoozy is a consumer download from its own website, so the portal is not a route here and the truncated form invited the next reader to think it was.** **At this project's scale the documentation says the warning does not go away on its own.** **It is checked against [Method](Method.md)'s third escalation in [Section 23](#23-moments-that-reached-the-creator) and the verdict is recorded there rather than absorbed here.**

**E8a. On Windows 11 the cost is not always a warning the user clicks through — it can be a block, and this clause used to state only the warning. Added 2026-08-22, out of the first sanity sweep's `raw-facts-head` finding, which reached the audit trail on 2026-08-20 and had never reached this document.** Microsoft's own words, in the same page E8 quotes: *"On Windows 11 devices, the Smart App Control feature may supersede SmartScreen Application Reputation. Smart App Control will block execution of unsigned files unless the file has a positive reputation. Smart App Control signature checks apply to all executable files, not just those downloaded from the Internet."*

**Three things follow and each is a different kind of cost.**

- **The ending is different in kind.** A SmartScreen warning is a thing the user gets past; **a block is not a warning at all** — there is no *run anyway* in it. So the Windows cost E8 and [Section 23](#23-moments-that-reached-the-creator) weigh is *warned, and clicks through* on one machine and *cannot start the application* on another, and the second is not a worse version of the first.
- **The trigger is not the download.** The last sentence removes the mark-of-the-web framing the rest of this section rests on: the checks *"apply to all executable files, not just those downloaded from the Internet"*, so an update the application places for itself is inside the boundary as much as the first download is.
- **What escapes it is reputation or a trusted signature, and which of the two Smoozy has is not settled by any page read.
