Narrowed 2026-08-23; this bullet concluded that what escapes the block is reputation and not signing alone, which neither quoted sentence supports.** The condition quoted above names *unsigned files unless the file has a positive reputation*, and the Smart App Control overview quoted below states that in enforcement mode *"Apps cannot be run unless they are recognized by Microsoft's app intelligence services, or they are signed with a trusted certificate."* **E3's Windows artifact is signed**, so it is outside the class the first sentence names and inside the exception the second names — **if the ordinary certificate E7 prices is one Smart App Control treats as trusted, and no page read says which certificates it treats that way.** **Reputation is not the route out at this project's scale**: E8's own quotations say it *"can take several weeks and hundreds of clean installs from a wide audience"*, with *"no need (or mechanism) to manually submit a file for SmartScreen reputation review for consumer endpoints"* *(quotation restored 2026-08-22 with E8's, which carried the same truncation)*. **So the block's reachability turns on the certificate question and not on scale**, and no Windows machine has been rented to settle it by trying.

**What is not established, and it bounds all of the above:** how many machines have the feature in enforcement. Microsoft's word is *may supersede*, and **no Windows machine has been rented to read it on** — which is [Section 22](#22-unresolved)'s standing hardware gap.

**But *unestablished* on its own was too weak, and the first-party words that narrow it belong here. Added 2026-08-23, out of `Facts.md` Fact 97, which has carried them since 2026-08-20 while this clause carried an unsourced paraphrase of them.** The paraphrase read *"the feature is off on an upgraded machine and evaluates itself on a clean install"*, attributed to the page E8 quotes, which does not say it. Microsoft's own words, on its own page — *Smart App Control*, `https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/apps/develop/smart-app-control/overview`, page dated 2025-11-18, read 2026-08-20: *"In most cases Smart App Control automatically turns on"*; *"it can only be enabled on a clean install of a version of Windows that contains the Smart App Control feature"*; and in enforcement mode, *"Apps cannot be run unless they are recognized by Microsoft's app intelligence services, or they are signed with a trusted certificate."*

**So three things are documented where one paraphrase stood.** The feature is **not opt-in** — in most cases it turns itself on. It is **gated on a clean install**, so a machine upgraded to Windows 11 does not have it and cannot be given it. And what enforcement mode admits — recognition by Microsoft's services **or** a trusted signature — is the vendor's own sentence rather than a reading across from the SmartScreen page. **Two things are still not established, and the third bullet above carries the second of them.** The **number**: what share of Windows 11 machines both reached the feature by a clean install and sit in enforcement, which no page read states. And **whether the certificate E7 prices is one the feature treats as trusted**, which no page read states either. **So the ending exists on a machine that has the feature in enforcement and whose signature it does not accept, and neither the frequency nor the acceptance is a thing anybody on this project has read or run.** [Section 23](#23-moments-that-reached-the-creator) weighs the moment; this clause exists so that the moment is weighed against a block and not only against a warning.

**Source: two first-party pages rather than one, corrected 2026-08-23.** This line read *"the same first-party page E8 quotes"*, singular, which was already false of the enablement claim the clause was making. The supersede sentence is that page, read 2026-08-20; the enablement and enforcement sentences are the Smart App Control overview page addressed above, read the same day. The evidence is at `Audits/Architecture/The user's own computer/fact-74.md`.

**Narrowed again 2026-11-01, with [E7a](#192-signing-and-where-the-cost-lands); no auditor has seen it.** The Smart App Control page now says which certificate it admits (page dated 2026-09-30, read 2026-11-01): *"If the app intelligence service is unable to make a prediction, then Smart App Control will still allow an app to run if it is signed with a certificate issued by a certificate authority (CA) within the Trusted Root Program."* **So under E7a the block turns on whether SignPath Foundation’s issuing authority is in that programme.** No page read names that authority; the first signed file’s certificate chain shows it, and reading it needs no Windows. Whether a Windows 11 desktop with the feature on then runs Smoozy is not established, no desktop Windows being reachable. **SmartScreen is unchanged in kind.** Microsoft’s table gives a signed file *"Warning — app flagged as unrecognized until reputation accumulates; verified publisher name is displayed"*, and the name is SignPath Foundation’s. No page read says whether that certificate brings reputation from other projects’ files; SignPath’s *"No more installation warnings."* is its own claim, not Microsoft’s. Both are parked in `Preparation/Groundwork/Open Threads.md`, *Windows signing through SignPath*.

**E9. Tauri is the only one of the six that addresses cross-platform signing head-on**, and its own words are that macOS signing needs an Apple device and that cross-compiled Windows installers need an external tool and are *"not tested as much"*. **That is not a reason to prefer it over Wails, and the reason it is not is worth stating**: what it documents is the platforms' own behaviour, which E6 and E7 already hold **from Apple's and Microsoft's own pages** rather than from any framework's. **A framework that names the problem does not remove it, and every other candidate's silence is not evidence that it is absent.** So it is carried because it is what the build arrangement will meet, and it excludes nobody.

### 19.3 Hosting, and what does not go up with a request

**E10. The artifacts and one signed version manifest are served from Smoozy's own hosting over HTTPS, and there is nothing else there.** No account, no registration, no email — **so an update cannot be fetched against an identity, and none is offered.**

**E10a. Where the desktop finds its update file, and a test copy never looks. Added 2026-10-24 by step 11's plan pass; no auditor has seen it.** E10's *Smoozy's own hosting* is the public repository `Server protocol and transfers` B8a names, where every release already publishes the desktop artifacts (`Audits/Build/Step 8a/Decisions record.md` §7 and §8). **The desktop's signed update file — E15's manifest with E19's key statements — is one more file in each release, under its own name, and the desktop reads it from [B8b](Server%20protocol%20and%20transfers.md#5-the-bootstrap-and-repair-request-and-package-inspection)'s fixed latest address on that repository and from nowhere else.** Its file name is Build's, and it is never the server package's release file. It is signed under E19's set and never by the server-package release root, so two files sharing a repository share no trust (`State, identity and recovery` J10). **B8d's own-release address is not this file's**: B8d keeps a desktop on its own release's server package, and the update check is the one read that has to see a newer release. **A throwaway test copy** (`App/Build a test copy.sh`, identifier `dev.smoozy.throwaway.test`) **never checks for an update**, so a test copy can never replace itself with a real release.

**E11. Nothing about the user goes up with an update request.** No computer name, no login account name, no server, no path, no count. **The request says which version is running and which system it is running on, because a manifest cannot be answered without them, and it says nothing else.**

**E12. That the platforms' own verification is compatible with the no-account promise is documented on two of three.** Apple states its download checks *"have never included the user's Apple Account or the identity of their device"*; Windows' signature-verification call can be made with a value documented as meaning *"There is no interactive user"*. **Linux's verification is Smoozy's own and carries nothing by construction.**

**E13. Hosting cost falls on the Creator rather than on the user.**

**E14. GitHub Actions is the Creator's stated intention for the pipeline and is an intention rather than a decision**, and **Build owns doing it**. Nothing above depends on which tool runs it.

### 19.4 What is verified, and by what

**E15. The signed manifest and artifact are verified before either is used, and a verification that does not pass is an update that did not happen.** The signed manifest carries a monotonically increasing unsigned 64-bit `release_sequence`, the human version, the exact artifact digest, platform/package row, artifact length, and signing-key identifier. The desktop stores the highest accepted sequence in the local record before publication and refuses every manifest below it — **and that stored sequence is only as good as the signed statement retained beside it**, which is verified before it refuses anything and discarded with it where it is absent, malformed or will not verify ([State, identity and recovery](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#11-on-the-users-computer) §1.1, added 2026-08-22). **Without that, a sequence that read too high refused every genuine manifest for good and in silence**, since a refused manifest is an update that did not happen. A publisher signature over an older but genuine artifact therefore cannot downgrade a copy. Not a partial replacement, not a retry into the same place, and not a fallback that skips the check. *(Added 2026-10-24 by step 11's D4, which found the running build's own release named nowhere in the code. **A build names its own release by its package version** — the one value the release stamps into every build, and the one `Server protocol and transfers` B8d already names a build's release by. **So a completed check has found a newer version when the verified manifest's human version is above the running build's package version**, the two compared part by part as whole numbers. A manifest naming the running version, or a lower one, is a check that found nothing newer, and a version that does not read as whole numbers is never taken as newer. **The release sequence keeps its whole job** — the stored high-water and the floor above — **and is never compared with the running build, which carries none.** What this rests on: every release carries a higher package version than the one before it, which every release so far has done and B8d's one tag per version already requires to differ. No auditor has seen it.)*

**D7b correction, 2026-10-26; no auditor has seen it. E16’s Windows half below is superseded.** **On Windows Smoozy’s signed update manifest, including the executable’s exact length and digest, is the update trust anchor.** The running executable’s standing is read once per application run through the same `ChecksTheProgram` adapter and `WinVerifyTrust` call used for the new executable, with E12’s value. Only a running executable that passes that call requires the new one to pass it too. A running executable that does not pass may accept an unsigned executable vouched for by the signed manifest. Length and digest must pass before any candidate is offered to the platform check; neither branch bypasses them. macOS and Linux keep their existing rules. E8a’s possible execution block remains; this change grants no Windows policy exception.

**E16. Per system.** **macOS: Smoozy's manifest signature and the operating system's signature and notarization checks over the complete bundle.** **Windows: Smoozy's manifest signature and the platform's signature-verification call**, made with the no-interactive-user value of E12. **Linux: the manifest and artifact signatures against E19's versioned key set.** On every system, the digest and length are checked against the signed manifest before platform verification; HTTPS is transport and never the update trust anchor.

**E17. Nothing rests on the self-update library Fact 74 read.** Its page is copyrighted to 2022, three of the four claims first quoted from it appear **nowhere on it**, its promise never to overwrite before *"all validation mechanisms report success"* covers **integrity only** because it states signature validation is **not implemented**, and the flags it says to respect are ones it says still need defining. **It is not used.**

**E18. The staged artifact is on the same filesystem as what it will replace**, so [6.5](#65-the-publication-operation-and-what-would-demonstrate-it)'s operation is available to the replacement in [Section 20](#20-update-verification-and-replacement). **On macOS, before publication, the complete staged bundle must carry the same Developer ID team as the running copy, read and verified through the system’s own code-signing tools; a running copy without a team never publishes an update.** *(Added 2026-10-25, step 11 D5b, a hole ruled by the build runner under the Creator’s standing delegation: **observed in D5’s test**, Gatekeeper with assessments disabled accepts an ad-hoc bundle (`Audits/Build/Step 11/D5, the Mac update route — what landed.md`). Documented: `codesign(1)` display and requirement verification, and Apple TN3127’s Developer ID requirement; citations in the companion sources file under *E18, the running copy’s Developer ID team*. No auditor has seen it.)*

**D7b clarification, 2026-10-26; no auditor has seen it.** E19’s Linux compromise consequence also applies to an unsigned running Windows build: a stolen authorised update key can authenticate a malicious Windows update as it can a Linux update. Windows adds E16’s platform requirement only when the running executable itself passes it. Read E19’s final Windows sentence with that condition; macOS’s independent requirement remains.

**E19. Every build carries a versioned Smoozy update-manifest trust set; on Linux it is also the artifact-signature trust set.** Each build embeds **two** offline recovery public keys and the current accepted online signing-key set, each key carrying an identifier and activation sequence. *(Two from 2026-10-24, by step 11's D3, on the build runner's ruling of that day on the Creator's word — *"On update keys — honestly, I have no idea what that even means, so you decide something reasonable."* — recorded in `Audits/Architecture/Desktop app and delivery/Decisions record.md`: two recovery keys from the first build, either one able to sign a new trust set, each kept by the Creator in a different place, one of them offline. It read ~~an offline recovery public key~~. **Wherever this clause says *the offline recovery key* below, it means either of the two, and either one alone is enough.** A statement can revoke a recovery key as well as an online key; it is never admitted under a recovery key it revokes or that an accepted statement has revoked, and a revocation, once accepted, is carried by every later statement. So losing one key no longer stops updates, and a stolen one is revoked by a statement the other signs. **What two keys do not buy, said so it is not read as more:** one key is still enough to sign, so a copy that meets a thief's statement before the revocation can accept it, and on Linux that is enough to authorise an update (item 41's compromise half). No auditor has seen it.)* A normal transition manifest is signed by the current and next online keys; its key statement is also signed by the offline recovery key and is retained on hosting indefinitely, so a copy that missed the overlap can move directly from any shipped set to the current one. A loss statement activates a prepared next key under the recovery signature. A compromise statement, signed by the offline recovery key, revokes the compromised identifier at a monotonically increasing trust-sequence and activates a replacement; a revoked key cannot authorize its own recovery. The client stores the highest accepted trust-sequence and refuses an older statement even when its signature is otherwise valid. **The offline recovery private key never resides in the service, hosting or build runner.** A build that cannot form a chain from its embedded recovery public key does not verify and therefore does not update. The manifest never supplies an unanchored key. macOS and Windows additionally apply their platform checks in E16; those checks do not replace the monotonic Smoozy manifest.

---

## 20. Update verification and replacement

**Constraint:** Row 280's first clause, quoted at the head of this document; Fact 74, answered on all three systems; and [The shape](The%20shape.md#the-users-computer) — *"the updater may use a short-lived helper when the running application cannot replace itself, with that helper's platform-specific lifecycle"* this document's. **The promise is reachable on all three systems and not by one mechanism.**

### 20.1 What the application does, and what the user does

**Z1. The application checks, fetches, verifies and installs, and the user does none of those things.** *"The user is not sent back to the website to fetch a newer copy, and updating is not something they have to remember: the app does it."* **The only trip to the website is the first one.** *(**Read narrower 2026-08-29, per Row 508, by this note and by nothing in the sentence**: from 2026-08-25 the spec permits the user to **ask for a check**, so **the user may cause one of the four acts and still performs none of them**. The application checks, fetches, verifies and installs exactly as before, by one mechanism, whoever asked — [Z26](#201-what-the-application-does-and-what-the-user-does). **The sentence above is untouched, word for word, and Z3's break test is untouched with it.** Corrected at the re-gate repair of 2026-08-29: this note read *"Narrowed 2026-08-29, per Row 508, and narrowed in one word only"* and *"The sentence is **otherwise** untouched"*, both of which assert that a word of Z1 moved. **None did** — what was added on 2026-08-29 was this parenthetical, so the clause stands as written and is read narrower by a dated gloss.)*

**Z2. What the user sees or is asked at that moment is Design's**, by that promise's own words, and this document decides nothing about it beyond what the platforms force.

**Z3. Row 280 breaks only if the user must independently discover, fetch, or initiate the update, or must remember to perform it later** — the shape's own statement of what the promise costs. **A prompt during an update the application itself initiated is Design's, bounded by the platform fact and the security rules**, and is not a break.

**Z4. The check happens while the application is running and never otherwise**, because there is no daemon. An update the application never had a chance to notice is not a promise broken; it is the same bound Row 284 already carries.

**Z21. What a successful start is, and what detects a failed one and performs the rollback on each system.** A successful start is the smallest observable that says the new version ran coherently: it verified its own release identity, ran or accepted the migration, opened the record, composed the first module and returned a one-use success token to the actor that launched it. Anything short of that before the bounded launch deadline is a failed start.

- **Windows:** Z19's relaunch helper starts the new build and watches for the token. On failure it restores L8's record copy, rewrites Z15a's shortcut to the immediately previous known-good build and relaunches that build.
- **macOS:** the still-running old application replaces the complete bundle, starts the new bundle in P2's probe mode and watches for the token. On failure it restores the complete named bundle backup and L8's record copy, then starts that previous bundle.
- **Linux:** the still-running old application asks the package mechanism to install the new signed package, starts it in P2's probe mode and watches for the token. On failure it asks the same mechanism to reinstall the one cached, verified immediately previous package and restores L8's record copy before starting it.

**Z21a. A probe is written down before publication, because the actor watching it can die. Added 2026-08-22, out of the third sanity sweep's verification round; until now the rollback existed only as an act of a live actor, and nothing recorded that one was owed.** The old process is an ordinary desktop application: the user may quit it, the machine may lose power, it may crash. On macOS and Linux it is also the only watcher, and by the time it is watching, **publication has already happened** — the bundle at the installed path is the new one, or the package transaction has completed.

- **What is written, and when.** Immediately **before** the publication step, and durably ([5](#5-the-local-records-database-and-its-migrations)): an **update-in-probe record** naming the release identity being published, the retained immediately previous artifact (the named bundle backup on macOS, the cached verified package on Linux, the retained per-version folder on Windows), and L8's record copy. It is written by the old process, which is still the sole record writer at that moment (P2).
- **What clears it.** The success token, at the same moment the old process exits and the probe owns the instance lock; or a completed rollback, whose last act it is.
- **What retention now hangs on.** Z7's named backup and Z11's cached previous package are removed when the record clears, rather than on the token alone. Where a rollback was owed and performed, the removal is the rollback's.
- **What a launch that finds one does.** An ordinary launch that finds an uncleared record **naming its own release identity** has, by getting that far, done everything Z21 calls a successful start except being launched by a watcher — it verified its release identity, ran or accepted the migration, opened the record and is composing its first module — so **it completes the probe itself and clears the record**. A launch that finds a record naming **a different** release identity is the retained previous build running after a rollback that did not finish; it completes that rollback's remaining acts and clears the record.
- **What this does not close, and it is [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 46.** A new build that cannot start at all never reaches the record, so on macOS and Linux nothing rolls it back — the user meets a build that will not start. **What the record buys even there** is that the previous artifact and the record copy are still on the machine and still named, so the route back exists rather than having been swept.

**Rollback is a local recovery exception, not an accepted downgrade.** It can select only the immediately previous artifact retained by the update being attempted, only after that new artifact failed this launch probe, and only before any ordinary work by the new build — the probe's one record write before the token is [5.2](#52-migrations)'s migration under P2's handover, and L13's restore of L8's copy undoes it. It never fetches an older manifest, never accepts a manifest below E15's high-water, never lowers the stored release sequence and never makes the restored version eligible for a future manifest below that high-water. A manual request to install an older genuine version is refused.

**Z21b. A launch in probe mode performs the run-mark's step-1 read and write and makes no crash offer, and that is the launch side's half of `Reporting a problem` U5a. Added 2026-08-29 at the stage-close repair, routed here by that document's [§12](Reporting%20a%20problem.md#12-unresolved) item 10 — probe mode is this document's, so only this document can say that a probe-mode launch carries the suppression, and its item 10 is closed on this side.**

- **What the probe does with the mark.** [Reporting a problem](Reporting%20a%20problem.md#2-the-run-identity-and-the-run-mark) U3 step 1 entire: the probe reads the field and then writes it durably as *(running, the probe's own identity)*, as sole writer after P2's lock handoff — **and it makes no offer on what it read**. That suppression is [U5a](Reporting%20a%20problem.md#2-the-run-identity-and-the-run-mark)'s, taken here by name; the whole exception, its width and its grounds are stated there and are not restated here.
- **Why the clause exists at all: without it the suppression has no ordinary-case half.** Z21 starts the new bundle or the new package in P2's probe mode while the old run is still alive, and **nothing writes *clean* in a handover** — U3 step 2 is written at the user's ask to quit and no user asks in a handover, U3 step 3 forbids a write on the way out. So the field the probe reads holds *(running, the old run's identity)*, and on C1's trigger alone **every ordinary successful update would open the crash box** about a run in which nothing crashed. That box is the one interruption C2 admits, and this is the path that would raise it systematically.
- **It reaches probe-mode launches and the build a rollback starts (the last bullet), and no others.** *(Widened 2026-10-24 by step 11's plan pass, with the last bullet; it read ~~It reaches probe-mode launches and no others.~~)* An ordinary launch keeps C1's trigger exactly as written, including [Z21a](#201-what-the-application-does-and-what-the-user-does)'s ordinary launch that finds an uncleared probe record and completes the probe itself — that launch was not started in probe mode and is not covered. **A crash of the probe's own run is offered at the next ordinary launch like any other** (U5a), the probe having overwritten the field with its own identity.
- **The build a rollback starts, on all three systems, is started in the update-launch mode and makes no offer — the launch side's half of [U5b](Reporting%20a%20problem.md#2-the-run-identity-and-the-run-mark).** The update-launch mode is the one mark the update mechanism puts on a launch it starts, and nothing else passes it: the probe is that mode with Z21's watch, and the build a rollback starts is that mode alone, performing U3 step 1's read and write as the ordinary running application. *(Replaced 2026-10-24 by step 11's plan pass, closing `Reporting a problem` §12 item 11. It read: ~~**The rollback branch is unchanged and takes no clause of its own.** L13's restore of L8's pre-migration copy puts back the old run's own mark — *(running, the old run's identity)* — the old process is still the running application, and it ends its run through U3 step 2 as usual.~~ Its ground was false on Windows, where Z18 and Z19's helper does the rollback after the old process has gone, and on every system Z21 ends a rollback by starting a build that reads L13's restored copy — so a builder following it shipped a crash box after every failed update.)*

**Z22. The update fetch draws from the download budget and is admitted at the desktop point** ([11.3](#113-the-one-desktop-side-admission-point) R11). It is bytes over the user's own line that Smoozy causes, so a fetch outside the budget would ignore a cap the user set while a render's dependencies uploaded beside it. **And it is admitted rather than merely capped, which is R7's own argument**: with the budget and admission apart, *"admission would let in channels the budget cannot feed, and the floor would be a wish"* — so a draw that bypassed admission would take R6's floor with it, and with the floor goes the thing `Server protocol and transfers` C19's inactivity deadline sits against.

**Z23. What triggers the check: application start, and one repeating check while the application runs.** Row 280's whole content is that the user does not have to remember, which requires something to trigger it, and **a start-only check leaves a user who keeps Smoozy open across a long render on an old build until they think to restart** — the ordinary use of this application, and the shape Row 280 forbids. **The interval is Build's to pick and this document names no number**, which is R14's own discipline everywhere else. **P10 records that this is the one timer on this side and why it is not a poll in §8.4's sense** — it refreshes no condition's observed value, withdraws no record and reaches no machine.

**Z23a. The last-completed-check moment, and About says a moment and never an age. Added 2026-08-25 at the tenth sitting, per Row 482; the behaviour is this clause's and the durable value is not.** When Z23's check **completes** — it reached the manifest and got an answer, a newer version found or none — the moment is written durably, on the desktop clock and stated as such; a check that could not be reached writes nothing, takes the ordinary failure shape, and leaves the moment what it was, which is exactly what *when it was last checked* claims. **The value's inventory row is `State, identity and recovery` §1.1's, being written in this same sitting, and is not added here** (L1). **About reads the stored moment and presents the moment, never an interval**: *last checked three hours ago* is arithmetic on the undefended desktop clock — T19's own ground, and a clock set back would make it negative — while the moment itself is D5's stated allowance, *the desktop's clock is used for presentation and for nothing a promise rests on*. The four readings' wording is Design's (Z2); what is bounded here is the value supplied and that no age is derived from it.

**Z26. The user may ask for a check, and it is Z23's own act reached a second way. Added 2026-08-29, per Row 508; the spec's clause entered on 2026-08-25 and no trigger list on this side carried it.** *"**They may also ask for a check themselves**, which is the same act reached a second way rather than a thing they have to do; nothing is promised differently if they never use it."* *(The spec's own emphasis restored 2026-08-29 at the re-gate repair; the quotation was word-perfect and dropped the bold.)*

- **The trigger list is three and no wider**: application start, the one repeating check while the application runs (Z23), and **one act of the user's, at the entry point Design draws**. Nothing else starts a check.
- **P10 and P10b are untouched.** The user's act fires with something under way by construction — their own press — starts no standing sequence and is not a schedule of its own, so **the repeating check is still the only standing timer on this side** and this clause adds no second one.
- **What it starts is the same act, not a second route to the same end.** Every clause binding Z23's check binds this one identically and by construction: **E11** (nothing about the user goes up — no count; the request says which version is running and which system, and nothing else), E15 and E16's verification, **Z22**'s budget and admission point, E15's release-sequence refusal, and [20.2](#202-macos-replace-the-complete-signed-bundle) to [20.4](#204-windows-side-by-side-and-never-in-place)'s per-system replacement including Z10a's Linux authentication. **There is one check and one update mechanism, and this clause adds a caller.**
- **Row 280 neither widens nor narrows**, on **Z3**'s own test: the user still must not independently discover, fetch or initiate the update, or remember to perform it later, and the spec's own clause says *"nothing is promised differently if they never use it"* — so the act is **permitted rather than required**, and a user who never presses it gets exactly what Z23 already gives them. **Where the act sits, and what it reads while it runs, are Design's** (Z2, Row 482).

**Z27. What a user-asked check does when something is already under way: it joins it, and never starts a second.**

- **A check already in flight**, whichever route asked for it: the user's ask **coalesces onto it**, and that check's completion is the user's answer. Two concurrent requests for one manifest would draw twice from the download budget (Z22) for one answer, and would let the user's reading be the older of two readings of the same file.
- **An update already past its check** — fetching, verifying, or inside [Z21a](#201-what-the-application-does-and-what-the-user-does)'s probe: **no check is started at all**, and what the user is told is the update already under way. **The wording is Design's** (Z2).
- **A completed check resets the repeating check's interval, whichever route asked for it.** The next automatic check falls one interval after the **end** of the last completed check, measured from its end and never from its start — [P10d](#33-ui-event-delivery)'s own pacing rule applied here. Without this a check the user asked for would be followed by an automatic one moments later, fetching the same manifest twice for one answer. **The interval is still Build's and this document names no number** (Z23).

**Z28. A user-asked check writes the last-completed-check moment, and Z23a's trigger widens to any completed check.**

- Z23a's *"When **Z23's** check completes"* is **read as any completed check, whichever route asked for it** *(widened 2026-08-29, per Row 508. Z23a was written on 2026-08-25, when Z23's pair was the whole trigger list; on its words as written a user-asked check wrote no moment, so the user would press the item, watch the check resolve, and read an About line naming an earlier one — the one reading Row 482 exists to make true, made false in exactly the case the user caused.)*
- **Nothing else in Z23a moves**: the moment is the desktop's own clock and stated as such, About presents a moment and never an age on T19's and D5's grounds, and the durable value's inventory row is `State, identity and recovery` [§1.1](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#11-on-the-users-computer) I4's and is not added here (L1).
- **What this clause does not touch, and it is [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 55**: whether a check that *could not be reached* writes the moment. **The two documents now say the same thing.** Z23a says such a check writes nothing and leaves the moment what it was, and `State, identity and recovery` [§1.1](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#11-on-the-users-computer) I4's first bullet reads *"**A check that could not be reached writes nothing and leaves the moment as it was**"*. This clause widens **which route** and moves **which outcome** in neither direction. **The ruling is recorded at item 55 — Z23a was the correct side, I4 was the side that moved, and both ends closed on 2026-08-29.** *(Corrected at the re-gate repair of 2026-08-29. This bullet quoted I4's superseded wording — *"any of the four, a check that could not be reached included"* — in the present tense as a live disagreement, and closed **"what is owed is the other document's and is routed rather than repaired from this side"**; I4's bullet had been corrected to Z23a's reading earlier the same day. **Nothing in this clause, in Z23a or in the ruling changes** — only the record of a seam that is now shut.)*

**Z29. On Linux, a user-asked check offers the box for a version the declined mark names, and Z10b's suppression is of the automatic route alone.**

- **Z10b's own stated ground is that nothing nags the user** (Z10a, and the spec's *"nothing nags them"*). **A box drawn because the user pressed for a check is not a nag.** And the alternative is a dead end: a user-asked check that found the version they had declined and then offered nothing would resolve to Row 482's *a new version found* with nothing behind it, on a control the user pressed deliberately.
- **So the mark is not read on this route.** The box is offered for any version newer than the running one. **A dismissal there writes the mark exactly as Z10b has it**, at the moment of the dismissal and with the version that was offered, so the automatic route goes on being quiet for that version afterwards. **Z10b's *once per version* is unchanged for the route it was written about.**
- **Mac and Windows have no box**, and a user-asked check that finds a newer version proceeds exactly as Z23's would.

**Z30. A user-asked check waiting on admission is *underway*, and no fifth reading is drawn for it.**

- **Z22 admits the draw at the desktop point** (R11), so a user standing in front of the control can be behind transfer load. **The indicator is drawn from the press**, which is a local act and waits on nothing — **the same shape M18 states and not the same mechanism**, since the update draw is *"a session on no connection and reaches no server"* (R11) and has no C3 local request record to hang off.
- **Waiting to be admitted is inside that one state and is not a state of its own.** Row 482's four readings stand unchanged: *Checking…* covers the wait and the request alike, because from the user's side both are the check being under way, and **a fifth reading would be a thing to look at that no spec grants**.
- **It is never starved, and the class the guarantee reaches it through is [R19](#113-the-one-desktop-side-admission-point)'s. Rewritten twice on 2026-08-29 and this is the standing form.** *(It first read "It is never starved. R17 guarantees every class a share of the admissible count and turn-taking where that count is one, so the check is admitted in turn. Which class the update draw belongs to is Rows 189's and 207's (R14) and is not decided here." The stage-close gate found that the guarantee is to **classes** and the update fetch was none of the declared ones, and the bullet was rewritten to say the class was owed and routed to `File Browser`. The `File Browser` gate then ruled that routing wrong: **O4's closing sentence hands the rank to the declaring document**, and `Rendering` §7 had already taken that route for its submission class, so the declaration belongs here and is now made at R19.)* **R17 guarantees every class a share of the admissible count and turn-taking where that count is one**, and the update fetch is a declared class of the one order — below `upload`, above the preview fetch — so the check is admitted in turn under O5's share rule, whose exception reaches a class that has had work waiting throughout the last N grants. **The rank is declared and no number is named** (R14, R19).
- **What R19 rests on, said because it is a declaration into another document's list rather than a mechanism here.** O4 provides the route and O5's bound scales with the count of declared classes by its own definition, so **no edit to `File Browser` is owed**; what would falsify the rank is that document withdrawing O4's closing sentence, which nothing proposes. [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 57 is closed on the ruling and records the whole route.
- **No deadline is put on the wait.** M22 promises *underway* with no limit on how long it may last, and Row 482's *a check that could not be reached* is an outcome of the request rather than of the wait — **nothing here turns a slow line into a failure the user is shown.**
- **[Method](Method.md)'s third escalation, checked and declined** for Z26 to Z30 together: the act is the spec's and the entry point is Design's, nothing new is put in front of the user, there is nothing to type, and nothing that can be lost — a user who never presses it is where Row 280 already left them.

**Z31. Publication is held while any job is running. Added 2026-09-28, outside any round, out of step 10c of Build, per Row 518. Label: Creator-stated (2026-09-28).** *"We need to have Smoozy not update on its own while jobs are running. The user should be able to override this block, but by default — updating only happens with no running jobs."* The spec sentence is [Whole app](../Specs/Whole%20app.md)'s, under *Getting the app, and updates*. No auditor has seen this clause.

- **What *running* is, read on this computer alone at the moment publication would begin.** (1) **Every local job record that records no ending** — `Queue & History` §11's record, written provisional at submission and filled from the machine's note — whether the job is unconfirmed, waiting in its machine's line, rendering, or held on the user's answer, **both of `Queue & History` §3's held kinds included**. Waiting counts: the spec rules it so, and it is the safe side of the Creator's words. (2) **That record is durable and outlives the connected period, and it is the one read.** The last-known queue reading is period-held and gone once a period ends (`State, identity and recovery` D4, R22), so it is never what this clause reads: at application start, with nothing connected, the job records alone still say what is running. A record on a server with no live connection counts until its ending is learned; silence is never read as an ending, which is the rule that record already keeps. **The one record with no ending that does not count is one marked *start never heard***: its server row is gone, it is a history record from that mark on (`Queue & History` C9), and nothing could ever end it. (3) **Every transfer under way, in either direction**, manual or auto-download, **except the update's own fetch** (Z22). **A setup run is not counted**: it is not a job, and Smoozy closing mid-setup is already `Server Setup` §4's case. **Nothing is read from a machine for this clause**: no connection is opened, no reading is refreshed, and a server that cannot be reached holds the update back exactly as its last-known entries say.
- **What is held is publication and everything after it, and nothing before it.** The check (Z23, Z26), the fetch (Z22) and verification (E15, E16) run as written, and the staged verified unit is kept as each system's stage keeps it. Held: macOS's complete-bundle replacement (Z5), Linux's package transaction (Z10) and Windows' shortcut rewrite and relaunch (Z15a, Z19). **Z21a's update-in-probe record is written immediately before publication, so a held update writes none.** **On Linux the automatic route offers Z10b's box only where nothing is running**, since *Install* is where that transaction starts; a box not offered writes no declined-version mark.
- **When the hold lifts.** At the first of Z23's two automatic triggers — application start or the repeating check — that reads nothing running. **No timer, watcher or second schedule is added**: the hold is one more condition read at a moment that already exists, and P10's one standing timer stays the only one. From there the update goes on exactly as it would have with no hold.
- **Row 280 on Z3's test.** The user still discovers, fetches and initiates nothing, and remembers nothing: a held update goes in by itself when the jobs end. **What it costs is the Creator's ruling's own cost and is stated**: a user whose jobs never all end — an answer never given, a last-known entry on a server never reconnected — stays on the running version until those entries end or Z32 is used.
- **Nothing here reaches a rented server's program.** That replacement is already held while any accepted work is live, held, uncollected or unacknowledged (`Rented-server runtime` G21–G23, `Server protocol and transfers` P3 and B6), which is the ruling kept on that side, and Z32 does not reach it.

**Z32. The user may install a held update anyway, by one act at the entry point Design draws, and is told first what it does. Added 2026-09-28 with Z31, per Row 518. Label: Creator-stated (2026-09-28).**

- **One press, for the held version, once.** It writes no durable value and sets nothing: the next version is held again under Z31. **On Linux the press offers Z10b's box** without reading the declined-version mark, on Z29's ground, and the password stays the system's (Z10a).
- **What it starts is Z31's held publication and nothing else**: Z21's probe and rollback, E15's and E16's verification and E15's release-sequence refusal bind unchanged. **The user causes the install and still performs none of Z1's four acts**, which is the reading Z1's dated note already gives a user-asked check.
- **What the user is told before the press, which the spec requires.** The application restarts; jobs go on rendering on their servers, since nothing there depends on this process (P1); and transfers under way stop exactly as closing the application stops them — an auto-download resumes at the next connection and any other must be started again (`Download`, `File Browser`). **The wording and its place are Design's** (Z2).
- **It is a form of Row 482's third reading, *a new version found*, and not a fifth reading** — the same kind of form the Linux finding already takes — so Z30's closing sentence stands.
- **[Method](Method.md)'s third escalation, checked and declined**: the control and its sentence are the spec's own grant, in the Creator's words; nothing is typed and nothing can be lost that the sentence does not name first.

### 20.2 macOS: replace the complete signed bundle

**Z5. The replaceable unit is the complete signed and notarized `.app` bundle.** A modern code signature seals bundle resources as well as the executable, in Apple's own words: *"Code signatures made in Mavericks and later always seal all files in a bundle; there is no need to specify this explicitly any more"*, and *"Bundles should be treated as read-only once they have been signed"* (Apple, Technical Note TN2206, *macOS Code Signing In Depth*, `https://developer.apple.com/library/archive/technotes/tn2206/_index.html`, page updated 2016-09-13, read 2026-08-22). **So the updater never writes, deletes or renames individual members inside the installed bundle.** It stages a complete sibling bundle on the same filesystem, verifies E16 over that complete staged unit, then uses the platform file adapter's directory-level replace operation with a named backup of the complete previous bundle. **The named backup is documented — Fact 74's supported replacement API takes it as a parameter, *"If provided, the name used to create a backup of the original item"* (`https://developer.apple.com/documentation/foundation/filemanager/replaceitemat(_:withitemat:backupitemname:options:)`, read 2026-08-22) — but that page speaks of *the item* throughout and nowhere states that the item may be a directory, so the directory form of the operation is unread** ([Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 43).

**Z6. The old process continues from its already loaded image only for Z21's handover.** It does not treat that observation as permission to mutate the sealed bundle. No new ordinary work is admitted after publication begins.

**Z7. The supported API's named backup is the complete previous bundle and is what a failed launch falls back to.** L8's copy of the local record is taken beside it, because a rollback to a previous binary is a rollback to a previous schema. Z21's old process applies both before it exits. The backup is removed only after the new process returns the success token and owns the instance lock.

**Z8. The complete bundle is replaced at the existing installed path.** Whether a quarantined macOS app re-triggers first-open approval for the copy it writes for itself remains undocumented; keeping the installation identity and path is the precaution available without violating the signed-bundle boundary. **Nothing here claims that precaution closes the question**, which remains in [Section 22](#22-unresolved).

**Z9. The keychain dialog that may follow an update is an ordinary outcome and not a failure path** (S9).

**Z24. The updater's direct writes are the platform file adapter's, like every other write in the application.** A4 and F10 admit no second direct route to the filesystem and **the updater is not a carve-out from them**. On macOS, Z5's complete-bundle replacement and Z7's named backup are the adapter's directory-level publication variant. On Windows, staging and shortcut replacement are adapter operations. On Linux, the adapter supplies the verified package to the package mechanism and does not imitate that mechanism's transaction. F1's re-check by path and F2's first-error rule hold over every direct update write.

**Z25. Every piece of bundle content the build produces moves as that one complete unit.** The bundle is immutable between signed releases; mutable user state is outside it in the local-record and application-data locations already owned elsewhere.

### 20.3 Linux: a package transaction

**Z10. Linux installs E4's exact signed package through the package mechanism for its release-matrix row.** The package transaction enforces the declared CPU, libc, GTK and WebKitGTK dependencies before it publishes a new launchable version. A raw executable copied over an old path is not a Linux release/update route.

**Z11. The immediately previous verified package is retained locally until Z21's probe succeeds.** The package mechanism performs both the forward install and the one permitted reinstall; the application does not invent a second file-level package database.

*D6 build detail, 2026-10-25; no auditor has seen it.* The Linux adapter uses PackageKit's system transaction interface and its session's own authentication agent. It retains the same system-bus connection across the probe; the reinstall permits no interaction. If permission is not retained, the update-in-probe record carries a durable **rollback phase**, written before the reinstall is attempted: no rollback owed, reinstall owed, or reinstall confirmed with record restore and cleanup still owed. A launch meets that flag before opening ordinary modules, asks the system to reinstall the cached previous package, restores L8's copy, then starts the previous build in update-launch mode. After reinstall succeeds, the confirmed phase is durable before cleanup removes the cache; a later launch in that phase finishes the remaining acts without requiring a package file or another authorization. Only a completed rollback clears the phase and its record. A watcher cannot be confused with an ordinary successful launch merely because both name the new release. This is the durable completion of Z10a's existing fallback, not another record kind. **L8's copy is restored through an opening of the record that runs no migration**, because at a later launch the build restoring it is the new one, and migrating the restored copy would hand the previous build a record from a newer version. **A forward transaction that fails is an update that did not happen only where the package database still reads the running version as correctly installed** (`dpkg-query`'s status word *installed*, which `dpkg(1)` defines as correctly unpacked and configured); any other failure is treated as a publication and rolled back exactly as a failed probe is, without the probe. **A failed install writes no mark: it is not a dismissal**, so the automatic route may offer the version again *(changed 2026-10-26 by step 11's D6b, the build runner's ruling under the Creator's standing delegation; it read ~~A failed install writes Z10b's mark, as any dismissal that does not end in a completed install does.~~ No auditor has seen it)*. About's *Install…* in the Linux form of the new-version reading opens the box for the version already found and starts no check, so Z26's trigger list stays three. *(Paragraph finished by the second D6 builder, 2026-10-25; no auditor has seen it.)*

*D6b line, 2026-10-26; no auditor has seen it.* **Where a Linux rollback is owed, the application is never quiet about it.** An old process whose unattended reinstall is refused starts the installed program in the update-launch mode as it exits, so the later launch comes at once. A launch that meets the reinstall-owed phase, or a later phase it could not finish, builds the one window over the one queue and shows only Design's rollback box: no spine, no record opening other than the recovery's own non-migrating reads, and no module, so Z21a's *before any ordinary work* holds. The package mechanism is asked only when the user presses the box's reinstall, and a declined or failed request is said in the same box, which offers it again; quitting leaves the phase for the next launch, which shows the box again. The box's answers travel on the existing update command. A record that cannot be read for the phase still ends the launch as before.

The adapter's cache is below F11's per-user application-data location, in `linux-update`, with a `previous` folder holding the exact running release's verified package and signed desktop update file. The first update obtains those from that build's own release, using E10a's repository and the own-release address form already used by `Server protocol and transfers` B8d; it accepts only this running version and this Linux row. It refuses the update if that verified previous package cannot be retained. This rollback-only reading ignores the release floor while retaining its trusted-key rules, including after a declined attempt raised that floor; the forward update still obeys the floor and no stored sequence is lowered. The Debian-family artifact declares `packagekit` as a dependency, so the system mechanism is installed with Smoozy. The forward package uses the ordinary latest-release fetch. Both package digests are checked again before installation. The retained older package is used only for this recorded rollback; it is never admitted as a new update and never lowers E15's high-water.

**Documentation alone:** PackageKit 1.2.8's transaction-interface documentation defines `CreateTransaction`, `SetHints`, `InstallFiles`, `ErrorCode` and `Finished`; its policy requires administrator authentication for a local package it does not recognise (`auth_admin`, not `auth_admin_keep`), and does not retain that permission. Its reinstall and downgrade permissions are separate actions, each `auth_admin_keep` for an active session. So the unattended reinstall of the retained package is expected to be refused, and the next-launch fallback is expected to be the ordinary Linux rollback rather than its exception. Polkit documents the graphical session's authentication agent and temporary authorizations. Consequently the retained-authorization branch is not claimed to work on a Linux desktop; item 47 remains open, and the next-launch fallback above is required. Sources: the D6 build account's companion sources file. No Smoozy password field, Smoozy helper or Smoozy service is introduced, and no privileged shell is used. PackageKit is the distribution's package service, declared as a package dependency.

**Z10a. The Linux update asks the user for their computer password, and that is the Creator's ruling rather than this document's choice. Creator-stated 2026-08-22**, put to them out of the third sanity sweep's verification round.

**The fact that forced it.** Installing a distribution package is a privileged operation, in the mechanism's own words — `dpkg(1)`, Debian trixie, read 2026-08-22 at `https://manpages.debian.org/trixie/dpkg/dpkg.1.en.html`: *"Any operation that needs write access to the database or the filesystem is considered a privileged operation that might allow root escalation"*, and among its force options, *"Try to (de)install things even when not root."* **Recorded beside it:** `apt(8)`'s own page, same site and date, says nothing about root at all — silence, not an answer.

- **What the user meets.** The platform's own authentication for the package transaction, at a moment Smoozy itself started. **It is not a break of Row 280** — [Z3](#201-what-the-application-does-and-what-the-user-does) already draws that line: the user still discovers nothing, fetches nothing and remembers nothing; the application does all of it and then asks once. **The prompt is the platform's**, never a Smoozy-drawn password box, and the password is never seen, held or carried by this application ([Security and licence](Security%20and%20licence.md#12-the-domains-and-the-complete-crossing-list) §1.2's crossing list is not widened by this clause).
- **A declined or refused authentication is an update that did not happen**, in E15's own words — not a failure, not an error state, and not a thing to nag about. Nothing is published, nothing is retained, and the ordinary check ([Z23](#201-what-the-application-does-and-what-the-user-does)) meets it again later. **A user who is not an administrator on their own machine never updates by this route**, and that is stated rather than hidden; what they get is the version they installed, working.
- **The elevation is asked once and covers the whole update, rollback included.** Publication and Z21's reinstall of the retained previous package are **one authorised transaction**, because the rollback is reached by construction at a moment nobody is sitting there. **Where the platform will not hold that authorisation across the probe** — not established here, [Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 47 — the rollback is not abandoned: [Z21a](#201-what-the-application-does-and-what-the-user-does)'s durable record survives, and the next launch performs the rollback and asks then, which is a prompt the user is present for by definition.

**Z10b. The Linux box appears once per version, and the mark that keeps it to that is written at the box's dismissal. Added 2026-08-25 at the tenth sitting, per Row 482; the behaviour is this clause's and the durable value is not.** The box — Design's, drawn 2026-08-23 on the Creator's ruling of 2026-08-22 — is offered when a completed check (Z23) has found a version **newer than the running one and newer than the version the declined-version mark names**. Its *Not now* — and any dismissal that does not end in a completed install, a declined system authentication after *Install* included — **writes the mark durably with the version that was offered, at the moment of the dismissal** (D6: the fact becoming true is the user's own act). A later check finding **the same version offers nothing**, which is what reconciles Z10a's *the ordinary check meets it again later* with the design's *nothing asks again until the next version*: the check goes on running and finding, and the box does not reappear for a version the mark names. **A newer version than the marked one is offered once more**, and a completed install makes the mark moot — the version it names is behind the running one. **The mark's inventory row is `State, identity and recovery` §1.1's, being written in this same sitting, and is not added here** (L1). *Not now* is not a failure and raises no record, which Z10a already states and this clause does not restate. *(Scoped 2026-08-29, per Row 508: **a later check** here means a check on the automatic route, which was the whole trigger list when this clause was written. A check the user asked for reads the mark not at all and offers the box — [Z29](#201-what-the-application-does-and-what-the-user-does), on this clause's own ground that what the mark prevents is a nag. Nothing else in this clause moves, the mark's write at dismissal included.)*

**Z12. This is documentation alone**, on a system this project has no desktop computer for. **Nothing here was observed on Linux, and no broad-or-narrow user-computer support ruling is smuggled into the package matrix.**

### 20.4 Windows: side-by-side, and never in place

**Z13. The Windows answer is side-by-side.** The **outcome** is documented: Microsoft documents an unpackaged per-user executable that *"downloads the update to a hidden folder without any user interaction"*, that it *"verifies and installs it"*, that *"If OneDrive is running, it stops and then restarts"*, and that users *"don't need administrative rights to install the update"*; and an interface a third party can call to update and relaunch, on a deployment technology needing no administrative rights. **So an unattended, unelevated, self-relaunching update of an unpackaged Windows app is documented.**

**Z14. Replacing the image at its own path is not documented and every route Microsoft does document forbids it.** Deletion *"fails if an application attempts to delete a file that has other handles open"*; a rename *"will still fail if a file with the same name already exists and is … a currently executing file"*; the deferred-rename route *"can be used only if the process is in the context of a user who belongs to the administrators group or the LocalSystem account"*; and the restart and installer facilities resolve an in-use file by shutting the app down or asking for a reboot. **One documented route says outright that the app *"is reinstalled from a new side-by-side folder".* Write it side-by-side or it is wrong.**

**This clause was challenged on 2026-08-20 and came back better supported than it knew.** The challenge was that the rename sentence quoted above is about the **destination** of the rename, not about the file being moved — *"a file with the same name already exists"* — so it forbids renaming a new build over a running image and says nothing about moving the running image aside, which is a common updater manoeuvre. **The first half of that is right and the conclusion is not**, because the same Microsoft page carries a second rule a few lines further down, under *Special rules for renaming open files*: *"A file cannot be renamed if it has any open handles, unless it is only open because of a batch opportunistic lock (oplock) and the batch oplock can be broken immediately."* **That page's address and date read were recorded nowhere in this project and are supplied 2026-08-23: `FILE_RENAME_INFORMATION` (ntifs.h), `https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows-hardware/drivers/ddi/ntifs/ns-ntifs-_file_rename_information`, page dated 2021-11-22, read 2026-08-23.** Both quotations are on that one page and both stand word for word — the destination rule reads *"Even if ReplaceIfExists is set to TRUE, the rename operation will still fail if a file with the same name already exists and is a directory, a read-only file, or a currently executing file"*, and the *Special rules* block follows it directly. **A running executable is held open by the loader, so the aside move is refused for the source as well.** And nothing documents the manoeuvre anywhere: `MoveFileExW` mentions no executable, running image or file in use on the whole page, and the structure a user-mode program would use says only *"Contains the target name to which the source file should be renamed."* with no Remarks at all. **One honest limit:** the open-handle sentence sits on a kernel driver page and does not say whether the loader's mapping counts as an open handle for its purposes — so this refutes the manoeuvre **as a documentation claim** and does not prove it fails on a real machine. Nothing rests on the difference, because the shape here is side-by-side either way.

**Z15. So on Windows the new build is installed into a new per-user folder, verified, and the application relaunches from it**, and **the previous build is removed only after the new one has started successfully**. One previous build is kept and no more.

**Z15a. The per-user Start-menu shortcut is the durable ordinary-launch identity.** Its target is rewritten atomically from the old per-version executable to the verified new per-version executable before the relaunch helper starts the probe. On success it remains there; on failure the helper rewrites it to the retained previous build before relaunching that build. The shortcut's application identifier does not change between versions. No later ordinary launch therefore depends on the one-time relaunch helper or opens the superseded folder.

**Z16. Two conditions ride on the third-party interface and are carried rather than assumed away**: it *"is not supported in .NET Core and .NET 5 and later versions"*, and on current runtimes *"checking updates before the application starts is the only update option supported."* **Nothing above requires that interface** — what it requires is the side-by-side shape, which the same pages document — and where it is used at all, those two conditions bound it.

**Z17. The Windows shape cannot take Z8's precaution**, because side-by-side means the new build is at a new path by construction. **Whether Windows' own reputation warning fires on a build the application launches itself is not established**, and it is the exact twin of Z8's macOS question. [Section 22](#22-unresolved) carries both, and [Section 23](#23-moments-that-reached-the-creator) records the check.

### 20.5 The short-lived helper, and where it exists

**Z18. A helper exists on Windows and on neither of the other two.** On macOS the still-running old application itself replaces the complete signed bundle at the installed path and starts the new bundle in probe mode (Z5, Z21); on Linux the still-running old application asks the package mechanism to install the new signed package and starts the result the same way (Z10, Z21). **Both replacements are driven by the still-running old process, so there is nothing for a helper to do there.** What the evidence covers is narrower than either mechanism and is not overstated: the Mac observation behind Fact 74 is that a running executable can be replaced under itself at the file level, while the directory form of the bundle replacement is unread ([Section 22](#22-unresolved) item 43); and the Linux half rests on the package transaction's own route, documentation alone (Z12, item 3) — not on Fact 74's `rename(2)` reading, which underwrites [6.5](#65-the-publication-operation-and-what-would-demonstrate-it)'s ordinary-data publication rather than the update.

**Z19. The Windows helper's whole job is the relaunch.** It is started by the application, it outlives the application for the length of one relaunch, it **holds no state**, it **binds no listener**, it takes no secret, and it is P3's second helper. **It is never a service, never registered with the operating system's own service facility, and never left behind** — a helper that outlived its relaunch would be the always-running local daemon the shape forbids.

**Z20. The helper is the same signed artifact's own component and is verified by the same check the artifact was** (E15). Nothing fetches a helper separately.

**D7b correction to the Windows build detail, 2026-10-26; no auditor has seen it.** Its unconditional candidate-platform-check instruction and final certificate-block sentence below are superseded by E16’s D7b rule: after length and digest pass, the running build’s cached platform result decides whether the candidate must pass `WinVerifyTrust`. **An unsigned running build can update to an unsigned build; no certificate is needed for that route.** Z20’s “signed artifact” means the artifact authenticated by Smoozy’s signed manifest here, with a platform signature additionally required only under E16’s condition; the helper remains the running executable itself. The helper’s work and publication do not change. **Documentation alone:** Microsoft documents an attachment-saving API that can record origin evidence and documents Smart App Control’s possible block on unsigned code. Neither reading establishes whether Smoozy’s own fetch is marked as downloaded from the internet or whether Windows will warn or block its relaunch. Z17 and item 6 remain permanently unresolved; no absence of a warning or mark is claimed. Sources: `Audits/Build/Step 11/Sources/D7b, Windows updates without a certificate — what landed.sources.md`.

*Windows build detail, 2026-10-26, step 11's Windows update piece; no auditor has seen it.* **The download is E3's executable itself**, fetched straight into its version's new folder (Z15), held to the signed manifest's length and digest, and then offered where it sits to E16's platform call: `WinVerifyTrust` with the Authenticode action, no interface, the file named, no revocation checking (as Microsoft's worked example has it) and E12's no-interactive-user value, zero being the only acceptance. **Z15a's rewrite** writes a complete new shortcut, with the same application identifier, beside the old one under a name that is not a shortcut's, and renames it over the old one through the platform file adapter, so the rewrite is F16's rename and carries F16's standing note on Windows. **Z19's helper is the running build's own program, started with a helper argument**, not a second executable: E3's artifact is one executable, so Z20's *the same signed artifact's own component* is met by construction, and **because it is the running build — the one known to start — a new build that cannot start at all is still rolled back** (item 46's Windows half). The helper starts the probe, watches it for Z21's token, and on a failed start ends it, takes the instance lock back, restores L8's copy as a file, points the shortcut back at the previous program, starts that program in the update-launch mode alone and lets the lock go; **it opens no record**, and **the previous build's own start completes the rollback** under Z21a's fourth bullet, clearing the record. **Z15's *one previous build is kept and no more*** is met by removing, before each fetch, every folder in the builds folder but the running one (never while an update-in-probe record is uncleared), and by Z21a's clearing removing the previous build's folder after a successful start and the failed build's after a rollback. **Only a folder directly inside the builds folder is ever removed, and never the one the program runs from**, so the program a user first downloaded and ran, wherever they put it, is never removed by Smoozy; a folder that cannot yet be removed leaves the record for the next launch. **While E3's certificate does not exist, the platform call refuses every Windows artifact the release builds, so no Windows copy updates itself** — no route skips the check — and E8a's possible block on Windows 11 stands on the first download as before. Documentation alone (item 2). Sources: the Windows update piece's account's companion sources file in `Audits/Build/Step 11/Sources/`.
