38. **RE-WRITTEN 2026-09-04 onto the shell that was actually chosen, and NARROWED later the same day when the reading half of its own closure condition was taken. Whether the selected shell's file-drop facility differs by platform is settled by one published statement and by nothing else, and it is observed by nobody.** The original entry is Wails' throughout and **scheduled a first Wails build at Build as its own closure condition, which is a build that will never happen**; it is kept beneath this one rather than deleted, on item 45's practice. [H4](#22-the-route-chosen-and-the-properties-it-is-held-to) and [H5b](#22-the-route-chosen-and-the-properties-it-is-held-to) settled the shell as **Tauri v2** on 2026-09-02, and this entry's ground was re-taken from that project's own reference at a Build sitting on **2026-09-04** — no machine, no rental. The two quotations, their addresses and the date read are at `Preparation/Architecture/Sources/File Browser.sources.md` entry **I2a**, carried into this document's own companion under *Item 38*.

    **One per-platform statement is published, and it is Windows'.** `WebviewBuilder::disable_drag_drop_handler` — *"Disables the drag and drop handler. This is required to use HTML5 drag and drop APIs on the frontend on Windows."* So on Windows **the shell's own handler and the page's own drag and drop are alternatives rather than companions**, and a build that wants the paths keeps the handler enabled. **Nothing else read states a per-platform difference in either direction** — neither that there is one nor that there is none — and **only two pages were read**, the drop event's reference and that builder page. **Nothing is observed on any of the three systems.**

    **What the wider reading added, taken 2026-09-04 at a Build sitting on `App/Desktop app and delivery/`, with no machine and no rental.** Six further first-party addresses of the selected project's own documentation were read, plus that project's crate sources shipped on this machine, and the whole guide corpus in the single file that project publishes for the purpose. **Three things came back.** **First, and it is the substantive gain: the setting has a published default, and it is *enabled*.** The configuration property `dragDropEnabled` is documented as *"Whether the drag and drop is enabled or not on the webview. By default it is enabled."* with a stated default of `true`, and the builder route carries the same default independently — `WebviewAttributes` sets `drag_drop_handler_enabled: true`. So **the shell hands a window the dropped paths unless a build turns the handler off**, which is a published fact rather than an inference from silence. **Second, the event payload is published with no per-platform note of any kind** — `DragDropEvent`'s four members state paths and a cursor position and name no system. **Third, the builder reference was swept for every *Platform-specific* note it carries rather than looked up for one: it carries fifteen, several naming macOS or Linux as unsupported outright, and `disable_drag_drop_handler` carries none.** **That last is a statement about the documentation and not about the systems**, and it is written here as such. **What the reading did not produce is any statement, in either direction, about how the facility behaves on macOS or on Linux**, which is why this item is narrowed rather than closed. The addresses, the dates read and what the reading does not cover are in this document's companion under *Section 22 item 38*.

    **Breaks:** `File Browser` [H3a](File%20Browser.md#10-the-picker-row-307)'s *what arrives is paths and nothing else* on whichever system turns out to differ, and with it the count shown at the moment of the drop and the enumeration that follows it. **And the Windows statement breaks the drop route outright if it is taken the wrong way round**: a build that disables the handler in order to use the page's own drag and drop is handed no paths at all, so a drop does nothing on Windows and there is no failure to report. **That setting is this document's and not `File Browser`'s**, which is why it is carried here rather than there. **The setting is now made rather than inherited. 2026-09-04**: the one window `App/Desktop app and delivery/src/main.rs` builds keeps the drag-and-drop handler, written as an explicit value at the window's own construction rather than left to the shell's default, and it names this item and `File Browser` H3a at the site. **What follows from it, and it is the whole of the constraint on the interface: Smoozy's page must not reach for HTML5 drag and drop**, because the reading establishes the two as alternatives on Windows and this application has chosen the shell's handler.

    **What holds without it:** the picker route entire, which is what Row 307 is actually about; [H12](#24-what-the-small-shell-costs-and-it-lands-on-the-interface)'s standing rule that no interface behaviour is built on a capability absent from one engine; and **`File Browser` [H4](File%20Browser.md#10-the-picker-row-307)'s answer entire**, which never rested on the drop — its fallback is a question inside the one Upload control, which Design states outright and which covers both kinds on every system whatever a drop carries. **The one per-platform statement that is established is written down rather than guessed**, so a build can honour it.

    **What would close it, in three parts with different standings, and one of the three is now shut.** **The reading half is CLOSED, 2026-09-04, and it did not close the item.** It read *the selected project's own documentation beyond the two pages read may state the handler's per-platform behaviour, and nobody has looked*; somebody has now looked, across six further first-party addresses, that project's crate sources on this machine, and its whole guide corpus. **It states the default and it states no further per-platform behaviour.** The half is shut because the route has been taken to its end, not because it answered — a further page of that project's documentation is not what is missing. **The Mac third is a Build act and open** — the first build of the chosen shell at Build, on the Mac that exists. **The Windows and Linux thirds want two computers this project does not have and will not rent, and are permanently unresolved, 2026-09-02** ([Y3](#what-binds-this-before-anything-is-decided)), not pending items and with no space left for them. **So what is left of this item is a Mac observation and two systems nobody will run**, and the Windows behaviour of the drop route is **unproved unless a hosted run proves it**.

    **The kind question is not this item's and the two must not be read as one.** Whether a folder may be dropped **at all** is unstated by the selected shell in either direction, and it is `File Browser` [Section 13](File%20Browser.md#13-unresolved) item 19. This entry is about the platform; that one is about the kind. **Wails' *"files and or folders"* sentence, which the original below leans on, is not carried across** — it is the withdrawn shell's, and `File Browser` H4's *any mix everywhere* was struck on 2026-09-04 rather than re-sourced.

    *(The original entry follows, unchanged, because it is a true record of what was written on 2026-08-21 against a shell that was then the provisional direction. Nothing in it is withdrawn as a reading of Wails; what is dead in it is the closure condition.)* **Whether the shell's file-drop facility differs by platform is stated by nobody and observed by nobody.** Added 2026-08-21 with Fact 120, for `File Browser` H3a and Row 341. Wails' own pages document dropping *"files and or folders"* and delivering *"a slice of absolute file paths"*, **with no per-platform caveat**, and this project has run it on none of the three. **Breaks:** `File Browser` H4's *any mix everywhere* on whichever platform turns out to differ, and with it part of what makes Row 307's negative cost nothing. **What holds without it:** the picker route entire, which is what Row 307 is actually about, and H12's standing rule that no interface behaviour is built on a capability absent from one engine. ~~**What would close it, in two parts with different standings:** the Mac third is **the first Wails build at Build, on the Mac that exists — a Build act and open**; the other two thirds want two computers this project does not have and will not rent, and are **permanently unresolved, 2026-09-02** (Y3), not pending items with no space left for them.~~

39. **What a frameless window costs on each system is published only in part, and none of it is observed anywhere.** Added 2026-08-21 with [H6a](#22-the-route-chosen-and-the-properties-it-is-held-to) and Row 425. Three things are unread rather than answered. **The shell states what frameless removes only as *"no borders or title bar"* and names the standard window buttons nowhere**, so whether a frameless macOS window keeps its own is unknown. **It publishes no statement about the behaviours a native frame carries** — drag-to-edge arrangement, double-click-to-zoom, the system window menu — on any of the three. **And Linux is the thinnest of the three**: its option block is Icon, WindowIsTranslucent, WebviewGpuPolicy and ProgramName (H23), so nothing about the frame is settable, and both resize and drag for a frameless Linux window arrived as release-note fixes rather than as documented behaviour. **Breaks:** nothing this document promises. What it costs is a window that behaves less like its platform's own in ways nobody here has listed, on two systems this project has no computer for (item 2, item 3). **What holds without it:** the frame is given up by one published option, the drag region and the four window acts are each published by name, and **Smoozy draws its own three buttons on every system**, so no clause rests on a system button surviving. **What would close it, in two parts with different standings:** the Mac third is **the first build of the chosen shell at Build, on the Mac that exists — a Build act and open — and, for the Mac alone, the second route H6a already names** *(corrected 2026-09-04 — this read *the first **Wails** build*, a build that will never happen; the shell has been Tauri v2 since 2026-09-02, [H4](#22-the-route-chosen-and-the-properties-it-is-held-to) and [H5b](#22-the-route-chosen-and-the-properties-it-is-held-to). **The second route survives the change of shell** and is not a stale half of this correction: [H6b](#22-the-route-chosen-and-the-properties-it-is-held-to) records that the selected shell keeps the same macOS transparent-title-bar route, read from its own documentation on 2026-09-02)*; the other two thirds want two computers this project does not have and will not rent, and are **permanently unresolved, 2026-09-02** (Y3), not pending items with no space left for them.
40. **The fullscreen carve-out on the drag region is documented and unconsidered by Design.** Added 2026-08-21 with H6a. The frameless guide states *"If you allow your application to go fullscreen, this drag functionality will be disabled"*, and Row 305's Window menu offers a full-screen item. **Breaks:** nothing — a full-screen window is not draggable on any of the three systems either way. **What holds without it:** the drag region entire in every other window state. **It is recorded because it is a documented interaction between two Design decisions that were taken apart from each other**, and reconciling them is Design's rather than a repair here.
**D7b correction to item 41, 2026-10-26; no auditor has seen it.** Its unconditional Windows independent-platform-signature statements below are superseded. A stolen authorised update key reaches unsigned Windows builds as it reaches Linux: the signed manifest can vouch for malicious bytes of the named length and digest. The same applies to an update key authorised by a stolen recovery key. macOS retains its independent platform checks; Windows retains them only for a running executable that itself passes E16’s call. The digest, length, sequence and rollback checks still stand, but do not protect against bytes a stolen trusted key deliberately authorises.

41. **Loss and compromise of the offline update recovery private key are the update trust-root residual, and they are not the same failure.** *(Note 2026-10-24, the build runner: on the Creator's word the trust set carries **two** recovery keys from the first build (E19 as changed that day), either able to sign alone. That closes **loss** of one key. It does not close **compromise**: a stolen key still signs until the other revokes it, and E19 says so. Kept as it is on purpose — the Creator keeps both keys offline, apart.)* **Loss:** already authorised update keys and installed artifacts remain valid, but after the last authorised online key is lost or compromised no client can authenticate a successor and automatic updating stops. **Compromise:** the holder can sign a higher-sequence trust statement authorising an attacker-held update key and can outrun the client's trust-sequence high-water; on Linux that is sufficient to authenticate an attacker-signed manifest and package, while macOS and Windows still require the independent platform signature E16 names. The same compromised root cannot authenticate its own trustworthy replacement. **What holds without an answer:** E15's release-sequence downgrade refusal, artifact digest checks, bounded rollback, and the macOS/Windows platform-signature layers; loss by itself does not make an existing artifact or manifest false. **What would answer it:** a written offline custody, separate backup and restoration drill answers loss; compromise requires independent recovery roots embedded from the first build and a threshold rule and tested ceremony able to revoke one root under the remaining threshold. A second copy of one root is only a loss backup. Until that arrangement is selected and tested, E19's online-key transition is complete and its recovery root remains a single point that can permanently stop updates on loss or subvert Linux updates on compromise.
42. **CLOSED 2026-08-22 at the Architecture seam close: `The shape` §7's scope line now names the privileged web-content boundary.** The original entry follows, unchanged. **The privileged web-content boundary is answered here and [The shape](The%20shape.md#7-desktop-app-and-delivery)'s section 7 list does not name it.** Added 2026-08-22 with [2.8](#28-the-privileged-web-content-boundary)'s constraint line. **Breaks:** nothing in the mechanism; what is unresolved is that this document answers a security boundary against a scope line that does not mention it — what forces it is `Security and licence` §1.4's *"`Desktop app and delivery` owns the adapter, the helper and the module API boundaries"* and the scope line's *"module API boundaries"*, and neither names web content. **What holds without it:** the whole of [2.8](#28-the-privileged-web-content-boundary), which is a property of the shell — Section 2's to name, provisionally Wails and re-checked if H5b selects otherwise (corrected 2026-08-22) — and has no other document to live in — `Security and licence` owns the rules and does not choose their implementation, and no feature module could hold a boundary that binds every one of them. **It is a seam rather than a defect and is not repaired here**: the shape's list is to be made to agree.
43. **The directory form of the macOS named-backup replacement is unread.** Added 2026-08-22. Fact 74 and the input document establish Apple's page for a file that contains signed code and a file-level replacement API; that API's own page documents the named backup — *"If provided, the name used to create a backup of the original item"* — and speaks of *the item* throughout, nowhere stating that the item may be a directory, while Z5's unit is the complete `.app` bundle, which is one. **Breaks:** the macOS rollback route rather than the publication — Z7's named backup of the complete previous bundle and Z21's macOS restore stand on an operation no page was read for, while [6.5](#65-the-publication-operation-and-what-would-demonstrate-it)'s ordinary-data operation is S19's `rename(2)` and is untouched, and TN2206's sealing rule, which is what forbids member-level writes, is read and quoted at Z5. **What holds without it:** the complete-bundle unit, the same-filesystem staging, E16's verification over the staged unit, and the Windows and Linux thirds. **What would close it:** Apple's own page for the operation over a directory, or F18's throwaway shape run against a bundle on the Mac that exists.
44. **WIDENED 2026-09-06 onto the shell that was actually chosen: three of H35's five production surfaces are established by no publication read, not two.** [H6c](#22-the-route-chosen-and-the-properties-it-is-held-to) took this re-check on 2026-09-02 and its answer never reached this entry. **The selected shell's own pages establish two — the inspector and the development/reload server** — and **say nothing about the web view's ordinary context menu**, which the other shell published a default for and this one does not; the configuration schema at the measured release carries no context-menu member and the webview builder publishes no method for one, both read 2026-09-06. **What holds without it:** the *Inspect Element* entry belongs to the inspector, which is absent from a release build (H6c), so what is unestablished is the ordinary menu rather than a route to the inspector. **What would close it:** a first-party page for that setting, or the first release artifact opened at Build. **The original entry follows, unchanged, because everything below it is still true of the two surfaces it was written for.** ~~Two of H35's five production surfaces are established by no publication read.~~ Added 2026-08-22. The shell's own pages establish three — the devtools inspector, the default context menu, and the development/reload server, each quoted at H35 — and **establish nothing about debug bindings or source maps being absent from a production build**. Those two stand as this document's own build requirement, H5b's build-mode row, rather than as a vendor-published property. **Breaks:** nothing in the mechanism; what is unresolved is whether a release artifact omits them by the shell's construction or only by Build's discipline. **What holds without it:** the three sourced surfaces, H36's refusal to compose where a development surface cannot be disabled, and H5b's row, which already requires *"symbols stripped; inspector, reload server and source maps absent"*. **What would close it:** the shell's own build documentation for the two, or the first release artifact inspected at Build.
45. **Whether a distribution package installed through the platform's package mechanism is the binary [Whole app](../Specs/Whole%20app.md) promises the user downloads — closed by the Creator on 2026-08-22: it is. No spec sentence moves and nothing returns to Specs.** Raised 2026-08-22, when E4 and Z10 made the Linux artifact *"a signed, dependency-declaring package for each supported user-computer combination, not an unqualified plain executable"*, installed through the distribution's package mechanism, against the spec's Creator-stated *"The user gets Smoozy by downloading a binary from its website — no email, no account, no registration."*

46. **On macOS and Linux, a new build that cannot start at all is not rolled back by anything.** Added 2026-08-22, out of the third sanity sweep's verification round, with [Z21a](#201-what-the-application-does-and-what-the-user-does). Z21a's record is read by a launch, so a build that never reaches the record — one that will not start rather than one that starts badly — never triggers the completion or the rollback. Windows does not have this case, because Z19's helper outlives the application and performs the rollback itself; **nothing states what happens if that helper dies either**. **Breaks:** the recovery half of Row 280 on two of three systems, in the conjunction of an update in flight, the watching old process ending in the window between publication and the token, and the new build being unstartable. **What holds without it:** the forward path and the ordinary failed-start path, both unchanged; Z21a's record, which keeps the retained previous artifact and L8's record copy on the machine and named rather than swept, so the route back exists; and Windows entire. **What would close it:** giving macOS and Linux the same short-lived relaunch helper Windows already has — Z18 declines one today on the ground that the old process drives the replacement, which is true of the replacement and not of the watching — at the cost of a second helper on two more systems, which is a real change rather than a wording and is why it is recorded here rather than taken.

47. **Whether one authentication for the Linux package transaction covers a rollback taken minutes later.** Added 2026-08-22 with [Z10a](#203-linux-a-package-transaction). The ruling asks the user once; what nothing read here establishes is how long the platform holds that authorisation, or whether a second transaction under the same authorisation is admitted at all — `dpkg`'s own page states the privilege requirement and says nothing about how privilege is obtained or retained, and the desktop mechanisms that obtain it were not read. **Breaks:** nothing silently. If the authorisation does not carry, an unattended rollback cannot run at the moment it is wanted. **What holds without it:** [Z21a](#201-what-the-application-does-and-what-the-user-does)'s durable record, which is what makes the failure a delay rather than a loss — the retained previous package and L8's record copy stay on the machine and named, and the next launch performs the rollback and asks then. **What would close it, and the two halves have different standings:** the platform's own documentation for the authorisation mechanism a desktop application uses to drive a package transaction — **open, a reading, and it costs nothing** — or F18's throwaway shape run on a Linux desktop computer, which is **permanently unresolved, 2026-09-02**, that computer being one this project does not have and will not rent ([item 3](#22-unresolved), Y3). **The run is not a pending item and no space is left for it.**

**What the ruling turned on.** Either way the user goes to Smoozy's website and downloads one file, with no email, no account and no registration — **which is what that sentence was protecting.** What differs is only what happens when they open it: it runs, or the system installs it and then it runs. The Creator was shown the difference the package buys, in the plainest terms available — a plain file gives the runtime library requirements no way to be checked, so a machine missing one meets a blank web view; a package makes the distribution's own installer refuse up front and name the library — and ruled that a package still counts. Label: **Creator-stated** (2026-08-22).

**What that leaves standing.** Row 280's first clause on Linux, whose Z10 package transaction is the release and update route and whose *"A raw executable copied over an old path is not a Linux release/update route"* is unchanged; the dependency enforcement E4 exists for, an unlisted combination being refused before launch rather than failing inside the web view; and the macOS and Windows thirds, which E4 never touched. **This entry stays in the list as a closed one rather than being deleted**, because the reasoning is what a later reader needs when the same question arrives about another platform.

48. **On macOS, what the interface needs of a web view frozen to the operating-system version is bounded by nothing.** Added 2026-08-23 with [H13a](#24-what-the-small-shell-costs-and-it-lands-on-the-interface), as the half of item 23 the Creator's ruling does not reach. The absent case cannot arise — Apple lists `WKWebView` as available from macOS 10.10 onward and the shell states no macOS runtime requirement (H13a) — and there is no macOS bootstrapper and no option to take, so **the too-old case has no remedy of the kind the ruling gave Windows**. **What is unestablished is the floor:** no publication read here states which macOS version carries a WebKit the interface needs, and H11 records that this engine is the one frozen to the operating-system version. **Breaks:** Row 282 on a Mac old enough that the interface draws wrongly or not at all, where the only route back is the user updating their operating system. **What holds without it:** [H12](#24-what-the-small-shell-costs-and-it-lands-on-the-interface)'s standing rule that no interface behaviour is built on a capability absent from one of the three engines, which is the same protection item 19 rests on; and a Mac kept up to date, which is what the one machine this project has is and on which this engine is the newest of the three. **What would close it:** the design values and the two typography substitutions checked against WebKit's own per-version feature statements, which is item 19's work and not a second question.
49. **The embedded bootstrapper's two documented limits, neither of which changes the ruling.** Added 2026-08-23 with H13. **It reaches Microsoft's servers** — the page calls it the online-client route, *"downloads and installs the Evergreen Runtime from Microsoft servers"*, and names a separate standalone installer *"For offline clients"* — so a Windows machine with no runtime and no route to those servers still cannot run the interface, and Embed does not close that case. **And the two publications price the same file differently**: the shell's *"~150k to the binary size"* against Microsoft's *"a tiny (approximately 2 MB) installer"*, both read and quoted at H13, reconciled by neither. **Breaks:** nothing in the choice — the ruling was taken against a *"~127MB"* and a *"~180MB"* alternative and holds at either figure — and, for the offline machine, the same outcome Row 282's edge already named, now narrowed to a machine that is also offline. **What holds without it:** the ruling itself and every configuration with a route to those servers. **What would close it:** the built artifact weighed at Build, and, for the offline case, the standalone installer the same page documents, which is the option this document did not take.

**One thing that did move, and it is not this item.** The Linux release matrix's rows are now bounded by the Creator's separate ruling of the same day naming the supported *rented-server* families — that boundary is `Server Setup`'s and is about the rented machine, **not** about the user's own computer, and E4's own sentence already said the two must not silently answer each other. **They still do not.** Which user-computer combinations the product promises is unchanged by that ruling and remains unnarrowed here.

50. **CORRECTED 2026-08-31: the entry priced the test as impossible for the wrong reason, and the reason it stated was contradicted by item 2 two entries above it.** A Windows machine **can** be rented and four have been, at the small-change scale this stage's machine work has run on, so *a Windows computer this project does not have* was not what stood in the way. **CORRECTED AGAIN 2026-09-02: *the test is not impossible* is no longer true, and this entry is permanently unresolved.** The Creator ruled on **2026-08-31** that there will be no further rented machine for answering a question, so **no rental is coming and the ten-minute test will not be run** — this is not a pending item, and no reader may leave a space in the code or in the record for its answer. *(Both corrections stand: the one of 2026-08-31 fixed a wrong reason, and this one records that the reason no longer matters, the route being shut.)* **What is actually out of reach is a Windows desktop edition**: every one of the four rentals reported a Server edition, and nothing read establishes that a Server edition reports the same menu font as the desktop edition the user runs — so a rental would answer for the edition it is, not for the question. **The question is unchanged, what breaks is unchanged, and what holds without it is unchanged**; only the stated reason moves. **The documentation route named below is untouched and is still open** — Microsoft's own window-metrics documentation can be read at any time, on any machine, and it is the one live route this entry has. The original entry follows, unchanged. **Which face the Windows web view resolves `system-ui` to is not established, and with it whether the app's italic there is a designed face or a machine shear.** Added 2026-08-25 with [H29a](#27-the-two-faces), per Row 483. The check relocated the question rather than closing it: the engine returns **the Windows menu font as the operating system reports it**, so the answerable question is which face Windows reports as the menu font — answerable against Microsoft's own window-metrics documentation, or by ten minutes' synthesis-off test on a Windows computer this project does not have (item 2). **Breaks:** the italic half of *a value Smoozy cannot confirm* on Windows — a ~14° synthesized shear if the menu font is Segoe UI Variable, which ships no italic at all — and Microsoft's own guidance advises against italic in Windows interfaces either way. **What holds without it:** the dash beside the italic, which the design's own sentence carries; the measured result that a synthesized oblique is distinguishable from upright at small sizes; and the macOS answer, observed five ways.
51. **Notification activation is documentation alone off the Mac, nothing was ever posted anywhere, and the cold-start case is unproven on Windows and undefined by publication on Linux.** Added 2026-08-25 with [13.7](#137-what-clicking-a-notification-does), per Row 480. The shell's Windows path from a click on a closed application to its callback is established by no publication — its notification file parses no command line — and the Linux specification describes a signal to a connected client and nothing else. **Breaks:** nothing promised — the spec's own bound is that a notification reaches the user while Smoozy runs and never once they have quit it, and T30 builds on the running case alone; what is lost on a cold Windows or Linux click is the going, never state, T22 holding. **What holds without it:** running-app activation documented on all three by each vendor, and built as this document's own platform code. *(Corrected 2026-09-09 at Build step 10a piece G. This read *"and shipped by the shell at its released version"*, which carried T28's Wails reading; see T28's own correction.)*

The shell states the dependency for macOS outright — the native edit menu is what makes the clipboard keys work at all — and publishes nothing either way for the other two, whose menus this application draws itself. **Breaks:** the Edit menu's three keystrokes on those two systems, where the drawn accelerator labels would name keys that do nothing outside editable fields. **What holds without it:** the menu items themselves, which act by click through K7's focus routing; and macOS entire, where K9's native bar carries the keys. **What would close it, and the two halves have different standings:** a statement from the shell or from either web view's own publication — **that route is open, costs nothing and can be taken at any time** — or a run on either machine, which is **permanently unresolved, 2026-09-02**, those being computers this project does not have and will not rent (items 2 and 3, Y3). **The run is not a pending item and no space is left for it.**

   **And the click route has a question of its own, which is parked here rather than rested on. Added 2026-09-12, Build step 10a round 1.** *"What holds without it"* above names the menu items acting by click, and on Windows and Linux that acting is one old engine call whose standing in either web view is established by no publication read either. **So the fallback this item leans on is itself unestablished**, and saying so is the point: [K18](#185-the-keyboard-shortcuts-beside-the-menu-items) is an assumption about the keystrokes and this is a second assumption about the click, of exactly the same standing and with no machine to settle it. **Breaks:** if that call is inert too, the Edit menu's four have no working route at all on those two systems, by key or by click. **What holds without it:** [K16](#185-the-keyboard-shortcuts-beside-the-menu-items) carries the one published Windows behaviour on this point — *"with a text box focused, the copy accelerator copies the selected text and app-defined accelerators are ignored"* — which is why Build stopped the application cancelling those presses on 2026-09-12 and let the engine have them: **the engine's own handling is the route with a published word behind it, and the menu items are the second one.** **PERMANENTLY UNRESOLVED** as a run, per items 2 and 3; the documentation route is open, as it is for the keystroke half.

55. **CLOSED 2026-08-29 at the stage close, and the closing is a ruling rather than a repair on this side: [Z23a](#201-what-the-application-does-and-what-the-user-does) is the correct side and `State, identity and recovery` §1.1 I4 is the side that moves.** **Three grounds.** *(1)* The design's four readings distinguish the two, and I4 collapses them — *a check that could not be reached* is given its **own** reading precisely because it is not the resting one, so under I4 the box carries both at once and, after the failure mark is cleared, only the resting one survives. *(2)* Under I4 the surviving line is false in the one way the whole-app promise forbids: a user offline for three days, the automatic check failing every interval, reads *last checked a minute ago* and concludes they are current — *"the app being wrong on screen about its own state, which is never past the line"*, the sentence [M6](#161-what-presenting-means-here) and [L9](#52-migrations) already hold this document to — while under Z23a the surviving line says *last checked three days ago*, which is true, signals the staleness, and points the user at the act [Z26](#201-what-the-application-does-and-what-the-user-does) grants them; nothing is lost by it, because what tells the user Smoozy is trying and failing is the design's own fourth reading, which stands either way. *(3)* I4's stated ground mis-describes the source it rests on: *"any of the four"* treats the design's four as four **outcomes of a check**, and two of them are not outcomes at all — *the version and when it was last checked* is the resting state and *Checking…* is the running one — and the moment is not free-standing in the promise but bound to the version, *"the version and when it was last checked"*. **A fourth ground stands inside the other document itself:** its §8.3 already withdraws the licence-check condition on *a check that completed, whatever it answered*, so inside it a check that could not be reached is not a completion. **Nothing in this document moves** — Z23a, [Z28](#201-what-the-application-does-and-what-the-user-does) and this entry stand exactly as written, and no file outside this document was touched. *(Still true of the substance, 2026-08-29 at the re-gate repair: Z28's third bullet and this entry's closing sentence were rewritten that day to record that both ends had shut, and no rule, value or branch in either changed.)* **What was owed elsewhere is written, and this entry is a closed seam rather than a ruling with a debt attached** *(corrected 2026-08-29 at the re-gate repair; this sentence read **"What is owed elsewhere is routed rather than written here: I4's own bullet, and Row 482's answer summary in `Rows/Design — Whole app.md`, which restates I4's side and is the runner's"**, and both had already been discharged the same day)*. **`State, identity and recovery` [§1.1](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#11-on-the-users-computer) I4's first bullet now reads** *"**A check that could not be reached writes nothing and leaves the moment as it was**"*, carrying its own dated note that names this entry as where the seam was filed, and that document's §13 item 14 records the move and routes the record back here; **and Row 482's answer summary in `Rows/Design — Whole app.md` was corrected the same day to the same reading.** **Nothing is owed elsewhere, and nothing of the ruling moves** — Z23a is still the correct side, I4 is still the side that moved, and [Z28](#201-what-the-application-does-and-what-the-user-does)'s third bullet now quotes I4's current wording instead of its superseded one. **The original entry follows, unchanged.** **Whether a check that could not be reached writes the last-completed-check moment is answered twice, differently, by two documents that wrote it on the same day for the same row.** Added 2026-08-29 with [Z28](#201-what-the-application-does-and-what-the-user-does), per Row 508, and it is a seam rather than a gap. [Z23a](#201-what-the-application-does-and-what-the-user-does) says *"a check that could not be reached writes nothing, takes the ordinary failure shape, and leaves the moment what it was, which is exactly what *when it was last checked* claims"*. `State, identity and recovery` [§1.1](State,%20identity%20and%20recovery.md#11-on-the-users-computer) I4 says the moment is written *"when a check reaches an outcome — **any of the four, a check that could not be reached included**, because *when it was last checked* is a fact about the checking and not about the answer"*. **Both are Row 482's answer, both were written on 2026-08-25 at the tenth sitting, and each reads the same four words of the design's own reading the other way.** **Breaks:** the About line's meaning after a run of unreachable checks — on Z23a's reading it names the last moment Smoozy actually learned something, on I4's the last moment it tried; a builder holding both writes one and the inventory row describes the other. **What holds without it:** the value exists, is user-held, carries its clock, and is a moment and never an age, on which the two agree; and Z28's widening, which is about **which route** asked and is true under either reading. **What would close it:** one of the two documents moving, which is the runner's to route — **it is not repaired in either direction here**, and Z28 states the disagreement rather than picking.
56. **NARROWED 2026-09-03 at Build step 2: the second half is answered on macOS and stands on the other two.** [F21e](#66-the-folder-identity-comparison) names the test — `statfs(2)`'s `MNT_LOCAL`, *"File system is stored locally"*, from that system's own manual page — so on the one computer this project has, F21d's branch is taken before any identity call rather than only where one refuses. **Nothing read establishes a counterpart on Windows or Linux**, and the first half is untouched: no vendor publishes identity across a network filesystem on any of the three, so the comparison stays a local answer everywhere. *(The entry follows as it stood.)* **No vendor publishes folder identity across network filesystems, so [6.6](#66-the-folder-identity-comparison)'s comparison is a local answer and a search folder on a network share cannot be compared — and, added 2026-08-29 at the re-gate repair, nothing establishes how the adapter tells a network volume from a local one in the first place.** Added 2026-08-29 at the stage-close repair with [F21d](#66-the-folder-identity-comparison), per Row 484 and Fact 140, which states the conclusion in as many words — *"And no vendor publishes identity across network filesystems, so the answer is a local one."* Windows publishes that the information call *"may fail, return partial information, or full information"* depending on the server; Linux's own client documentation refuses it both ways, the server's inode numbers carrying no uniqueness guarantee across multiple server-side mounts under one share and client-generated ones varying *"after unmount or reboot"*; and **Apple publishes nothing found on it across five sources read**. **Breaks:** the *not added twice* half of Row 484's promise wherever the folder is on a network share — two entries naming one folder go undetected, so the user's own ordering describes a list with two rows where the share has one, and the flash the design promises never fires; and a search folder on a share is ordinary rather than exotic. **What holds without it:** F21d's honest outcome — *cannot determine*, said rather than guessed, so nothing is asserted that no publication supports; the whole of the comparison on local volumes, which is where F21's evidence is; and the containment half of Row 484's promise entire, delivered by construction at `Dependency Upload & Remap` F2, F3 and F4 and never part of this question. **What would close it:** a statement from any of the three vendors on identity over a network filesystem, or a run against a network share — free, and possible later on the one Mac this project has.

**Widened 2026-08-29 at the re-gate repair, and this is the second unestablished half: the detection, not the identity.** F21d's branch is selected by its own sentence — the adapter answers *cannot determine* *"wherever the path resolves onto a network volume"*, on all three systems — and **no per-system reading that tells a network volume from a local one is established anywhere in this project.** Fact 140's answer of record establishes what the three vendors publish about identity **over** a network filesystem and supplies no such reading, its own *Not answered* line naming the Windows and Linux runs and the bind-mount question and not this; `Facts.md` Fact 140 carries the same conclusion and no more; [F5](#61-the-one-rule-the-whole-adapter-is-built-on)'s free-space reading is a choice of which mount to read and is a different question; [F11](#62-what-the-adapter-is-for)'s enumeration of resolvable locations reaches it nowhere; and [6.6](#66-the-folder-identity-comparison)'s three per-system bullets name only the identity calls. Label: **unresolved**, on all three systems. **Breaks:** a share the adapter does not recognise as one falls through to F21's identity comparison and is answered from **F21** rather than from F21d — with exactly the value F21d exists to refuse, *"a value the vendor will not stand behind, passed off as an identity"* — and answered **silently**, since F21 returns a match or a mismatch either way and nothing in the result says which volume it came from. Row 484's subject is a search-folder list and F21d's own words are that *"a search folder on a network share is ordinary"*, so this is the ordinary case and not the rare one. **What holds without it:** every share the adapter does recognise takes F21d's branch and is refused honestly rather than guessed at; the whole of the comparison on local volumes, which is where F21's evidence is; and **F21d's answer itself, which is the right branch either way** — what is missing is the fact under the sentence that selects it, not the sentence. **What would close it:** the call each vendor publishes for *is this path on a network volume*, read from that vendor's own publication and entered with a per-system label — one reading each, and **nothing is supplied here from memory** (Rule 2). [Sources](Sources/Desktop%20app%20and%20delivery.sources.md) standing gap 14 carries the absence.
